The middleware now mints it in its `except` branch (it runs before the exception handler) and stashes it on `request.state`; the handler echoes the same id into the 500 body, so a screenshot of the error and the record in the log name each other. (5) **`_buckets` is bounded** — idle-and-refilled entries first, then least-recently-seen past the cap; eviction can only ever grant a page more budget, never less. (6) **Client-disconnect detection is NOT implemented** — see the rationale | (6) is the substantive decision. The goal was to mark an abandoned run `disconnected` instead of `ok` (CL-5a's named gap). Both approaches are dead ends under this app's shape, each verified: from a **route**, `BaseHTTPMiddleware` wraps the downstream `receive`, so `request.is_disconnected()` never sees `http.disconnect` and a watcher waits forever — while the same watcher fires immediately with no middleware in front, which is what makes it easy to "verify" wrongly. From the **middleware** it does fire — but `is_disconnected()` peeks by CONSUMING a message off the receive channel, so polling it steals the `http.request` body the downstream route is waiting for and **every request with a body hangs**. The first spike used a body-less route and looked like a clean success; the hang only surfaced when a test made a real POST with a body and waited patiently. That test is now kept as a **regression guard** (`test_a_run_with_a_body_completes_under_the_middleware`) rather than deleted, because the trap is re-approachable. Doing it properly means converting `no_cache_and_log` to pure ASGI so it can tee the receive channel — a change to the hottest path in the server, which belongs in its own commit and not riding along with an observability feature. Meanwhile the common case (a slider supersession) IS reported by the page (D137), so the gap is a closed tab or reload only. Second time this session that a green spike hid the failure case; the pattern is recorded in the design doc's §9.5 |
| D139 | **Two more call-log corrections from a fourth review pass: a day's store files are MERGED on append time (name order does not rank pids in time), and the oversized-record shrink re-runs the same prune every other write does** | (1) **Same-day files are merged, not concatenated.** `_iter_records` walked files in reverse name order and drained each before the next. But a name orders records only by its **date** segment: the pid segment is arbitrary AND compared lexically, so `2026-07-25-8000-000` sorts after `2026-07-25-12345-000`. With two live servers — the case per-pid files exist for (CL-7) — `query` returned the stale process's tail as the newest page, and `--follow` waited out its entire timeout while the live server logged in front of it (reproduced: cursor frozen on the stale file across a fresh write). Now each day's files are `heapq.merge`d on `recorded_at`, descending. The merge is **per day** because whole days cannot interleave (a file only takes appends while the UTC date still matches its name), which bounds open handles to one day's files and keeps the walk lazy — a `limit` satisfied by today never opens last week's — and the merged streams are closed deterministically when the walk is abandoned mid-page. (2) **The shrink path prunes.** A record still over `RECORD_CAP` after the per-field caps was serialized straight from `_shrink`, which marks dropped fields by setting them to `None` — so oversized records kept explicit nulls and carried no `level` or `recorded_at`, resurrecting D138(1)'s `"error": null` ERROR misread on exactly the records most likely to matter. `_prune` is idempotent, so the fix is to run it on the shrunk dict. | Inclusion semantics are deliberately **unchanged**: each file keeps its own exact `since` stop (D137), and the merge decides only the order records come out in. That split is what makes the fix safe to reason about — a store with out-of-order stamps comes back in a slightly odd order rather than losing records, and the proof behind the per-file stop (`occurred_at <= recorded_at`, so a record appended before the window cannot have started inside it) is untouched. Both defects are the same shape and are recorded in the design doc's §9.5: `_iter_records` already carried a comment saying same-day files interleave in time — the fact was understood, written down, and then applied to the file-skip bound only, one line short of the ordering that depends on it equally; and (2) is a fix that missed its own second exit. A comment explaining why a bound is shaped a certain way is evidence to go re-audit every other place that fact bears on |
| D140 | **The call log's read cursor is scoped to the caller's FILTERS, not to the whole store** | `query` took its returned cursor from the first record in the store walk, before `_matches` — so with any filter the cursor was the newest id in the store rather than the newest the caller was shown. `--follow --page X` consequently woke on another page's traffic and then printed no calls for X (reproduced: baseline `other-1` while the only matching record was `mine-1`). The cursor is now taken on the first MATCHING record. Two supporting rules: when nothing newer matched, the caller's own cursor comes back unchanged — returning `None` would read as "start over" and be answered with an unbounded newest page — and with no matches at all it is `None`, with the CLI omitting the cursor line rather than printing one that cannot be passed back. The walk still stops at the cursor by IDENTITY, checked before the filters, so an older cursor that no longer matches them ends the walk instead of reading as purged. | Third distinct cause of the same symptom, which is the reason this is recorded rather than just fixed: D138/D139 and this one were all *the follower reporting no activity when there was activity* — first append-vs-start time, then file-order-vs-merge-order, now store-scope-vs-filter-scope. A false negative in a verification tool is one symptom with many causes, and fixing two of them was no evidence the third was absent. The `calls` view template is unaffected: its Follow re-polls the reader ops rather than comparing cursors |
| D141 | **`--follow` stops waiting out records that are already on disk, and a cursor the scan never reached is no longer reported as purged** | (1) **Follow waits only when the caller's cursor is at the tip.** It compared the store tip against a pre-wait baseline even when an explicit `--since-cursor` already had unseen records behind it — so the normal agent race (ask a human to open a page, the calls land, THEN run follow) timed out holding exactly the records it was waiting for and answered "no new calls" (reproduced: two records on disk, a 6 s timeout fully consumed). A cursor is the caller stating what it has already seen, so when the tip is past it the answer is available immediately. The wait is unchanged when the cursor IS the tip, and when no cursor is given. (2) **`scan_truncated` is surfaced.** The seeking walk gives up after a bounded scan, so `cursor_missing` alone could not distinguish "purged by retention" from "never reached" — a valid deep cursor read as a dead one, and the skipped gap went unmentioned. The flag is now in the CLI's JSON and switches the text note from a confident "purged, or wrong" to a statement that the store is deeper than this read. | (1) is the FOURTH distinct cause of one symptom — the follower reporting no activity when there was activity (D138 wrong stop key, D139 file order, D140 cursor scope, now the wait predicate). It landed immediately after I wrote that such a symptom deserves a sweep rather than a point fix, and then swept only the store and read paths — never questioning the predicate that decides whether to wait at all. Knowing the method is not applying it widely enough; recorded in the design doc's §9.5 because that is the transferable part. (2) is a smaller instance of the same discipline: do not report absence that was never verified. Both negative cases are tested explicitly, since a careless version of (1) would answer instantly for every follow and quietly delete the feature |
| D142 | **The Calls gate (and the size trim) order store files by mtime, not by name — the third and last home of the name-order-is-time-order belief** | `condition.py`'s bounded probe took `reversed(names)[:MAX_FILES]` as "the newest files". Since a store name ranks records only by its date segment (CL-7), a few same-day files are enough for that window to be entirely stale: reproduced with three stale pids sorting lexically after the live one, where the gate returned False while the page's records sat in the live file — so the Calls mode silently never appeared on it. Now ordered by mtime, which for an append-only file IS its newest record (the same fact the reader's file-skip already relies on). The bounded-probe guarantee is unchanged and tested: still exactly MAX_FILES tails read, just the right ones. `sweep()`'s size trim carried the same belief as a COMMENT ("oldest first (store_files is name-sorted)") — harmless, because same-day files are the same age and today's are excluded, but it is now mtime too rather than left standing as justification. | Third round running where the finding is *the fix was not propagated widely enough*: D140 across the layer, D141 across the symptom, D142 across the codebase. D139 fixed this exact belief in `_iter_records` and I never asked where else it lived — Bugbot's report cites CL-7/D139 back at me. The cheap habit that would have caught all three: after fixing a wrong belief, grep for the BELIEF, not for the bug. Doing that here is what surfaced the trim comment, which no reviewer had flagged |
| D143 | **`--follow`'s early-answer test is a bounded read, not an id comparison — fixing a regression D141 introduced for cursors that came from a different read** | D141 skipped the wait when `cursor != baseline`. That asks "is the caller's cursor the current tip", which is a different question: a cursor from a BROADER read is never the tip of a narrower one. So the ordinary agent pattern — take a global cursor from `calls --json`, then `--follow --page X` — skipped the wait, matched nothing and printed "no calls recorded" after 1 s of a 5 s timeout (reproduced). Now the test is the actual question, answered by one bounded read (~1 ms): are any MATCHING records newer than this cursor? A cursor that cannot be found is deliberately NOT counted as "already new" — absence proves nothing about what arrived, so it falls through to the wait, which is what `--follow` was asked to do. | The lesson is about the SHAPE of negative-case testing, and it is uncomfortable because D141's own note said "a careless version of this fix answers instantly for every follow and quietly deletes the feature" and then tested exactly two cursor shapes against that: at-the-tip, and absent. The third — a cursor from elsewhere — is the one that broke. Enumerating the negative cases you thought of while writing the fix is the same blind spot as testing the consumer you just wrote; enumerate the INPUT SPACE instead ("where did this cursor come from" had three answers, and I covered two). Recorded in the design doc's §9.5 |
| D144 | **A lost `--since-cursor` makes `--follow` resume from the baseline, and the prefs snapshot honours an invalidation that lands mid-read** | (1) **An unfindable cursor is replaced by the pre-wait baseline.** D143 correctly made such a cursor fall through to the wait, but the post-wait read still used the ghost id — which `query` answers with "the newest page", and the cursor-bounded digest then summarised that history as what arrived. Note the shape: the TIMEOUT path was already empty and correct, so the failure mode was waiting *successfully*. The baseline is exactly "everything up to the moment the wait began", so resuming there makes the digest what a follower actually asked for; `cursor_missing` still reaches the caller (JSON key plus a text note) so a lost cursor is never silent. (2) **`_prefs_snapshot` guards its store with a generation counter.** The `prefs.json` read is deliberately outside the lock — holding it across file I/O would serialize every logged call behind one disk read — so an `invalidate_prefs_cache()` from the prefs endpoint can land mid-read, and storing the result unconditionally repopulated the cache with the PRE-write snapshot for the whole 1 s TTL. A capture on/off toggle then looked ignored, defeating CL-14a's invalidate-on-write guarantee. The read may still return the value it fetched (it was true when fetched); it just no longer outlives the request. | (1) is the third consequence in a row of the same primitive's edge cases, and worth noting for its shape rather than its size: the fix that introduced it was itself a fix for the prior round, and each time the *newly reachable* state was the one left unconsidered — here, "we waited, and the wait succeeded, but the anchor was never valid". When a change makes a previously-unreachable path reachable, that path is the one to test. (2) is the first genuine concurrency finding in the feature; the 1 s TTL made it look benign, but it is exactly the window a user toggling a preference is watching. Both negative cases are tested — an uncontended read still caches, and a timeout after a lost cursor still reports nothing |
| D145 | **A lost cursor is reported on the timeout path too, and the Calls view remembers a reload that arrives mid-read instead of dropping it** | (1) **`--follow`'s timeout branch reports `cursor_missing`.** It returns before both output sites, so the `cursor_lost` flag D144 plumbed through reached the caller only when records happened to arrive — the branch hardcoded `"cursor_missing": False` and printed a bare "no new calls within Ns". That withholds the flag in precisely the case it explains: an agent holding a ghost id usually has nothing arriving either, and "nothing ran" versus "I could not tell you what is new" are different answers. The proof/guess split (`scan_truncated`) is carried from the **probe**, not from the post-wait read — once the cursor is replaced by the baseline that read is not looking for the caller's id at all, so a valid-but-deep cursor was being called "purged by retention, or wrong" on every follow. (2) **The Calls view's `load()` keeps a pending flag.** `if (inflight) return;` dropped the newer request outright: the in-flight read is already committed to its filters, so changing scope/window/query/Failed mid-read rendered the PREVIOUS filters while the controls and the URL showed the new ones, and nothing reloaded until the user pressed Refresh (or Follow's poll happened to fire). Now coalesced to exactly one re-run in `finally`, so the single-flight property holds — a burst of clicks costs two reads, not N — and a transient reader failure cannot strand the view on its error card. | (1) is D144's own claim ("a lost cursor is never silent") failing on the branch its test did not assert against: the test called the timeout path and checked only `timed_out is True`. Touching a value's *producer* without checking every *exit* that consumes it is the same omission as D139→D142, one layer down — and a flag that is only reported when it does not matter is worse than absent, because its silence reads as "fine". (2) is the first UI-concurrency finding in the feature, and it needed a harness the repo did not have: the template's JS had only source-grep assertions, so the fix is tested by extracting the real `load()` from `template.html` and driving it under node with stubbed ops. Extracted rather than copied on purpose — a copy keeps passing after the shipping function regresses, which is the one thing a test of a single-flight guard must not do |
| D146 | **The Calls gate resolves the store path by the writer's rules, and the duplication is pinned by a test instead of by inspection** | `condition.py` must work as a standalone copy in the user template dir, so it duplicates the branch-aware store path rather than importing it — and the duplicate had drifted to "join the raw `FUSED_RENDER_BRANCH` value", which is wrong three separate ways (reproduced, all three): a **default-branch** ref is a baseline opt-out, so `FUSED_RENDER_BRANCH=main` writes to `~/.fused-render/calls` while the gate probed `branches/main/`; refs are **sanitised** (`Feature_X` → `feature-x`); and refs are **truncated to 12 chars**, so this very branch writes to `branches/claude-fused/` while the raw join nested `branches/claude/fused-api-call-logging-d97w88/` — an extra level deep, on the `/`. Each one puts the probe in a directory that never receives records, so the gate fails closed and the Calls mode silently never appears on a page with plenty of history. Now mirrors `_branch.sanitize` + `branch_dir` + the ref priority (env var wins even when empty, then the build-time baked ref, read through one guarded import), and the fix is anchored by an **equality test across a table of refs** (`condition._store_dir() == calls.store_dir()`), plus the end-to-end default-branch case. | The uncomfortable part is that the correct version of this code already existed one template folder over: `zarr_aoi/tile_server.py` inlines the same resolution with a docstring saying "keep the sanitize rule in lockstep with `_branch.sanitize`" — a precedent from an earlier review round that I did not go looking for when writing the second standalone copy. Two lessons, and the second is the durable one. (a) When you deliberately duplicate a rule, the *first* question is who else already duplicated it. (b) A duplicate justified by a comment is a duplicate that will drift; the fix is not a better comment but a test that asserts the two implementations agree — which is why the table test, not the three individual rules, is the real deliverable here. Note also which case the raw join happened to get right: baseline and an already-canonical ref, i.e. exactly the two a developer would try by hand |
| D147 | **The call log stores one path form — the shell's canonical, forward-slashed form — and it is enforced at the single write point** | Path filters compare with `==`, but on Windows the store held two incompatible forms of the same path. `page`/`target_file` arrive from headers and the read routes' `path` param already canonical (router.ts: "the shell's canonical form keeps forward slashes"), while `/api/run`'s relative-`py` branch builds its target with `os.path.normpath` and gets backslashes back, and the CLI ran `--page` through `os.path.abspath` and got backslashes too. Reproduced with `ntpath` standing in for Windows' `os.path`: `calls --page` matched **nothing at all** — not even the page the caller was standing on — the Calls view on a `.py` (which matches via `entrypoint`) rendered empty on a file the gate had just confirmed has history, and the gate's own substring probe missed that history too. Fixed by canonicalizing every path field in `record()` (the single writer) and both path filters in the CLI, through one named rule — `_view_url_codec.canonical_fs_path`, which `view_url_path` had been computing inline and now calls. Drive-letter paths only: on POSIX a backslash is a legal filename character, so an unconditional `.replace` would silently rewrite a page's identity. | Three things worth keeping. (a) **The fix belongs at the boundary, not at the three call sites.** The report named three broken readers; there was one broken *writer* plus one broken filter, and normalizing at the writer fixed the third reader — the gate — with no code in it. Patching each reader would have left the store holding two forms and the next reader broken by default. (b) **This is D146's class of bug one layer down, and I had just written the lesson for it.** D146 was two spellings of a directory; this is two spellings of a file path. The habit that catches both is to ask what canonical form a value has and where it is established — and after D146 I checked that the gate agreed with the writer about the *directory* without ever asking whether they agreed about the *paths inside it*. (c) **The platform I do not run on is the one the invariant breaks on.** Both forms are identical on POSIX, so every test and every manual check I did was blind to it by construction. The regression tests therefore simulate Windows with `ntpath` and drive-shaped literals rather than sitting behind a `sys.platform` guard, which would have meant the coverage only ever ran where the bug cannot happen. |
| D148 | **The prefs payload reports whether the call store exists; the read never creates it** | `Browse call logs` navigated to `calls.dir`, which the writer creates on its first append — so before any page had made a logged call the path did not exist and the explorer landed on a stat error (reproduced: `dir_exists False` on a fresh home, `True` after one `_append`). The tempting fix is to `makedirs` it, and that is the wrong place twice over: `GET /api/prefs` is a read, and a read that provisions storage is a side effect nobody asked for; and the lazy create is what keeps an empty store from appearing for a user who never records a call. So the payload gains `dir_exists`, the button waits on it, and the muted line says *why* — "no calls recorded yet" is the same answer as "why has no page got a Calls mode?". | The instructive part is the asymmetry the fix explains rather than hides: `log.dir` sits two lines above `calls.dir` in the same payload and needs no flag, because logging does `makedirs` at startup while the call store is created by its writer. Two directories in one payload with two different lifetimes, and the UI had been treating them as one kind of thing. Also worth noting what the D147 belief-sweep found here: `calls.dir` has exactly one consumer, so there was no second site to fix — the sweep is cheap and its negative result is worth as much as a hit. The prefs comment claiming `dir` exists "to reveal the store through /api/fs/reveal, exactly as log.path does" describes an affordance nothing actually calls; left alone, but it would have had the identical bug. |
| D149 | **The call-log prefs report their effective state and what forced it, from the writer's own resolvers** | `FUSED_RENDER_CALLS` and `FUSED_RENDER_CALLS_RETENTION_DAYS` beat the stored prefs inside `calls.enabled()`/`calls.retention_days()`, but the payload reported only the stored values — so the Preferences page showed **capture on, keep 90 days** while the process had **capture off, keep 1 day** (reproduced exactly that way). The engine block two lines above exists to prevent precisely this and says so in its own docstring — *"the SAME resolver the server's /api/run dispatch uses, so the page never reports a different running engine"*. Now mirrored: `effective_enabled`/`effective_retention_days` come from the resolvers the writer itself calls, `enabled_forced_by`/`retention_forced_by` carry the raw env value, the controls stay bound to the stored prefs (a PUT must round-trip and the choice applies once the var is removed) and are disabled while forced, and a muted line names the variable. | Three things. (a) **The fix is a call, not a copy.** The tempting shape is to re-derive precedence in the prefs layer; a test pins the effective pair to `calls.enabled()` across the spellings the real resolver accepts (`off`, `no`, `false`, `0` — it treats any non-false-ish value as on), which a reimplementation checking `== "0"` would fail. That is D146/D147's lesson applied *before* the drift instead of after. (b) **The sweep found a site the report didn't name**: the param and retention selects were gated on the stored `enabled`, so a forced-off process left them live and a forced-on one greyed them out while calls landed. Gated on `effective_enabled` now. (c) **Absence is a decision worth asserting.** The param mode has no env override, so it deliberately gets no `*_forced_by` pair — with a test that the keys stay absent, because an always-null pair would imply an override exists. That test passes on both sides by design, like D147's POSIX guards. |
| D150 | **`forced_by` means the variable is in force, not merely set** | D149's `*_forced_by` flags were `os.environ.get(...)` — non-null whenever the variable existed. But `calls.retention_days()` honours `FUSED_RENDER_CALLS_RETENTION_DAYS` only as an integer, so `=abc`, `=` or `=3.5` left the **writer on the stored pref** while the page disabled the retention select and captioned it *locked by FUSED_RENDER_CALLS_RETENTION_DAYS=abc* (reproduced: writer 30 days, UI locked and blaming the variable). The user could then change retention neither from the page nor by fixing a variable that was never in force. `calls.py` now owns the question as `retention_days_override()`/`enabled_override()` — returning what the variable imposes, or None when it imposes nothing — `retention_days()`/`enabled()` are expressed in terms of them, and prefs' one `_forced_by(env_name, override)` helper reports the raw value only when the override is real. | D149 fixed the `effective_*` half by asking the writer and left the `forced_by` half a presence check, which is the **same second copy of the precedence rule in the same function** — the tell being that presence and force coincide for `FUSED_RENDER_CALLS` (every set value decides, empty included) and diverge for retention, so one control looked right and the other was wrong. `0` is the case that keeps the resolver honest in both directions: a real override (age pruning off) that a truthiness check on the *resolved* value would drop, sitting one character from the empty string that must be ignored — so the tests pin `0`, `-5`, `""`, `abc` and `3.5` explicitly. Absence of an override is now stated by the writer rather than inferred by the reader. |
| D151 | **The call-log store is partitioned per app: `logs/<slug>-<hash>/`, keyed by the page's containing directory, resolved by ONE named resolver** | The flat store (D136) had three coupled defects: a quiet app's reads paid for a chatty app's volume, the size trim let one app evict another's history, and the gate's bounded newest-files probe filled with the busy app's files so a quiet page's Calls mode silently never appeared — a fourth cause of the D140-class false negative. Partition = the page's folder (an app is an `.html` + sibling `.py`s; a `.py` is never a record's `page`, so any per-page scheme strands the data-file lookup that D139/D147 fought for). `partition_name()` hashes `canonical_fs_path(normcase(realpath(dir)))` — symlinks and case folded BEFORE the bug this time, D147's lesson — behind a small LRU; the gate carries a pinned standalone duplicate like `_store_dir`. `query()` deliberately does NOT narrow to a partition — the existing filter test failed the narrowed build and was right: "this file" is a three-role match (D139/CL-16) and a template's record about your file lives under the TEMPLATE's partition, so only the gate narrows (partition-first, old global scan as fallback — its probe was always a bounded heuristic, so this strictly improves it); the size cap trims the largest partition first; the sweep reaps emptied dirs; `index.json` is advisory, written only on partition creation. Renames truncate (owner-accepted): a moved app is a new partition and the old history ages out. | The decisive rejection: a composite `<time>:<id>` cursor so partitions could stop independently. Unnecessary — the walk stays MERGED (`store_files()` spans partitions, the CL-12 day-merge is unchanged), so the identity stop, the shown-id guarantee, `cursor_missing` and the scan budget — five rounds of reviewed cursor semantics (D140–D146) — carry over verbatim instead of being re-litigated. Partitioning changes which FILES a filtered walk selects, never how the walk works. Per-partition quotas rejected (waste the cap when one app is active — the common case); a rename chain in the index rejected as bookkeeping that can drift for a rare event. No migration: a flat day file interleaves apps so files cannot be moved, and the log has never shipped — the reader ignores stray root files. |
| D152 | **The subprocess worker bootstraps the package onto its own `sys.path`, and the in-process allowlist covers both copies of every helper** | Reported symptom: the Calls view showed *"Could not read the call log: No module named 'fused_render'"* — and log_studio was fine. Cause was neither the call log nor the store: `_child.py` is spawned as a standalone SCRIPT, so `sys.path[0]` is the PACKAGE directory rather than its parent, and `import fused_render` resolves in the child only when the package is pip-installed into that interpreter. Reproduced by stripping exactly the path an editable install contributes, leaving site-packages: the child could not reach the real package. `_child.py` now appends the package's parent itself (a real install and the user's own module dir both keep precedence). Separately, `INPROCESS_HELPERS` listed only the STAGED core-templates copy, so a run served from the bundled original fell to the subprocess path silently; both copies are now listed, while a user fork under `~/.fused-render/templates/` deliberately stays user code on the subprocess path. | The diagnostic lesson: **the helper that broke was the only allowlisted one whose CORRECTNESS depended on the allowlist.** Every other in-process helper imports pandas/duckdb, so falling to the subprocess path costs them ~700 ms and nothing else — the same fall made this one fail outright. That asymmetry is exactly why the bug read as "the call log is broken" rather than "the child cannot import the package", and why log_studio working was the clue rather than a contrast: it is stdlib-only, so it never needed what was missing. Fixing only the allowlist would have restored the built-in path and left a user fork of the reader broken; fixing only the child would have left a silent per-poll spawn. Both halves, and a test for each — the child test drives the REAL child with the parent stripped, so it asserts the bootstrap rather than however the checkout happens to be installed (verified: it fails on the unfixed child). |
| D153 | **log_studio's expanded row renders a structured line as FIELDS, and the level facets list only the levels the file contains** | Two owner-reported gaps in the `.log` viewer. (a) The expanded row showed the whole line as one escaped `<pre>` string — worst of both worlds for a JSON log (too wide to read, and the field you want is buried mid-line), and the call log's own records are JSON, so the viewer the docs point at for them was the least readable way to read them. Now: `parseStructured` accepts leading text + a trailing JSON OBJECT (arrays and scalars are not field sets and are refused), renders nested objects indented under their key, keeps a multi-line or >160-char value as a block so a traceback's newlines survive, and makes each scalar a `[data-query]` chip that filters the log by that value — reusing the contract the patterns view already had, which is also why a click there does not collapse the row. A **Raw JSON** toggle returns the exact text on disk, per row, and survives Follow polls now that rows persist (D150-era reconcile). A plain-text line keeps the raw `<pre>` it always had. (b) The facet list offered all seven levels on every file, because the reader's `_counts()` seeds them at zero — correct for histogram buckets, which need a zero to stack, wrong as a filter menu: six controls that answer "no matching lines" on an INFO-only log. Filtering the LIST alone was wrong and was reported immediately: unchecking a level made the absent ones reappear, because the click handler recomputed "which levels exist" from the unfiltered `overviewLevels` while the list used the filtered set — so unchecking WARN wrote all six others into the URL, and the list kept them since "selected" beats the count filter. It also broke the all-selected reset (`next.length === available.length` could never hold against seven). One `facetLevels(data, selected)` resolver now answers for both, and `overviewLevels` has exactly one caller. | Two things worth keeping. **Nothing in the line may be dropped to make it pretty**: the text before the JSON is shown as its own `(line prefix)` row rather than discarded, because a view that silently hides part of a log line is worse than one that does not parse it. **Filtering a facet list by count has two traps.** A SELECTED level at zero is reachable (a narrowed window, a rotated file), and hiding its chip removes the only control that undoes an active constraint — a filter the user can neither see nor clear; so selection beats the count. And the list is not the only place that decides which levels exist — the control that WRITES the filter decides too, and filtering only the display left the two disagreeing, which is D147/D149's one-rule-two-copies shape in a fourth place. The tell was available at the time: the handler's `available` had to mean the same thing as the list's, and a shared resolver was the way to say so once. Tests drive the full click round-trip (click -> URL param -> re-parse -> re-render), which is what catches a mismatch between the two sides; verified failing on the display-only version. Values are HTML-escaped in both the cell and the `data-query` attribute (log content is untrusted text), and recursion is depth-bounded. Tests extract the shipping functions and run them under node — a copy would keep passing after the real code regressed — and the facet tests were verified to fail against the unfiltered list. |
| D154 | **log_studio drops its own theme button and becomes a tier-1 view: one resolver in the codebase, and no built-in view both opts in and keeps a switch** | Third attempt at the same question, and the first one that removes rather than adds. The viewer hardcoded `data-theme="light"`, so it opened WHITE inside a dark app; D153's round gave it a private resolver reading the same `fused-render:theme` key — which fixed the default and **silently broke its toggle**, because the toggle writes `?theme=` through `fused.params` and that lands on the SHELL's URL (D46), not the iframe's, so the view's `pinned()` never saw it and the resolver won every time. The button is gone, the private resolver with it, and the view now carries the ordinary AP-8 `data-fused-theme` opt-in: `static/runtime.js` resolves, applies pre-paint and follows (`storage`, `matchMedia`). Fallout that mattered: **six level accents had no light value** (`--danger/--fatal/--info/--warn/--debug/--trace`) and inherited hues picked against `#101318` — latent while light was toggle-only, the common case once light became the default for a light-mode user; **13 colour literals** outside the palettes (submit button, tooltips, notice bar) are now tokenized, which the tier-1 literal test requires; the charts read the palette through `getComputedStyle` at draw time, so a `MutationObserver` on `data-theme` re-draws them; the `data-theme="light"` literal on `<html>` is dropped (dead — the runtime overwrites it before first paint — and a difference from the other twelve); and the collapsed rail, which kept a 44px strip only so the toggle stayed reachable, now hides outright. AP-9 gains `log_studio`, AP-11 loses it and gains the general rule, AP-13 is narrowed to "no signal *by default*" (the opt-in is a property of the document, not of its author), and the storage key is back to exactly three readers. | The rule worth keeping is the one AP-11 now states: **a view either opts in or owns its appearance — never both.** An in-view switch layered on top of a pushed theme is not a switch, it is a value that gets overwritten by the next `storage` or OS event, and the failure is silent because the button still animates. That is what made this three rounds instead of one: round two treated "opens white in a dark app" as a missing default and bolted a resolver alongside a control it had not checked still worked, which is the same one-rule-two-copies shape as D147/D149/D153 — here the two copies were *the shell's resolver and the view's*, and the view's lost. Removing the control was cheaper and better than repairing pinning: the app already has a three-way Appearance setting, and a per-view override of it is a feature nobody asked for. Second lesson, on latency: **making a code path the default converts its latent defects into live ones.** The six missing light tokens had been wrong since D134 and were invisible because reaching light mode required clicking a button most users never clicked; the tier-1 palette test catches exactly this and is why the promotion surfaced them in one run rather than in a bug report. `excel` and `tableau` keep their buttons and their private keys — they never opted in, so nothing overwrites them, and AP-11 is now about that consistency rather than about a list. |
| D155 | **Self-review round: the log viewer's Raw/Fields toggle is fixed at the rule rather than the instance, and the worker's PYTHONPATH stops outranking the caller's** | Five fixes, two owner-reported and three found by reading the diff. (1) **The Raw JSON / Fields toggle did nothing**, on exactly the records worth reading. Two defects stacked: the raw text was addressed by POSITION (`querySelector("pre")`) while `fieldRows` renders any multi-line or >160-char value as its own `<pre class="field-block">` INSIDE the table — so a record with a traceback has two and the nested one is first, and clicking Raw un-hid a node inside the table it had just hidden. It now has a name, `.detail-raw`. (2) Then `.hidden` **did not hide**: the UA's `[hidden] { display: none }` loses to any class rule setting `display`, and this template sets one on nearly every box, so with (1) fixed the raw text appeared UNDERNEATH the fields. Measured in Chromium — `el.hidden = true` on `#pager` left `display: flex`, height 47. `.field-table` (grid), `#pager` (flex) and `.context-lines` (grid, a 2px sliver) were all affected; `.hist-skel[hidden]` already carried a one-off `display: none` for exactly this. Replaced by one global `[hidden] { display: none !important }` and the one-off removed. (3) `enrich_write` mapped **any** 403 to `readonly`, including the X-Fused guard's refusal — a `unauthorized` flag now separates them, derived by re-asking `_require_fused` rather than re-spelling the rule. (4) `calls_dir` reached the runtime as raw `abspath`, i.e. backslashed on Windows, where every path the runtime holds is forward-slashed — canonicalized through the codec this change itself introduced. (5) `_child_env` **prepended** the package's parent to PYTHONPATH; it now appends, matching `_child.py`'s own `sys.path.append`. | The through-line is the one D144/D147/D149/D151/D153 keep finding: **a bug report names an instance, and the fix belongs at the rule.** (2) is the clearest case in the set — the trap had already been hit once and patched at `.hist-skel`, which is precisely why it was still live everywhere else; one global rule also covers the element nobody has written yet. (5) is a reversal worth stating, because the original reasoning was *locally* true and still wrong: prepending was justified with "the user's own module directory still wins, since `run()` puts it at `sys.path[0]`" — true, but the wrong axis. What it displaced was the caller's PYTHONPATH, and on an installed layout the prepended value IS site-packages, so it reversed the one override PYTHONPATH exists to provide, for every module rather than for this package. Appending satisfies the actual requirement (the entry has to be REACHABLE, not first) and cannot displace anything. Verifying it also produced a useful near-miss: the first repro, `import tests` against the repo root, did NOT shadow — `tests/` has no `__init__.py`, and a namespace package has lowest precedence by design, so a regular module later on the path wins regardless of order. The effect is real only for REGULAR packages, which is what site-packages holds; the demonstration was redone with two of those. A repro that fails to reproduce is not the same as a bug that is not there, and the difference was the mechanism, not the severity. Every behavioural fix here was pinned by a test verified failing against the unfixed code first, and the two UI fixes were confirmed in a real browser rather than at source level — the standing lesson from §9.5's green-spike failures. |
| D156 | **The supersession mark rides the superseding request (`X-Fused-Supersedes`) instead of a deferred POST, and the worker's bootstrap diagnostic fires only for the package itself** | Two Bugbot findings, both upheld. (1) **The supersession report lost a race with fast calls.** `reportSuperseded` deferred `POST /api/calls/event` by `setTimeout(0)` to batch, while `finish()` stamps `superseded` only if the mark is ALREADY present — the store is append-only, so a late mark cannot reclassify a written line. Measured in Chromium against a local server, that POST landed **~19 ms after the abort**; every abandoned call whose handler finished inside that window was written `ok` and averaged into the latency percentiles, which is exactly what CL-5 exists to prevent, and in-process helpers (D72) finish that fast routinely — so a template re-querying per keystroke hit it repeatedly. The mark now travels as `X-Fused-Supersedes` on the request that CAUSED the supersession: one only ever happens because the page is issuing a new call on the same channel, and that request leaves in the same synchronous task as the abort, so `begin()` takes the ids at the earliest moment the server sees anything. The header TAKES the queued ids rather than copying them (a duplicate arriving after `finish()` consumed the mark would sit in the map until its TTL); the POST survives as the `pagehide` backstop, where no request is left to carry them. Verified end to end in a browser: the superseding `/api/run` carries the abandoned id, **no `/api/calls/event` request is made at all**, and that id is recorded `superseded`/DEBUG with the rollup showing `count=8 superseded=4`. (2) **The worker's ImportError diagnostic was too broad** — it matched on the first dotted segment, so a missing SUBMODULE under an importable package got the bootstrap blame (executable, PYTHONPATH, sys.path). Now an exact `e.name == "fused_render"`. | (1) is the round's real lesson and it is about **evidence, not design**: the deferred POST was defended in a comment that reasoned "the superseded run keeps executing for as long as it would have, so the report reliably lands before the record is written". That is true of the case the author pictured — a slow run superseded by a slider tick — and false of the case that matters here, because the calls that lose the race are precisely the FAST ones. The comment was plausible, was never measured, and stood through eleven review rounds; the fix took one `PerformanceObserver` reading. Anywhere a correctness argument rests on "X reliably happens before Y" across a network boundary, the number is cheap and the intuition is not. Worth noting what the fix did NOT do: it does not make the ordering a guarantee — request #2 could still, in principle, reach the server after the abandoned call's record is written — it removes a macrotask and a whole round trip, which is the most that can be done without buffering records server-side, and buffering would trade a rare mislabel for a delay on every record. (2) is a smaller instance of the same discipline as D152: `.split(".")[0]` looked equivalent to an exact match because the author only had the package-missing case in mind, and Python's own semantics settle it — a missing package reports `name` as the top-level name even for a submodule import, while a missing submodule reports the full dotted path, so the exact match is not merely narrower but precisely right. |
| D157 | **`claude`, `history`, `annotate` and `zip` become tier-1 views; `claude` leaves the light-by-design list it never belonged on, and `zip`'s breadcrumb/Extract bar moves to the bottom of the pane** | Four AP-9 conversions of the ordinary shape: `data-fused-theme="shell"` on `<html>`, a `:root` dark palette that IS each view's previous appearance value-for-value, and a `:root[data-theme="light"]` twin over the same token set. `zip` was already fully tokenized and needed only the twin. `history` had a partial palette plus 19 stray literals (the dot grid, the continue banner's gradient and both its borders, the warn/error cards, the timeline node ring, the accent glow, the skeleton shine); `--spine-x`, a geometry constant, moved out of `:root` onto `body` rather than being duplicated into the light block to satisfy the equal-token-sets rule. `claude` had 6 tokens and 18 literals, and its brand orange is the one value that could not carry over — `#d97757` is ~2.6:1 on white and serves as body text (the working verb, bullets, the beta badge) as well as a fill, so light gets the same hue at `#b8542f`. `annotate` had **no palette at all** — one `color-scheme: dark` and 57 literals — and is the only one of the four with colour outside CSS: the Custom Highlight API paints inside the FRAMED document, so its two tints are resolved off our own `<html>` with `getComputedStyle` and rewritten from a single `MutationObserver` on `data-theme` (one observer for the page's life — the frame reloads on every view switch, and re-observing per load would stack them). Separately, `zip`'s header became a footer: the listing is what the eye lands on, and the Extract button now holds a fixed position instead of being pushed around by a growing breadcrumb. `#notice` follows it down. | The interesting one is `claude`. It sat in AP-10 **light by design** while rendering `#131417` — a dark view labelled light — and nothing caught it, because AP-10's whole contract is "carries none of the tier-1 structure", which a dark view satisfies just as well as a light one. The exhaustiveness test (AP-14) proves every template is *classified*, not that its classification is *true*; the four buckets are only distinguishable by what a view does NOT have. Worth remembering the next time a list is made exhaustive and that is mistaken for making it correct. `annotate` is the other lesson: a view with zero tokens looks like the cheapest possible retheme and is the most expensive, because every literal has to be given a NAME and a role before it can be given a light value — and the ones that turn out not to be CSS at all (the Highlight API strings) are exactly the ones no palette test can see. The deferred list keeps the views where the colour lives in a canvas, a map style or a third-party viewer; these four left it because they are plain DOM chrome. `annotate`'s stage is still whatever the framed document chose to be, which is correct — that document opts in on its own terms. |
| D158 | **The `csv` template is deleted; `.csv`/`.tsv` fall through to `duckdb` as the default and `excel`/`code` as the alternates** | `csv/` was a 221-line pandas-backed paged table — the same job `duckdb` already did as the FIRST mode on both keys, so nothing reached it unless a user picked it out of the switcher. Removed: the folder, its entry in `executor.INPROCESS_HELPERS` (the in-process allowlist is now duckdb/structure/xlsx/sqlite readers + the api inspector), its `NICE` label in `learn/list_templates.py`, and its place in the AP-9 tier-1 list. `registry.json` drops the mode from `.csv` (`duckdb, excel, code, reader, annotate`) and `.tsv` (`duckdb, code, reader, annotate`). Tests that used `"csv"` as a stand-in for *a real builtin template* switch to `excel`, which is still bound to the same key; `test_core_templates.py`'s tier-1 delivery check parametrizes on `text` instead. A user registry that still names `csv` is not an error — it resolves to no folder and surfaces as a dangling `exists: false` ref, the same as any other unknown name (D73). | The one thing lost is the **pandas** path: `duckdb` needs duckdb installed, and on an install without it `.csv` now falls to `excel` (pandas/openpyxl) and then `code`. That is a real narrowing of the fallback chain, accepted because `duckdb` ships in the bundled extra and `excel` covers `.csv` natively. Worth noting for the general case: a template being *reachable* is not the same as it being *used* — a mode that is never first in any registry list is only ever seen by someone who went looking for it in the switcher, which is the signal that it has stopped earning its maintenance. Historical decisions D59 and D72 still name `csv` and are left alone: they record what was true when they were made. |
| D159 | **`fused.ai` runs the `claude` (Claude Code) CLI directly instead of relaying to a local OpenAI-compatible proxy** | `/api/ai` invokes the `claude` binary as a one-shot completion subprocess (`-p --output-format json --tools= --setting-sources= --no-session-persistence --max-turns 1` — the empty flags in single-token equals form, since cmd.exe's %* expansion behind a Windows .cmd shim drops separate empty argv elements — the prompt over **stdin** — argv has an OS size cap that a data-heavy prompt can blow — the system prompt via `--system-prompt-file` (a temp file, deleted after the call: no user-controlled string may enter argv, since cmd.exe re-parses it behind a Windows .cmd shim and cannot be escaped reliably; model ids are charset-validated `[A-Za-z0-9._-]+` for the same reason), replacing the shipped agent prompt, `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS` carrying the effort→tokens mapping). Binary from PATH, `FUSED_RENDER_CLAUDE_BIN` overrides; the `ai_base_url` pref and `FUSED_RENDER_AI_BASE_URL` are removed. The wire contract is unchanged (`{ok, result:{text, model, usage}}`, same validation and 400s); error types gain `"timeout"`, and `"ai_unavailable"` now means "binary not found/runnable". The model echo prefers the full id from the CLI's `modelUsage` key, so an alias request (`sonnet`) reports the model that actually ran. | The proxy asked the user to install, run and keep alive a separate service before the first `fused.ai` call worked — for an audience that already has Claude Code installed and logged in, which makes the CLI both the credential and the runtime with zero setup. Local-only stance (RH-11) is unchanged: the CLI lives on the author's machine, so the exporter still rejects hosted pages. Rejected: keeping both backends behind the pref (two code paths to test for an MVP with one real user population); calling the Anthropic API directly (requires key management the shell deliberately avoids). Subprocess-per-call over a persistent session matches the executor's own fresh-subprocess discipline (D70). |

### Markdown v2 — read/write notes, wikilinks and the link graph (2026-07-28)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D153 | **Markdown v2: one template that reads, writes, resolves `[[wikilinks]]`, shows backlinks and graphs them — with read/write behaviour copied from Obsidian rather than invented** | Full contract in SPEC §32 (MD-1..MD-22); design and rejected alternatives in `docs/markdown-graph.html`. Shape: `templates/markdown/graph.py` is the one data module — parse, resolve, index, assemble — and all three surfaces read it (the note view, the local graph panel inside it, and the new folder-level `graph` directory mode, which calls it as `../markdown/graph.py` because `/api/run` resolves a relative `py` against the page's directory). `.md` keeps its mode list; the universal `/` key gains `graph` as a CT-12 conditional peer. The editor is the CM6 bundle we already ship, rebuilt with `@codemirror/lang-markdown` on a GFM base. The rendered surface tokenizes wikilinks and tags through `marked`'s own inline-extension hooks rather than pre-processing the source. | Obsidian parity is a *reachable* target rather than an aspiration precisely because Obsidian's editor IS CodeMirror 6 and its Live Preview is a decoration set over the source document — the mechanism we already vendor for annotate mode. **One Python module over one-per-surface**: the failure mode of three surfaces is that they disagree about what a link is, and a shared module makes that impossible rather than unlikely. **marked extensions over a source pre-pass**: a pre-pass would need a second copy of `graph.py`'s code-masking rule in JS, and the two copies would drift on the first edge case (a wikilink inside a fence). marked already knows what is inside a fence, so the duplication is simply not created. Rejected: teaching the existing `code` template about markdown (it is the plain-text escape hatch and should stay one), and a separate `notes` template (the `.md` default IS the note view). |
| D154 | **The index stores RAW link targets and resolves at assembly time; an ambiguous target is a ghost, never a guess** | `parse_note` records the target exactly as authored; `resolve_link` turns it into a path against the candidate set that exists *now*, tried shortest-first (sibling folder → vault root → path suffix → bare basename, case-insensitively, extension optional). Two notes sharing a basename with no path to choose between them resolve to **nothing** — a dim ghost node and a dashed link. Backlinks are computed the same way, by resolving every other note's links, never from a stored reverse index. The `[[` popup's inserted form is chosen by running each candidate form through `resolve_link` itself and taking the shortest that comes back to that note, with a property test over a vault of colliding basenames. | Caching a resolved edge is the one mistake that makes incremental updates *wrong* rather than merely slow: renaming `Foo.md` silently changes what every other note's `[[Foo]]` points at, and a stored reverse index would keep asserting the old answer. So the invalidation story stays honest — a row is invalid only when its own file changed. **Ambiguity as a ghost** over Obsidian's nearest-file tie-break: picking one of two `Design.md`s makes the graph assert an edge the author never wrote, and the dashed link is a prompt to disambiguate rather than a silent wrong answer. **The popup's link form is derived from the resolver, not from a parallel "shortest path" rule** — that is the D146/D147 lesson applied before the drift instead of after: two implementations of one rule need a test that asserts they agree, and the cheapest such test is to make one of them call the other. |
| D155 | **Obsidian's save model is adopted wholesale — no save button, no dirty indicator, no prompt — with exactly one deviation (conflicts) and no stat ticker to detect external changes** | 2 s idle autosave, plus a flush on blur, tab switch, `pagehide` and before any navigation this view initiates (via `__fusedFlushEdits`, so a mode switch cannot drop edits); `⌘S` only forces the flush early. Saves are single-flight and re-compare the buffer after the write, so edits landing mid-write stay dirty. `fused.autoReload(false)`. **The deviation:** a clean buffer silently reloads when the file moves under it, exactly as Obsidian; a dirty buffer whose `mtime` moved gets a reload-or-keep banner, autosave suspends while it is up, and "keep my version" is the one write without `expectedMtime`. External changes are noticed on focus / visibility change and at the next save through the optimistic lock — **not** by polling `stat`. Read-only comes off `stat.writable` (which consults `mounts.mount_read_only`), so the editor opens locked. | The absence of save ceremony IS the behaviour being copied, so the earlier dirty-dot/save-button sketch was dropped deliberately rather than forgotten — a dirty indicator would be a visible admission that saving is something the user has to think about. **The conflict deviation is not a preference**: Obsidian assumes it is the only writer in the vault, and fused-render sits on shared and mount-backed paths where that is false, so last-write-wins would eat somebody's edit. **No stat ticker** is the interesting call: a poll is exactly the traffic that killed a mount in the `fs/events` stat-storm incident, and branching on mount-ness in the template would break the standing rule that templates stay mount-agnostic. Focus/visibility plus the `expectedMtime` lock covers every case a poll would, a moment later — accepted. **`stat.writable` over `os.access`**: on an rclone mount with `CacheMode=full` a doomed write succeeds locally and 403s later on async upload, so `os.access` is a lie there and the honest answer already exists in the shell's persisted `read_only` flag. |
| D156 | **The graph does not support remote mounts at all — refused in two independent places, never bounded** | The folder mode's `condition.py` returns `False` for any mount-backed path (so the mode is never offered), and `graph.py` refuses a mount-backed root **before it walks anything or even creates the index directory**, returning a clear `mount_unsupported` result. Both call the app's own `shell.mounts.is_mount_backed` rather than carrying a copy of the rule, and both treat a failed import of it as *refuse* rather than *guess*. Single-file `.md` read and write on a mount stay fully supported. The gate additionally never enumerates a directory (CT-12): mount check, one `isdir`, then two `os.path.isfile` probes for the vault marker `index.md` (`Index.md` too, for a case-sensitive filesystem) — with the tests making `listdir`/`scandir`/`walk`/`glob` **fatal** inside a gate call. The marker set was originally a wider guess at an "entry-point note" (`README.md`, `readme.md`, `Home.md`, `notes.md`, `.markdown` variants); it is now `index.md` only, because `README.md` is in essentially every code repository and the mode was therefore offered on every checkout on the disk, where a link graph says nothing. Narrowing it costs discoverability, not capability: `_mode=graph` still reaches the folder mode by hand, and the note view's own graph panel is untouched. | Owner instruction, and the right shape anyway: a recursive walk over a remote mount is precisely the pattern that wedges it (kernel NFS listing on a flat million-key prefix — the MUR SST incident), so bounding the risk is strictly worse than not taking it. **Two halves rather than one**: the gate is the UX (nothing offered, nothing to click) and the module is the guarantee (a hand-written `_mode=graph` URL or a `root` param cannot reach the walk). Either alone would be a rule that holds only until someone types a URL. **A refusal, never a partial walk** — a truncated graph over a mount would look like a working feature while silently omitting most of the vault, and the user has no way to tell those apart. **Enumeration made fatal in tests** rather than documented: `zarr_aoi/condition.py` explains the rule in prose and this gate is the second place it binds, so the cheap way to keep it true is a test that fails when it is broken, not a third copy of the paragraph. **The marker, not a likely-note list**: what the gate is actually asking is "is this a notes vault?", and the wide list answered a different, easier question ("does this folder contain any conventional markdown entry point?") whose answer is yes for most of a developer's disk. `index.md` is the convention a vault's own entry note follows and a repository's does not, so it separates the two cases with the same two stats — and the tests now assert BOTH directions, since a gate that says yes to everything passes any test that only checks the yes. |
| D157 | **The graph canvas was extracted to `templates/shared/` the moment the second surface needed it; Live Preview decorations are deliberately NOT built, and `view=live` resolves to Source rather than lying** | `templates/shared/graph-canvas.js` (served from the existing `/template-shared/` mount, like `ro-badge.js`) holds the sim and every interaction rule; the markdown template's local panel and the folder-level `graph` mode both drive it. A hand-rolled O(n²) spring layout on Canvas 2D — no force library vendored. Colours are read from CSS custom properties at draw time, because `var()` cannot resolve inside a `fillStyle`. **Not built, listed in MD-18a rather than left implied:** Live Preview decorations (the vendored primitives are in place and the mode is accepted as an alias of Source), a frontmatter properties table, inline note embeds, image paste/drop, `.fused-graph.json` tuning, gitignore-awareness of the walk, and a watch-registry subscription. | Extracting at the second use (not the first, not the third) is what kept this from becoming the fourth inline copy of a thing — the shell-navigation helper in this same template is the counter-example, a rule already duplicated inline in `history`, `canvas` and `preview`, which this change does **not** fix and which is called out as a follow-up rather than quietly extended. **`view=live` resolving to Source** over dropping the value: a URL saved against a later version then degrades instead of breaking, and only the two modes that exist are offered as buttons — a third button that behaved like Source would be a mode that lies. **Naming the omissions in the SPEC** is the point of this entry: the design doc describes Live Preview as the largest single piece and explicitly sequenced it last so the valuable parts land without it, and an unbuilt item that is written down as unbuilt is a decision, whereas one that is merely absent reads as an oversight the next reader has to rediscover. *(The Live Preview clause of this entry is superseded by D158 — it is now built, and the mode alias it describes no longer exists.)* |
| D158 | **The markdown template is one Live Preview surface: no reading/source modes, no toolbar, `code` left untouched — and the decoration layer is covered by executing it, not by asserting about its source** | `marked` and the whole rendered-HTML pipeline are gone (`renderReading`, `rewriteRelativeImages`, `rewriteRelativeLinks`, `enableTaskBoxes`, the mode buttons, the `view` param, `⌘E`). What replaces them is a single `Decoration` set over the CM6 document, revealing raw source per **line** the selection touches. The toolbar is gone with them: the shell's breadcrumb names the file, Obsidian shows no save state, so the read-only badge and save *failures* float, and the only persistent chrome is a 26px toggle opening a right sidebar that holds backlinks **and** the graph (Obsidian's layout). `code` stays exactly as it was and stays registered for `.md`. New: `scripts/vendor-codemirror/live-preview-probe.mjs` + `tests/test_markdown_live_preview.py` run the real builder against the real grammar headlessly. | **One surface over Obsidian's two** because the split does not transfer: in Obsidian, Reading and Editing are leaves of one `MarkdownView`, so the editor instance — and with it the caret and the undo history — survives `⌘E`. Here a mode is a *template swap*, so the same toggle would cost more and buy less; and a read-only file already gives us the reading view for free, as decorations over a locked buffer, which unifies that path instead of branching it. **Two editors for `.md` with two save models is accepted, not reconciled** (MD-16 here, AS-1 in `code`): the owner asked for `code` untouched, `annotate` already writes `.md` on its own terms, and a user who picks the generic text editor asked for generic behaviour. Stating the divergence in SPEC beats a refactor nobody wanted. **The probe is the load-bearing part of this entry.** Everything else here is visible in a diff; buildDecorations is not, because its correctness depends entirely on what the vendored grammar calls each range — and the grammar was wrong twice in ways no source assertion could catch: `---\ntitle: x\n---` parses as `HorizontalRule` + `SetextHeading2` (so YAML rendered as a rule plus a heading), and the inner brackets of `[[Wiki]]` parse as a `Link` node, which an over-broad code-guard silently treated as un-linkable — no wikilinks rendered at all. Both were found by *running* it, within minutes, after the source looked correct. CM also throws on overlapping replacements, so building the set is itself the collision check; the tests skip where the gitignored vendor `node_modules` is absent rather than pretending to pass. |
| D159 | **Markdown follow-ups: decorations move to a StateField, the scan root climbs to a vault marker, "unknown" stops masquerading as "missing", read-only becomes a mode rather than a second view, and the palette is Obsidian's neutrals with fused's accent** | Seven changes to `templates/markdown` and the shared canvas. (1) The Live Preview decoration set is a **`StateField`**, not a `ViewPlugin`, and `buildDecorations` takes a *state* and walks the whole document. (2) The note view's default scan root **climbs** from the note's folder to the nearest ancestor holding `.obsidian/`, `.fused-graph.json` or `.git`, bounded to 8 levels, never entering a mount, `isdir`/`isfile` probes only; `main` also coerces a string `depth`. (3) `resolved === null` now means **unknown**, rendering wikilinks inert (no ghost, no `data-create`), with the sidebar saying why in graph.py's words. (4) A **read-only/editing mode** in the `edit` param, implemented as the same two CM facets the unwritable-file path already used. (5) The side panel is **resizable** (localStorage, not params) and the graph layout is roughly twice as open and **fitted to the canvas**. (6) A ghost is only offered where a target could *be* a note, and creating one cannot escape the scan root. (7) Both palettes are Obsidian's `--color-base-*` neutrals, with the editing surface forced to the page's own background. | **The StateField is a bug fix, not tidying.** CM throws "Decorations that replace line breaks may not be specified via plugins", and the table widget replaces a multi-line `Table` node — so scrolling a note that contained a table threw mid-update and abandoned the render, which is what the user saw as parts of the page going white. A state field is *allowed* to span line breaks, so the constraint disappears instead of being worked around; the cost is a whole-document walk (fine at MD-10's 256 KB cap) and one subtlety worth knowing: the language extension parses in the background, so the field must also rebuild when the syntax **tree** advances or everything past the parse frontier stays undecorated. The probe could not have caught this — it calls the builder directly and never builds an `EditorView` — so the coverage is a *pair*: a source test that the decorations come from a StateField, and a probe test that a replacement really does cross a newline, neither meaningful alone. **The root climb** is the owner's actual complaint: `dirname(file)` meant every link leaving the note's folder was a ghost and every inbound link from outside it invisible, so a real note sat in a halo of grey `../…` ghosts. Marker-based rather than "walk up until it looks like a vault", because a *probe* is constant-time and a *listing* is not — the CT-12 discipline, here doubly binding since the ascent decides the scope of a walk. It refuses to cross into a mount so that a local note under a mounted folder is scanned where it is rather than answered `mount_unsupported`. **Unknown vs missing** is the same class of error twice: a ghost asserts "this note does not exist" and an empty backlinks panel asserts "nothing links here", and on the no-scan path *nobody looked*. Both now say so. The page still resolves nothing itself (MD-6) — its only two answers are graph.py's map and "we do not know". **Read-only as facets, not a view:** the reading/writing split was deliberately removed in D158 and does not come back; a mode that changes only writability costs two facets and a rebuild whose caret and scroll `buildEditor` already preserves. Editing stays the default. The reveal guard in `selectedLines` turned out to be load-bearing rather than defensive: verified in a real browser, a text selection inside a non-editable view *does* reach the state's selection (`anchor:115, head:182` after swiping one line), which without the guard would un-render whatever was swiped over. **Graph spacing was measured, not eyeballed** — the sim run headlessly over a panel-sized neighbourhood and folder webs of 60/200/600, counting label collisions at the resulting zoom. That killed the first attempt (scaling spacing by node count) as *worse*: once the layout is fitted to the canvas, scaling it uniformly changes nothing on screen, so the knob that should vary per surface is the zoom. It also exposed a pre-existing failure — all nodes seeded on one 70px ring, with velocity accumulating across steps, threw a 600-note graph 1263x2016px in a 1200x800 canvas *before* any change here — fixed with disc seeding and a speed ceiling. **The palette borrows Obsidian's neutrals but not its accent.** `oneDark` was the diagnosis behind "the dark theme is ugly": it ships its own `#282c34` background, a blue cursor, red headings and green markdown markers, so the editor was a second, lighter, bluer slab inside the page with an acid-yellow accent fighting it — and the owner's specific gripe, the red title, was `oneDark`'s `tags.heading`. It is kept only for fenced-code colours; every surface colour is overridden to a token, and prose highlight spans are reset to `inherit` outside fences, so the Live Preview's own classes are the only thing colouring the document. Obsidian's own violet accent was tried and rejected by the owner as off against the greys — which is the point of a neutral scale: it carries any accent, so fused's yellow stays. Light `--warn` is darkened (2.8:1 on white is not readable for the one message in this view that must be read), and light `--accent` likewise. The costs accepted: fence markers keep `oneDark`'s green (they are inside a code block); a `HighlightStyle` of our own would need the vendored bundle rebuilt, whose blast radius covers `code` and `annotate`, so it was not done. |

### Claude chat template on Windows (2026-07-28)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D160 | **The claude template finds `claude.exe` in Claude Code's install locations rather than requiring it on PATH — and its detach, liveness and cancel paths each take the win32 route instead of the POSIX one** | `_claude_bin` resolution order is now `FUSED_RENDER_CLAUDE_BIN` → `shutil.which` → per-platform install locations, with the Windows list ordered `%USERPROFILE%\.local\bin\claude.exe` (PowerShell installer), winget's `Links` shim dir, npm's `%APPDATA%\npm` prefix, legacy `%USERPROFILE%\.claude\local` — `.exe` ahead of any `.cmd`. Three POSIX-only mechanisms are replaced on Windows: `start_new_session=True` (accepted and ignored there) becomes `DETACHED_PROCESS \| CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP`; `os.kill(pid, 0)` becomes `../shared/procutil.pid_alive`; `os.killpg(pid, SIGTERM)` becomes `taskkill /PID <pid> /T /F`. Two adjacent fixes rode along: `PROJECTS` follows `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (the supervisor sets it in every packaged build), and `_bad_id` rejects `\` and a drive prefix in `run_id`/`session_id`, not just `/`. `tests/test_claude_agent_windows.py` simulates win32 from any host (patching `os.name` plus the two win32-only creationflags constants) and is also wired into the real `windows-desktop` CI job. | The PATH assumption was the *reported* bug and the least interesting one: the Windows installer appends `%USERPROFILE%\.local\bin` to the user PATH in the registry, so a process that inherited its PATH at login — which is every GUI-launched app, and the supervisor's children — cannot see a `claude` that works perfectly in a fresh terminal. **The `os.kill(pid, 0)` bug is the one worth remembering.** It is the canonical POSIX liveness probe and it silently means something else on Windows: signal 0 *is* `CTRL_C_EVENT`, so Python routes it to `GenerateConsoleCtrlEvent` — a Ctrl+C send that either lands or errors, and the error made `_alive` condemn a healthy run. The page polls 400 ms after `start`, so the first poll ended every turn as *"claude exited unexpectedly"* — our own string, with an empty `err.log`, which reads as a Claude Code crash and is not one. This repo already knew: `templates/shared/procutil.py` exists precisely for this and documents the aliasing in a comment; the claude template simply never used it. The flashing terminal window came from the same class of mistake one level down — `executor.py` already passes `CREATE_NO_WINDOW` so a windowless server doesn't flash a console per run, but the worker then spawned a console-subsystem `claude.exe` with no flags of its own, and a console-less parent forces Windows to allocate a new console. So the lesson is not "port the POSIX call" but **a POSIX-only kwarg that the platform accepts and ignores leaves no trace at the call site** — `start_new_session` raised nothing, warned nothing, and simply did not detach. Rejected: reading the user PATH back out of `HKCU\Environment` to pick up a post-login install (the install locations answer the same question without a registry dependency, and a non-standard install dir is what the env override is for); refusing a `.cmd` shim outright (it works, it is just second choice); and testing only on the real Windows runner (that job is path-gated and would leave the Linux matrix blind to a win32 regression, so the branches are simulated there too). |

### Claude template: tool approvals reach the browser (2026-07-28)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D161 | **The chat window becomes the permission prompt, via `--permission-prompt-tool` and a shipped one-tool MCP server; `--permission-mode acceptEdits` is dropped** | New `templates/claude/permission_server.py` — a stdlib-only stdio MCP server whose single `approve` tool writes each request to `perm/<id>.req.json` under the run dir and blocks until `agent.py` drops `<id>.res.json` next to it. `_start` generates the run's `mcp.json` (`command: sys.executable`, perm dir in argv, per-server `timeout` above the server's own wait) and spawns claude with `--mcp-config` + `--permission-prompt-tool mcp__fused_approvals__approve`. `poll` returns the run's whole request list (not just the unanswered ones, so a re-attaching frame rebuilds cards it never saw) and reports `phase: "awaiting"` while any is parked; a new `decide` action writes the verdict; `cancel` releases every parked request before killing the group. `template.html` renders each request as a card — tool name, a per-tool summary (Bash command, Edit `-`/`+`, Write content), and Allow / "Allow all X in this reply" / Deny — all through `textContent`. Three properties are load-bearing: the request id is **minted by us**, never the CLI's `tool_use_id` (it is joined into a path); the decision file is a **one-way latch** (`O_EXCL`, first writer wins) so a double-click or a cancel racing an allow cannot overwrite a verdict the tool already acted on; and anything that is not the exact string `allow` **fails closed**. A reader that *loses* the create waits the write out (`DECISION_WRITE_WINDOW`) instead of substituting its own verdict — `O_EXCL` publishes the file's existence before its content, and reading that gap as "nobody answered" is a real misreport (Bugbot, PR #308): the loser of a double-click showed *its* verdict on the card, and the server's timeout claim could hand claude a deny for a tool the user had just allowed. `poll` is the one reader that never waits (it re-reads every 400 ms), the UI re-renders a card whose polled verdict differs from the optimistic one, and a writer that dies after the create unlinks the file it claimed — an empty one holds the latch forever while never parsing. `--disallowed-tools AskUserQuestion,ExitPlanMode` because naming a prompt tool also un-gates those two in headless mode. Wire contract, flags and the spawn line are pinned by `tests/test_claude_permission_bridge.py`, which drives the server over its own stdio JSON-RPC and never invokes `claude`. | The reported symptom was "approval prompts not coming through", and the honest description of the old behaviour is worse than that: they were not *late*, they were **never asked**. Headless claude denied anything the session's rules did not already allow with `tool requires user interaction; no prompt available in headless mode` — a refusal with no prompt, no error and no trace in the UI, so Claude simply appeared to give up. `acceptEdits` was never the fix for that; it only widened the silent-yes to file edits (anywhere on disk) while leaving every Bash/WebFetch on the silent-no path. Dropping it is therefore not a separate hardening — the ask *becomes* answerable in the same change that makes it visible. **MCP over the SDK's `canUseTool`**: `canUseTool` rides the `--input-format stream-json` control protocol, which needs a live stdin pipe for the process's whole life, and this template's executor is fresh-process-per-call (PY-6) with a detached run — there is no writer to keep that pipe open. A file-mediated MCP server fits the architecture that already exists (detach, redirect, poll) with no server changes, which is the same reasoning that chose poll-a-file over a push channel. **"This reply", not "this session":** an allow-all returns `updatedPermissions … destination: "session"`, which was *verified* to die with the process — each turn is a fresh `claude -p --resume`, so turn 2 asks again. The button is labelled for what it does rather than for what the CLI calls it, and cross-turn persistence is left unbuilt on purpose: it needs a grant store of ours replayed as `--allowedTools`, i.e. a durable, invisible, un-revokable grant, which is a larger call than this bug. **The rule is the bare tool name.** The `--permission-prompt-tool` wire hands over `{tool_name, input, tool_use_id}` and no permission *suggestions* — the narrowed `Bash(npm test:*)` patterns the interactive prompt offers are computed on the CLI side and never reach us. Deriving our own would be a hand-rolled matcher deciding what counts as "the same command", in the one place in this repo where getting it wrong grants something nobody approved; handing the CLI a bare tool name keeps all matching on its side. **Which is exactly why the second button is not offered for every tool** (owner feedback on the first build, looking at "Allow all Bash in this reply" sitting under a single read-only `gh pr diff`): a bare-tool-name rule is the *only* rule we can write, and for Bash that one rule is "stop asking about commands", which is not a proportionate second option next to one command. `WHOLE_TOOL_GRANTABLE` limits it to the repeat-heavy file tools (`Edit`, `Write`, `Read`, `Glob`, `Grep`, `NotebookEdit`) where the grant buys one click instead of eight against the same target; Bash, the web tools and every unrecognised or MCP tool fall through to Allow/Deny, and the default for an unknown tool is no grant. The general lesson is that an affordance inherits the granularity of the mechanism behind it — the coarse rule was defensible, offering it as a *button on every card* was not. **The allowlist is enforced in `agent.py` too, and that reverses a call made in the first build** (owner, round two): the UI was left as the only place the restriction lived, on the reasoning that the browser *is* the user and a second copy would drift. The first half is true and the second half is answered by this repo's own D146 — a duplicated rule needs a **test that asserts the two agree**, not a comment — and it does not survive the real objection, which is that a view is the wrong place for the only copy of a security-relevant rule: any other caller of `decide` inherits the gap. A session scope asked for on an ungrantable tool now narrows to allow-once and reports the scope it actually got.
