The join branch's author had already seen the gap and closed it with a synthetic record, but **a fallback that protects one response body does not protect the separate request that follows it**: the client's next act is `GET /api/env/progress`, which calls `progress()` fresh and got null, so the fix was in the wrong place — it covered the POST's own payload and nothing else, not a reloaded page that polls without ever having POSTed. Which is why this is fixed in `progress()` and the synthetic record is **deleted** rather than merely persisted to disk: two record shapes for one state, one of them existing only in a response body, is the same divergence D177 is about, and the ONE place that can be right for every poller is the function every poller calls. `_claim_is_stale()` had already worked out the missing reasoning ("claim present, no record" = mid-spawn, not dead, bounded by `_CLAIM_GRACE_S`) and `progress()` had simply never been given it; the age rule is now `_claim_within_grace()`, read by both, so they agree by construction rather than by comment. Past the grace window a claim with no record resolves as **done-with-an-error** ("the installer never started"), not as a perpetual "starting": a server killed between claiming and writing must self-heal, and the poller needs an answer it can show and retry — the retry's `_claim` then takes the stale claim over, which is the pre-existing `_claim_is_stale` semantics unchanged. **The trap, and the reason the old `progress()` body survives as `_recorded_progress()`:** `_claim_is_stale` itself calls into progress, so if it saw the claim-derived record the claim would be its own alibi, nothing would ever be stealable, and one crash would wedge a key forever — the exact failure that function was written to prevent. So takeover reads the **record-only** view; the claim may be evidence to a poller, never to the decision about the claim. `cancel()` reads the record-only view for a different reason: a claim has no pid to signal, and a "cancelled" record written inside the spawn window would be overwritten moments later by the spawner's own `_write`, so the page would show cancelled and then watch the install continue — cancelling mid-spawn is a no-op reporting `False` and the poll keeps showing "starting". Why the race was routine rather than rare: `templates/docs/template.html` fires `warmup` unawaited and immediately awaits `import` for the same file, so one of the two always loses the claim and the user-visible one is the file open. The template was left alone — the concurrency is legitimate and the server must tolerate it — and a sweep found one more instance of the same shape, `templates/latex/template.html`'s `Promise.all([py(outline), py(bib)])` against a PEP 723 `engine.py`, which the server fix covers identically. The `map`/`netcdf`/`zarr_aoi` fire-and-forget warmups are safe only incidentally: their scripts carry no PEP 723 header, so they never reach the loader. Tests: a fresh claim reads as in flight, a stale one resolves, takeover still turns on claim age in both directions (the circularity guard), the join yields a pollable record, and `GET /api/env/progress` is non-null with a claim held — the last named to be distinguishable from `test_the_loader_polls_the_key_the_installer_actually_returned`, which reaches the same user-visible message via the wrong key. |
| D181 | **A guarantee, not an assertion: the install loader's orderings are now enforced by the OS, and a reaped pid must be one we spawned** | A review pass over the D173/D178 work found four things that were true only by luck, all in the same shape — something silently not happening. **(1) The pre-flight escaped its own error guard.** `run_python`'s `if requirements: envinstall.is_installed(...)` sat ABOVE the `try:` whose comment says it catches "every other failure". `is_installed` -> `venv_key_for` imports `fused.agent_core...venvs` unguarded and `_backend_attr` raises `RuntimeError` BY DESIGN when an upstream private attribute disappears — `routers/env.py` catches exactly that `(ImportError, RuntimeError)` pair for its own calls. Uncaught, /api/run became a 500 whose body is `{"error": "<string>"}`, and runtime.js reads `data.error.message` off that: the diagnostic `_backend_attr` wrote to be READ reached the user as the literal word `undefined`. Moved inside the guard. **(2) The spawn record could lose the worker's.** `_spawn` returns the moment `Popen` does, with the worker already running, so a resolver failing on its first import can write `done` before the parent writes `spawn`. The parent's `_write` was unconditional and the comment ASSERTED the parent wins — the same reasoning that produced the D180 bug. It is now an `O_CREAT|O_EXCL` create (`_write_if_absent`), so the worker's `os.replace` is the unconditional winner and the parent only fills an actual absence; because that write can no longer overwrite, winning the claim first unlinks the PREVIOUS attempt's record, or a retry would open showing the last failure's error while the new worker downloaded fine behind it. **(3) `_pid_alive` could reap another part of the server.** The pid comes out of `progress.json`, a not-`done` record survives a crash mid-install, and that pid can since have been recycled onto a child of the CURRENT server (an rclone rcd, a tile daemon, a pyramid build worker). `waitpid` reaped it and the owner's later `poll()`/`wait()` failed with ECHILD, which subprocess reports as **exit status 0** — a live or crashed child read as "finished successfully", which every owner branches on. Catching `ChildProcessError` cannot help: it only says the pid was not ours after the reap. The reap is now gated on `_SPAWNED`, the in-process set of worker pids we started and have not reaped. Deliberately in-process, not recorded next to the pid: a persisted owner pid would still need a boot identity to survive server-pid reuse, whereas "is this our child" stops being true the instant this process exits (the kernel reparents orphans to init, which reaps them), so the set is exact rather than an approximation. **(4) A cancel the server could not honour was silent.** `cancel()` reports `False` inside the spawn window — no pid is recorded yet — and the client ignored that: it had painted "cancelling…", the next `paint()` overwrote it with the installer's detail, the install ran on, `poll()` RESOLVED, and the script the user had just cancelled executed. Left server-side (a cancel marker the worker polls would add a cross-process protocol for a sub-`Popen` window) and fixed in runtime.js, in two halves because either alone still leaves a lie on screen: the dropped cancel is stated in a `notice` the painter defers to, with the button left live so a second press reaches the pid once the record carries one; and the resolve path honours `cancelled` — the venv stays built, but the run is abandoned, because a successful run is the one outcome pressing Cancel must never produce. Tests: the interleavings directly (a `_spawn` that writes the worker's record first, a retry after a failure, a child this module did not spawn left unreaped with its real exit status asserted), and the cancel through the loader's own JS under node. |
| D182 | **The bundled `uv` goes on the server's PATH, because a template may ask a fact about its environment but never branch on how the app was installed** | Five templates set their daemon's venv up with uv and resolve it as `shutil.which("uv") or ~/.local/bin/uv` — geotiff, zarr_aoi and netcdf's tile servers, plus las and pyramid. They have to resolve it that way: SPEC §26/MD-11 and D166 forbid a template from branching on its host, so "if this is a bundle, look in `Contents/Resources/bin`" cannot live in a template file. The macOS bundle ships uv at `Contents/Resources/bin/uv`, which is neither beside the interpreter nor on anyone's PATH — `envinstall._worker_env()` was the only thing that prepended it, and only for the install worker. So on a DMG with no user-installed uv, all five silently degraded: geotiff and zarr_aoi's `_daemon_python()` fell back to `sys.executable`, losing `imagecodecs`/`pyproj` (LZW and JPEG stop decoding) and `s3fs`/`gcsfs`/`crc32c` (every remote zarr store fails to open) — none of which is in the core deps or `[bundled]` — while las and pyramid raised advice a DMG user cannot act on. Dropping the PEP 723 headers (D174) removed the script venv that had incidentally supplied those deps, which is why it surfaced now. Fixed ONCE, in `export_app_env()`: the /api/run child and the daemon it spawns both inherit this process's environment, so prepending uv's directory makes every existing `shutil.which("uv")` start working with no template edit and no fifth copy of the resolution. Two reasons that is the right layer rather than a per-template helper — it is the SAME mechanism the Linux and Windows desktop supervisors already use (`supervisor/paths.py` prepends their payload's tools dir to the server's PATH), so this closes a platform gap instead of adding a second pattern; and the resolution is `envinstall.uv_bin()`'s, shared rather than restated, since it already knows all three packaged layouts. No uv anywhere stays a non-error: a dev checkout without uv is normal, and the templates already say so when they need it. Test asserts through `shutil.which("uv")` — what the templates actually call — with a fake `.app` layout as the only uv on PATH. |
| D183 | **A step that exists only to report a failure has to survive the failure — and nothing may re-resolve the payload after the payload is resolved** | Two bugs in `scripts/build_dmg.sh`, both invisible by construction: the build still failed, just without the answer. **(1)** `VAR="$(cmd 2>&1)"` under `set -euo pipefail` aborts on the ASSIGNMENT, so when the command fails — the only case the step exists for — the ERR trap fires and the following `grep -q` plus `echo "$VAR" >&2` never run. The traceback `2>&1` had just captured is discarded and the operator is told only "failed at line N". Four sites (google-auth import, the duckdb `_child.py` smoke, uv, rclone) now use `|| true` inside the substitution, the idiom `UV_SRC` already used; the grep IS the check and the exit status was never read. uv's and rclone's stderr still goes to the terminal on purpose — rclone's check is line-anchored, so folding stderr in could shift line 1. **(2)** `pip install pytest` went into `$BUILD_VENV`, which IS the shipped payload: py2app runs under that venv and copies modules out of its site-packages, and its purelib is `cp -R`'d into the .app. pytest's constraints could therefore repin `pluggy` (in setup_py2app.py's explicit force list) or `packaging` (in the derivation closure) with nothing but a pip warning. Now `--no-deps` plus a named `iniconfig`, and a loud import check so a future missing pytest dependency cannot read as a broken reconciliation step. The reconciliation itself is untouched — it is the only place `[bundled]` is genuinely installed, so it is the only place test_bundle_contents.py's per-distribution skips become assertions. Both are pinned by structural tests (`tests/test_build_dmg_diagnostics.py`), because no CI job on any other platform can run this script. Same review also made the `fused-engine` job's zero-skip gate premise TRUE rather than narrowing the gate: `tests/test_server_env_install.py` skips four tests without `node`, the job never installed one, and it passed only while `ubuntu-latest` happened to ship it — `setup-node@v4` now declares it, and `tests/test_ci_engine_gate.py` derives that requirement from the policed files so the next node-dependent test cannot re-break it. |
| D184 | **Apps live two levels under the workspace, `<fused_dir>/<tag>/<name>/`, and a tag is just a folder — there is no registry to update when a new one shows up** | The Home apps grid (`api_apps()`) previously treated every non-hidden top-level dir in `fused_dir()` as an app, flat. That made it impossible to tell a just-created app apart from a seeded example, from a git-cloned repo (D110's deep-link destination is the same `fused_dir()` root), from any other folder the owner happens to keep in the workspace — all three read as the same kind of thing. `api_apps()` now scans two levels: every non-hidden top-level dir is a "tag", every non-hidden dir directly inside it is an app, listed with a new `tag` field. Deliberately no whitelist/constant enumerating valid tags — the owner's call, made explicit when asked: "any subfolder is a tag", so introducing a new category later is one `os.makedirs` away, never a code change. `POST /api/apps/new` writes to `<fused_dir>/local/<name>/` (was `<fused_dir>/<name>/`); the packaged example seed (`shell/seed.py`) now copies into `<fused_dir>/examples/<name>/` instead of loose at the root. The seed's crash-safety moved with it: instead of one hidden `.<name>.partial` per example rsync'd and renamed individually, the whole `examples/` tree is staged under a single hidden `.examples.partial` at the workspace root and published with one `os.rename` — a crash mid-copy still leaves only a hidden entry at the root (never a half-written non-hidden `examples/` dir that would make the root look non-empty and wedge the empty-dir seeding gate off forever), at the cost of redoing the whole copy on retry rather than just the interrupted example, an acceptable trade given how small and fast the seed set is. Frontend: `AppInfo.tag` renders as a quiet mono pill on each `AppCard`; the "Apps" doorway and empty-state copy on Home.tsx were reworded to mention the tag-folder shape. Explicitly out of scope, the owner's call when asked directly: no migration of pre-existing flat workspaces. An install that already has flat top-level app folders from before this change (this repo's own dev workspace among them) keeps them exactly where they are — they simply stop appearing in the Home "recent" grid (they're not two levels deep) until moved by hand into a tag folder; they remain fully browsable via the file explorer regardless. Tests: `test_apps_api.py` and `test_shell_seed.py` updated throughout for the extra nesting level, plus new cases pinning the no-registry behavior directly (`test_any_top_level_folder_is_a_tag_no_registry`, `test_sorted_by_tag_then_name`) and the seed's new single-staging-dir crash recovery (`test_partial_seed_leftover_is_cleaned_and_reseeded` rewritten against `.examples.partial`). |
| D185 | **Scaffolded folders (apps AND templates) carry no `.claude/` of their own — the canonical skills are synced to Claude Code's user-level skills dir instead (`user_skills.py`), the only place skills are copied to; refreshed at server startup and at scaffold time** (superseded in part by D216: the sessions fused-render spawns now get the skills as a `--plugin-dir` plugin, and this sync is convenience for sessions it did not spawn) | The owner's ask: a freshly scaffolded app should be pure content, not boilerplate — the per-app `.claude/skills/` copy (a D106-style build-time duplicate) was the offender. Considered and rejected: a `CLAUDE.md` that tells Claude to fetch the skill from a GitHub URL as a fallback — it breaks offline (against the product's no-network-at-runtime stance), skews versions (the URL tracks `main`, the runtime serving the app is whatever's installed — and the skill describes bridge APIs that must match that runtime), and "follow instructions fetched from a remote URL" is a trust pattern not worth normalising. The accepted design leans on the fact that fused-render controls the environment its app sessions run in: `sync_user_skills()` copies **all three** canonical skills (`fused-render-authoring`, `fused-render-custom-templates`, `fused-render-usage`) into `<CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR or ~/.claude>/skills/` — the same env resolution the claude template backend uses (`templates/claude/agent.py`), so on packaged desktop builds (where the supervisor points `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` at the app's own state dir, supervisor/paths.py) the skills land exactly where the sessions we launch will look. Source resolution mirrors D106: repo `skills/<name>/` wins (editable installs, always-current), else a new packaged copy at `fused_render/skills/` (build-time copy in `scripts/hatch_build.py`, gitignored, shipped via `artifacts`). Ownership by marker file: only a dir carrying `.managed-by-fused-render` is ever overwritten, so a user-authored skill that happens to share a name is never clobbered; marked dirs are refreshed on every sync, which also fixes the staleness the per-app copies had (a copy scaffolded by an old version never updated). Sync points: a `create_app` **startup event** (deliberately not the `create_app` body — tests build the app without running lifespan, so they never write outside the conftest-redirected `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`) plus `POST /api/apps/new` and `POST /api/templates/new` (repairs a deletion right before the session/scaffold that needs the skill). Best-effort throughout — a failed copy logs a warning and must never break startup or creation. Both scaffolds' copytrees now ignore `.claude` (a dev checkout can hold a stale pre-D185 build copy in either starter kit), the build hook deletes those legacy dirs, `templates_api._ensure_starter_skills` is deleted (its D106 per-folder copy job no longer exists — D106's single-source rule lives on in the sync's source resolution), and both starter `CLAUDE.md`s reference the skills by name with "restart fused-render" as the repair path. The owner's explicit call: skills are copied to the user location and **nowhere else** — the trade accepted with it is that an exported/moved template or app folder no longer carries its own guidance onto machines fused-render doesn't manage. Tests: `test_user_skills.py` (marker ownership both directions, refresh, missing-source no-raise, packaged fallback, and that the `SKILLS` tuple matches the real repo dirs), plus `test_apps_api.py::test_new_app_has_no_dot_claude_and_syncs_user_skills` and the reworked `test_templates_api.py::test_new_template_scaffolds_and_binds`; `tests/conftest.py` now redirects `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` for the whole run so no test touches the real `~/.claude`. |

### macOS quit teardown (2026-07-30)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D184 | **Quit is a teardown with an ORDER, run off the AppKit main thread under a DERIVED deadline, entered through ONE function by every surface — including the ones AppKit owns — and the process that mounts something is the process that must unmount it** | Quitting from the tray icon hung for seconds, raised macOS "server connection interrupted / disks not ejected properly" alerts for every mounted remote, then died in a "quit unexpectedly" SIGABRT dialog. Three independent defects, all funnelling through one four-statement `_do_quit`, and each one is a different instance of the same mistake — treating "ask for it" as "it happened". **(A) The mounts were orphaned.** On darwin `attach_mount` uses `"mountType": "nfsmount"`, so the KERNEL holds a real NFS mount whose server IS the rcd process; quit killed rcd with those mounts still attached, the loopback NFS client timed out, and macOS complained. The comment banked on "rcd unmounts cleanly on SIGTERM" and was wrong twice: rclone's handler issues a PLAIN `umount`, which a busy macOS nfsmount rejects — the exact state `reconnect_mount` documents and the whole reason `_force_unmount` exists — and `_kill_current_rcd` escalates to SIGKILL after 5s, skipping rclone's unmount altogether. This was the ONLY mount teardown in the tree that did not go through the rc-unmount → force-unmount ladder. Now `lifecycle.unmount_all_for_quit` runs the existing ladder per mount, on its own daemon thread — the `mount_state` pattern — so a wedged `umount -f`, which blocks in the kernel and cannot be cancelled, costs only its share of a bounded budget instead of the quit. The rung is `detach_mount(m, force=True)`, exactly what `restart_rcd` already does before it kills a daemon (quit is the same "the daemon is about to go away" situation, so it gets the same call rather than a second copy of the ladder — a first cut open-coded rc-unmount + `_force_unmount` and thereby skipped detach_mount's EBUSY handling, which quiesces the tile daemons that hold files open under the mount and retries). `force=True` is load-bearing: the plain call refuses to force ("failing loudly beats corrupted reads"), right for a user action on a live mount and wrong here. One rung is added on top, gated on the KERNEL's view (`_is_mounted`) rather than on rcd's answer, because detach_mount can report done with a mount still attached — its own force gate is `_ismount`, which is False for a wedged mount, and its success path trusts an rcd OK that the INCIDENT 2026-07-16 split-brain shows can sit over a surviving kernel entry. The persist/ownership gate that guarded only the reap is factored into `_rcd_is_ours_to_reap` and now gates BOTH rungs, because unmounting a daemon's mounts is as much a teardown of it as signalling it: a persisted dev daemon (`FUSED_RENDER_RCLONE_PERSIST`) or a still-live foreign spawner (a CLI server sharing the per-home rcd) keeps its mounts. **And unmounting is not enough while something else is still MOUNTING** (review's catch): `health.startup()` runs `run_automount` on a daemon thread calling `attach_mount` per mount, seconds each against S3, so quitting during startup — the readiness-failure abort above all, whose own premise is that automount has had time to run — could land an attach after that mount's unmount thread had finished, and the reap then killed rcd under a live nfsmount: defect (A) restored by concurrency rather than by omission. Interlocked in two layers, since neither covers the other's window: `_QUIT_TEARDOWN_LATCH` (a `threading.Event`, never rebound — a bool set through `global` would freeze at False for every importer, the D178 `_STAT_CACHE_GEN` bug) is armed before the mount snapshot and `attach_mount` DECLINES while it is set (declining, not unwinding: the mount would either outlive the reap or need tearing down twice, and every caller already treats an error string as "not mounted, logged, move on"); and for the attach already inside `mount/mount` when we armed it, **the attacher itself is responsible for leaving nothing attached**. That second layer started as a one-shot sweep of the kernel mount table after the fan-out, and review was right that a sweep cannot be the answer: `mount/mount` carries a 60s timeout, so no choice of "when to look again" is correct when the writer may take a minute — it can land after the sweep, or after `stop_local_rcd`. So `attach_mount` re-reads the latch when `mount/mount` returns and immediately detaches what it just created. That works even after rcd is reaped (the force rung is a kernel `umount -f`, which needs no daemon), and it is the only party that both knows a mount was created and is positioned to act at the right moment — the general principle being to make the last writer responsible rather than have the teardown guess. The sweep stays as a narrowing, together with a bounded wait on an in-flight-attach count (a dict mutated under a Condition, spanning the whole region that can leave a mount behind, self-detach included); both spend only the leftover join budget, so neither can extend what `app.py` was promised. The latch is armed only AFTER the ownership gate passes: a daemon we are deliberately leaving running keeps its mounts, so an attach racing that quit is harmless. **(B) The beachball was structural, not slow code.** The whole reap ran inside a menu-item action with the AppKit run loop blocked: an rc `core/pid` probe (3s) plus SIGTERM and SIGKILL polls (5s each) — ~13s of guaranteed hang. `start_quit` moves it to a worker and returns at once; a watchdog thread calls terminate when teardown finishes OR after `QUIT_HARD_DEADLINE_S`, whichever comes first. **That deadline is DERIVED, and review caught it being a lie:** it was a hand-picked 15.0 while the steps it waits on summed to 21 (drain 2 + unmount 6 + the reap's 3s rc probe + 3s `ps` + two 5s exit polls), so on a slow-but-healthy teardown it fired DURING the SIGTERM wait, skipping the SIGKILL escalation — and on macOS production rcd is a normal child that survives the parent's `exit()` by reparenting to launchd, so an rclone ignoring SIGTERM was left alive under mounts whose teardown had not finished: defect (A) reintroduced by arithmetic. `rcd.py` now names its inline timeouts and exports `RCD_REAP_WORST_CASE_S`, and `app.py` computes the deadline as drain + unmount budget + that + a stated margin, so tightening any inner budget moves it and nothing restates the sum. **A second review pass then found the derivation itself still incomplete, twice — the lesson being that "derived" is only as honest as the term list.** (i) Every iteration of both kill polls evaluates `_live_rcd_port()`, which makes its OWN `core/pid` probe with a 3s timeout, and the loops test the clock BEFORE an iteration, so one entered just under the deadline blocks a full probe past it: each phase is bounded by 5+3, not 5. Counted per phase now (the true ceiling — the probe's dead-answer cache outlives a phase, so a phase cannot pay for two), and the poll's conjunction is reordered to `not _pid_alive(pid) and _live_rcd_port() is None` so the free syscall short-circuits the expensive probe: a live process now costs no probes at all, where a wedged daemon used to be re-probed for the whole wait. (ii) The tile-daemon quiesce ran before the join deadline was even computed, i.e. outside the number `app.py` is handed — 3s per wedged daemon, sequential. It now has its own bound (`_QUIT_QUIESCE_BUDGET_S`, joined as a whole so adding a daemon to `DAEMON_STATE_FILES` cannot stretch the deadline) and `_QUIT_UNMOUNT_BUDGET_S` is the sum of quiesce + joins; the halves stay separate pools because a slow quiesce must not eat the time the unmounts need. 34s worst case is deliberate: it is a backstop for a genuinely stuck step, not a target — the teardown is off-thread so the UI stays responsive, and the normal path finishes in well under a second. Tests assert the inequality strictly, that the reap bound equals its full term list, and that the poll makes no rc probe while the pid is alive. Both halves of the watchdog are load-bearing: teardown must get a real, bounded chance to complete (a fire-and-forget quit would reintroduce (A) whenever the process won the race), and a wedged step must never leave an app that refuses to quit. Exactly one terminate call, because only the watchdog ever makes it; it hops back through `AppHelper.callAfter` since `NSApplication.terminate:` is main-thread-only. **(C) The SIGABRT was the GIL, not DuckDB.** From the crash report's main-thread stack (`FusedRender-2026-07-29-135823.ips`): `rumps.quit_application()` → `NSApplication.terminate:` → C `exit()` — NOT Python finalization — and pyobjc releases the GIL for the duration of that ObjC call, so the C++ static destructors `exit()` runs execute with NO GIL held. One destroys the `DuckDBPyConnection` that `templates/duckdb/reader.py` stashes on the *duckdb module* to keep DuckDB's HTTP range cache warm across reader runs; its destructor touches the Python C-API (`PyEval_SaveThread`) → `Py_FatalError` → `abort()`. It lives in the app process because the duckdb reader is an in-process helper (`executor.INPROCESS_HELPERS`) and on macOS the server runs in-process inside the rumps app — so previewing one mounted parquet file made every later quit crash. Linux/Windows exit through normal finalization, with the GIL held, and were never affected. Fixed with `reader.close_http_connection()`, placed next to the stash it owns (plus a shared `_HTTP_CON_KEY` so writer and closer cannot drift), called from the quit path while Python is healthy; it drops the attribute BEFORE closing, since a closed-but-reachable connection is still an object for `exit()` to trip over. **A close is an event; the invariant needed is "no stash from here on"** — review's catch: the drain ahead of the close is a bounded join that can time out, so uvicorn may still be serving while unmount and reap run for seconds, and one parquet read in that window called `_http_connection` and restashed a fresh connection. So the close sets a one-way latch (on the duckdb module, since the reader module object is transient) and `_http_connection` RAISES past it instead of building another — **and a latch is only a barrier if it is read at the moment of the write** (the next review's catch): the check sat at the top of `_http_connection` while the `setattr` that installs the stash is ~30 lines and one `duckdb.connect` + `LOAD httpfs` + four PRAGMAs later, so a read that passed the check could install its connection after the close had latched and cleared, and nothing would ever close THAT one. The install decision now happens under a lock that `close_http_connection` also holds while it latches and takes the stash, which turns the property into one that can be stated: once `close_http_connection` returns, nothing can put a connection back on the duckdb module. Building stays outside the lock (reads must not serialize behind each other's setup) and a builder that loses — to the latch, or to another builder — closes what it built, because handing an unclosed `DuckDBPyConnection` to the collector is the same crash on a longer fuse. The lock itself lives on the duckdb module too, installed via `dict.setdefault` on its `__dict__` — the one way to create a shared lock when you do not yet have one to guard the creation, and necessary because a module-level `Lock()` in a file the executor re-execs per request is a different object every call, i.e. no lock at all — the right shape because the remote fast path's call site already treats any failure there as "fall back to the plain file path", which is what a process on its way out should do. The order is deliberately unchanged rather than moving the close to just-before-terminate: the steps in between are the ones that can hang, and a deadline firing there would terminate having never closed anything — precisely the crash. A sweep of the other in-process helpers found no second module-stashed native handle — `templates/excel/reader.py`, `templates/h3/h3_reader.py`, `duckdb/writer.py` and both sqlite helpers all use function-local connections — so the parquet reader's stash is the only one. **(D) A fixed quit path is worth nothing on the surfaces that don't use it.** The most important finding of the review, and the one nothing in (A)–(C) would ever have surfaced: `setup_py2app.py` sets no `LSUIElement` on purpose (D34 — regular app, Dock icon AND menu bar item), and there was no `applicationShouldTerminate_` anywhere, so **Dock icon → Quit, ⌘Q and logout/restart bypassed the entire teardown** — straight to `-[NSApplication terminate:]` → `exit()`, with `state["quitting"]` never consulted. Every defect above was still fully live on the surface a user reaching for the Dock icon actually hits. Fixed with the canonical Cocoa pattern (return `NSTerminateLater`, tear down off-thread, then `replyToApplicationShouldTerminate:`) — the ONLY answer that keeps the teardown off the main thread, since `applicationWillTerminate:`, the other hook rumps does define, runs synchronously on the way to `exit()` and would just reinstate the beachball. The hook is added to rumps' delegate class the same way the openFiles/openURLs/reopen handlers already are, which is what makes it upgrade-safe (it adds a method rumps has no opinion about rather than wrapping one): verified against the installed rumps 0.4.0, which defines no `applicationShouldTerminate_`, and pyobjc resolves the dynamically added selector to AppKit's real signature — `I@:@`, an unsigned-int return, so `NSTerminateLater` arrives as 2 and not as a bridged object that AppKit would read as "terminate now". A failed install is logged, never raised (PV-8: an app that won't launch is worse than one whose Dock quit skips the teardown), and the reply wait carries its own backstop because an app AppKit is awaiting a reply from cannot be quit at all. **The ordering is now a tested property, not statement order in a closure, and there is exactly ONE teardown rather than one per surface.** `_do_quit`'s body became module-level `quit_teardown` (drain the server → close the duckdb stash → unmount → reap rcd, each step independently guarded so one failure cannot skip the rest), `start_quit`, and `begin_quit` — the single entry every surface funnels through: it starts the teardown unless one is running, and sets a shared `quit_ready` event BEFORE the surface's own end action, which matters because that action is what re-enters `terminate:` (a delegate hook answering `NSTerminateLater` to our own end-of-teardown terminate would hang the quit forever; it answers `NSTerminateNow`). `make_quit_action` is what the tray item and the popover's `quitApp_` get, via the actions dict. Idempotence spans surfaces, not just clicks: a Dock Quit during a tray-initiated teardown joins it instead of racing a second unmount + reap — and that claim needed a LOCK, not a premise: `begin_quit`'s check-then-set was documented as safe because "both entry points are AppKit callbacks on the main thread", which routing the readiness-failure abort through the same action falsified in the same round (the bootstrap thread now calls it). Held open, the unlocked window really did produce two fan-outs, two reaps, and — worse — two separate lazily-created `quit_ready` events, so the delegate hook would wait on a signal the other surface never set: an app AppKit never gets a reply from. Review also found the last direct bypass — `_bootstrap_server`'s readiness-failure abort called `quit_application()` straight, and by then the server may have been up 15s, long enough for `run_automount` to have attached mounts — so it goes through the quit action too, and a structural test asserts `quit_application` is reachable from nowhere but the terminate hop, because a new bypass is invisible in behavior. One efficiency fix from the same pass: the tile daemons are quiesced ONCE before the per-mount unmounts fan out, rather than per busy mount by each `detach_mount` (each of which quits all daemons) — and hoisting it beats reacting to EBUSY anyway, since a released file cannot cause a busy unmount in the first place. The server drain is a bounded join on the server's thread, now returned by `_start_server_thread`: `should_exit` alone is fire-and-forget, uvicorn never resets `started`, so the thread ending is the only observable "it stopped serving" — and it comes first because an in-flight `/api/fs/raw` read holds files open under a mount, a measured cause of busy-mount unmount failure. Tests (`tests/test_app_quit.py`, the first tests app.py has ever had) fake at the rc/subprocess boundary like `test_shell_mounts.py`, so they need no macOS, no rclone and no real mount: the ordering trace (duckdb before any unmount, every unmount before any `os.kill`), a prompt return against a time budget while teardown sleeps past it, terminate firing on a teardown that never finishes, a wedged AND a hanging mount not stopping the others or the reap, both gates leaving everything attached, the stash close in its no-duckdb / no-stash / raising-close variants and the latch refusing to restash, the delegate hook's three cases (immediate `NSTerminateNow`, joining a tray teardown, starting one) with an injected reply callback so no AppKit is needed, the deadline inequality, and the two structural assertions above. Three test-quality lessons from the review, all now written into the tests themselves: a one-way latch tripped on purpose LEAKS unless the fixture clears it unconditionally (`monkeypatch.delattr(..., raising=False)` records an undo only when the attribute already exists, so `tests/test_duckdb_reader.py` would have kept passing while silently asserting on the local-path fallback — hence an autouse guard there that fails loudly on a latch from any source); a process-global interlock needs the same treatment or every attach test in `test_shell_mounts.py` starts refusing; and a race test that starts N threads and hopes the GIL switches in the right microsecond PASSED against the unlocked code — only holding every caller inside the window with a barrier fails deterministically. |
| D185 | **Annotate picks the line-number anchor strategy on a lineNumbers gutter that is actually LAID OUT, not on "is this CodeMirror" and not on the gutter merely existing** | Annotation was dead on the markdown notes view — clicks did nothing, drag-selections did nothing, stored comments never painted, and nothing was logged. `isCmDoc` was `!!doc.querySelector(".cm-editor")`, and the notes view IS a CM editor (MD-18a), so every gesture took the code path whose only source of a line number is `.cm-lineNumbers .cm-gutterElement`: `cmVisibleLines()` returned `[]`, and both handlers bail on an unresolvable anchor AFTER `preventDefault()`/`stopPropagation()` — swallowing the gesture rather than failing it. The first fix was wrong in a way only the running app showed: querying for `.cm-lineNumbers` still matched, because the notes view ships `CM.basicSetup` (so the gutter element exists) and merely hides `.cm-gutters` in CSS — the "no line numbers" of MD-18a is a display rule, not a missing extension. The condition that matters is the one `cmVisibleLines` itself applies, `height > 0`, so the probe now measures the gutter's box. `.md` binds `annotate` as a trailing mode and the view picker defaults to the first non-annotate mode, so a `.md` file landed on the broken view by default, which is why it read as "annotation doesn't work on markdown". The probe now requires the gutter that strategy reads from, and a gutterless editor falls through to the ordinary element-path/quote anchors — the right model for prose anyway. Quote containers are hoisted to `.cm-content`, because the `.cm-line` div a selection sits in is remounted on scroll and its `nth-of-type` path drifts onto another line; the quote plus its occurrence index locates the span inside the stable content element, and off-screen text has no pin until it scrolls back in (off-screen, not detached — the same property code anchors have). Rejected **teaching the markdown view to keep a hidden lineNumbers gutter** for annotate's benefit: it puts annotation-aware code inside a view template, which D78 removed on purpose, and `cmVisibleLines` filters on `height > 0` so `display:none` would not even work. Rejected **deriving the line from `posAtCoords`** for now: it fixes this and any future gutterless editor, but needs the framed view's `EditorView` instance, which annotate deliberately cannot reach. Kept as the follow-up if path-anchor drift in long notes turns out to bite. |
| D186 | **A note OPENS read-only; editing is one click away and lives in the URL** | Reverses MD-1a's original "editing is the default, as in Obsidian" (owner call). Obsidian's default is right for a notes app you open to write in; fused-render is a FILE EXPLORER, where opening a file is a read — and on a surface that is always a live editor, a stray keystroke on a note you opened to look at silently rewrites it (MD-16 autosaves after 2s idle, with no save button to not press). So an absent `edit` param is read-only and only an explicit `"1"` grants editing; the preference still rides in `fused.params`, so an editing session survives a refresh and travels in a shared URL. `aria-pressed` on the corner toggle flips to track EDITING, since the accent must mark the state worth noticing and that is now "this surface is writable"; the glyph still names the current mode (padlock/pencil), and an unwritable file (MD-15) is unchanged — always locked, toggle disabled. Side benefit, not the reason: the notes view makes a better annotate stage (§17), taking no edits and revealing no markers under a reviewer's clicks. |
| D187 | **The graph's band names, and the gutter they reserve, appear only where there is more than one band** | Reverses the explicit earlier choice to draw the name even for a single band ("these are all in `x/`" is the answer the layout exists to give). With one band the vertical axis distinguishes nothing, so the legend is one folder name repeated down the left edge of a panel it labels in whole — which the shell's breadcrumb already says — and it costs up to 92px of a 320px sidebar. Names and fills now share one condition (`> 1`), where the fill already had it, and `gutterFor` returning 0 hands that width back to the lane layout: two long-labelled notes in one folder fit one lane of the full width where they used to wrap onto two for a legend that is no longer drawn. |
| D188 | **Path completion is free: the `candidates` payload already carried the file list, so `](…)` completes with no new Python action — and the target is percent-encoded on insert** | MD-14 completed `[[`/`![[`/`[[#`/`[[note#` only, which left the most-typed target of all — the `](path)` of an ordinary markdown link or image — with nothing, even though `_candidates_payload` had been returning `notes[]` **and** `assets` (every non-note file under the root, root-relative, capped at MAX_ASSETS) since the embed index needed them. `assets` was simply unused by the page. So the whole feature is client-side string work over a payload that already exists: no new action, no second walk, no extra run per popup, and it can never offer a file the graph does not know about — the same free-once-the-index-exists property `[[` has. Candidates are written **relative to the open note's own folder** because that is `resolve_link`'s FIRST tier (MD-4), so the inserted form resolves back to that exact file with no basename ambiguity to lose to, and it doubles as a real relative path the shell can navigate (MD-4a) where no scan ran at all; `../` is emitted only where the target is genuinely above the note, since climbing to the root and back down is noise nobody would type. **Encoding on insert is load-bearing, not cosmetic:** `[x](my file.png)` is not a link to the GFM parser — the space ends the target — so an unencoded suggestion would insert markup that Live Preview cannot render and the delegated click handler never sees, i.e. the popup's own output would be the bug. The escapes therefore go in the document (per segment, so `/` and `..` survive, and parentheses too since one closes the target early, which `encodeURIComponent` leaves alone) while `displayLabel` shows the human-readable path; MD-4a already decodes on the way back. Rejected wrapping the target in `<>` instead: it is one form more for the parser and the widget to agree about, where percent-escapes were already a supported path. Assets in `![[…]]` are offered root-relative and **unshortened**, because `_link_form`'s guarantee (every offered form run through `resolve_link` itself) is a Python one with no asset equivalent in the payload, and reimplementing the shortening in JS would be the parallel-rule divergence MD-3/MD-14 exist to prevent. Finally, a failed scan is now a **row that says why** rather than a `null` return: on a mount-backed root the action refuses by design (MD-11) and every source bailed silently, which the popup drew as "no matches" — the same unknown-vs-missing lie MD-11a forbids everywhere else. The reason is cached with the failure, so that root costs one run per 5 s TTL instead of one per keystroke. |
| D189 | **A markdown toggle is only "free" where the GRAMMAR already has a rule for it — so `~~`/backtick were added and `==` was refused — and ⌘K may only trust the tree about a `Link` after re-reading the source, because the grammar calls a wikilink's brackets a `Link` too** | Four editing gaps closed at once (MD-18), and two of them are the same lesson about what the vendored grammar does and does not know. **(1) The marker set stops where the grammar stops.** `toggleWrap` was always marker-agnostic, so strikethrough (`~~`) and inline code (backtick) cost one keymap entry each and nothing else: `Strikethrough` and `InlineCode` are real nodes, so Live Preview already renders both (MD-18a) and both already hide their markers off the caret line. `==` highlight looks like the same one-line change and is not: no rule, no node, no decoration — the markers would sit bare on the rendered page, which is worse than not having the toggle, and the alternative is a regex pass in the decoration builder plus a place for it to disagree with the grammar (MD-3). Refused deliberately rather than left as an oversight. `⌘⇧X`/`⌘⇧E` were picked because Obsidian assigns no default hotkey to either command, so there was no default to match — only collisions to avoid (`markdownKeymap`, `basicSetup`'s default/search/close-brackets keymaps, and the `Prec.highest` completion keymap `autocompletion()` installs). `⌘E` was available and was NOT taken: the read-only↔editing toggle has no keyboard binding by owner call, and leaving ⌘E unbound keeps that decision visible instead of quietly spending the key on inline code. **(2) The `Link` node is not evidence that a link is there.** ⌘K on a caret inside `[a](b)` used to nest a second link, and the fix has to ask the tree which node encloses the caret — but the trap that bit the link layer once already (MD-3, MD-18a) is that the vendored grammar wraps a `[[wikilink]]`'s inner brackets in `Link`/`Image` nodes, so "an enclosing `Link`" is also true inside every wikilink and every embed. Treating those as inline links would have made ⌘K select a target that does not exist and mangle the wikilink. So `enclosingLinkTarget` applies the SAME second test the decoration builder applies before it draws a link widget — the range must also match the plain-inline-link regex — rather than inventing a second rule about what a link is; a wikilink, an embed, a reference link and a titled link all fail it and fall through to today's insert. Verified by executing the real grammar (the discipline MD-18a's probe exists for), not by reading the tree's documentation. **(3) `⌘B` with no selection now wraps the WORD under the caret**, as Obsidian does, which also makes the unwrap reachable from a bare caret; the word comes from `state.wordAt`, so there is no second definition of a word in this template, and its `null` (whitespace, empty line) is what keeps the old empty-marker-with-the-caret-inside behaviour where that is still right. **(4) `Tab` accepts an open completion** before indenting — CM's `completionKeymap` binds only `⏎` to `acceptCompletion`, so every MD-14 popup was unreachable by the key a Tab-completing habit uses — which needed `acceptCompletion` added to `entry.js` and the bundle rebuilt (MD-13: what is not re-exported is tree-shaken). **One thing every new binding forced:** the read-only facets do not filter a hand-built dispatch, and each of these commands builds its own. `editable.of(false)` keeps the keys away from the view, but that is a property of the VIEW, so a `whenWritable` wrapper checks `state.readOnly` and swallows the key — the invariant MD-1a/MD-15 state belongs in the commands, not in an argument about focus. |
| D190 | **The headings outline is the TOP SECTION of the one right sidebar, and it reads the LIVE CodeMirror document rather than the saved payload** | MD-19b. The note view had the open note's headings in three places (the `[[#` popup, the `](#` popup, `?heading=` scrolling) and no place that simply *showed* them, which is the one navigation aid a long note needs and the sidebar did not have. **Where:** a section above backlinks inside the existing `#side` panel, not a panel of its own — MD-19a allows exactly one right sidebar behind exactly one 26px toggle and MD-2a forbids the toolbar row a second control would want, so a second toggle was never on the table. Above rather than below because the ordering is by subject, not by size: the outline is about the note in front of you, backlinks and the graph are about the rest of the vault, so the thing describing the open document sits nearest it — which is also where Obsidian puts it. The only structural cost is that the first section is now the one that has to clear the floating corner cluster, so the 72px padding moved from `#links-head` to `#outline-head`. **What it reads:** `view.state.doc`, on the `docChanged` the editor already reports. The payload's `notes.headings` was the obvious source and is the wrong one: it re-parses only on save (MD-9), so an outline fed by it would lag every heading typed by up to one autosave interval — a stale outline is worse than no outline, because it is a navigation aid that would send you to the wrong place. Reading the document is also not a new idea here: `?heading=` scrolling already treats the document as the heading index (MD-4b). **No timer** — the existing `docChanged` listener is the only trigger, keeping the template poll-free (MD-17's `fs/events` stat-storm is the standing reason). Two consequences, both deliberate: the heading scan mirrors graph.py's `_mask_code` (frontmatter and fenced code masked, ATX only, trailing `#`s stripped) so a `# not a heading` inside a fenced block is absent here exactly as it is absent from every other heading surface; and `syntaxTree(state)` was **rejected** as the source despite being what the decoration builder uses, because it is parsed only as far as CM has got — on a long note the tail headings would silently be missing, and `ensureSyntaxTree` over the whole document on every keystroke is the cost this avoids. Rows scroll by **line number**, not by heading text: the row was built from that line, and re-finding it by text (as `scrollToHeading` must, since a URL param carries only text) hands the second `## Notes` to the first one — so MD-4b's caret rule moved into one `scrollToLine` both paths call. Indent is by **nesting depth computed from the levels present**, not by hash count, so a note whose headings all start at `##` is not a uniformly indented note and `#` → `###` is one step in. **No state, so no param:** the outline has no open/closed and no collapse of its own (MD-20 carries panel and depth because those are states a shared URL must reproduce; inventing an `outline=` param for a section that is always drawn when the panel is would be state for its own sake). A redraw is skipped when the heading list is unchanged — load-bearing rather than an optimisation, since rewriting the list's innerHTML on every keystroke resets its scroll position and drops the row under the pointer. It is fully functional read-only (MD-1a): rows are buttons outside the editor going through the one delegated click handler, and scrolling is not an edit. |
| D191 | **An asset ships a resolver-VALIDATED wikilink form like a note does — validated against the asset index, from the note the popup is running in — and `null` where no form resolves back, rather than a bare path that resolves elsewhere** | Closes the gap D188 documented and accepted: `![[…]]` completion inserted the asset's bare root-relative path, because `_link_form`'s run-every-candidate-through-`resolve_link` guarantee existed for notes only. Two concrete failures, both real rather than theoretical. **(a) Tier 1 runs first.** Resolution tries the linking note's own folder before the root (MD-4), so `![[img/a.png]]` written in `docs/` binds to `docs/img/a.png` the moment that file exists — which means a form validated *from the root* is not validated at all for a note in a subfolder. `_candidates_payload` therefore takes the open note (`file` is now sent with the `candidates` action) and validates every form from it. That also fixes the same latent hole for notes, and makes the sibling reachable as a bare basename where the root-validated form had to carry a folder. Where the note's own folder shadows every suffix of the target, a **`../` escape** is the last form tried — tier 1 lands back on the root path and resolves — which is a form MD-14's `](` completion already writes, so nothing new has to understand it. **(b) A collision has no form at all.** `_candidate_index` files a note under its stem, so an asset `img/a.png` and a note `img/a.png.md` share the key `img/a.png` and `_only` refuses to pick (MD-4); no form of either resolves back, and the ladder used to *return the full path anyway* — a target the resolver reads as a ghost. So `_link_form` returns **None**, the payload ships `null`, and the popup **drops the row** instead of inserting a lie. **Two fields, not one:** `link` validated against the note index and `embed` against the notes-plus-assets index, because `_resolved_links` sends a non-note embed target through the second and everything else through the first — one field could only be right for one of the two popups. Asset rows carry `embed` only (an asset outside an embed is not a wikilink target), which turns `assets` from a list of strings into a list of `{rel, embed}`; `](` path completion reads `.rel` and still computes its own relative form, as it must (MD-14). **Cost, measured rather than guessed:** this is per-candidate resolver work over up to 5000 notes **and** 5000 assets, and resolution's third tier was an O(index) `endswith` scan — on a synthetic deep tree at both caps that cost ~1.9s for the note and asset passes alone (and the notes half of it, ~0.8s, was already being paid unmeasured). So `_suffix_index` memoises that tier the way `_candidate_index` memoises the first two: the whole payload now computes in ~40ms, a ~50x improvement over what the naive version of this change would have shipped. It is a memoisation and must stay one — a key's suffixes at every `/` boundary are exactly the keys the scan would match — and an equivalence test pins both paths to the same answers, ambiguous ones included, rather than trusting the argument. Rejected **eager per-asset work without the memo** (1.9s in front of a popup is not free-once-the-index-exists), and rejected **computing the shortening in JS** for the same reason D188 did: it is the parallel-rule divergence MD-3 exists to prevent. |
| D192 | **The completion popup is themed through the EDITOR's own theme extension, not page CSS — and it has no icon column at all** | MD-14's popup shipped as stock CM6 with one page rule over it, and that rule was the whole problem: `li[aria-selected]` filled the selected row with `var(--accent)`, a full-bleed acid-yellow bar with near-black text, the loudest thing in a view whose accent is otherwise spent on links only. Two choices worth recording. **(1) A `theme` extension rather than page rules.** The appearance flip does not rebuild the editor — it dispatches `StateEffect.reconfigure` over the extension array (MD-14/MD-15's reason: a rebuild throws away unsaved edits, the caret and the scroll position) — so a theme extension in that array is reinstalled with everything else, whereas a page rule has to keep out-specifying whatever oneDark reinstates, at the one place in this template where CM's stylesheet wins on source order. It also means one theme covers both palettes: every value is a `var()` token resolved off `<html>` at paint time, so there is no dark copy of these rules to drift out of sync with the light one. The generic `.cm-editor .cm-tooltip` shell stays a page rule (the search panel is a tooltip too), which is exactly why the popup's own selectors carry `.cm-tooltip.cm-tooltip-autocomplete` — equal specificity would lose to it on order. **(2) `icons: false`.** CM renders the `type` field as a glyph and its glyph for `text` is the literal string `abc`, which appeared on every path row naming nothing. The alternative was four distinguishable marks (note / heading / image / other file) — rejected on two counts: the sources set two `type` values across four contexts, so meaningful marks would mean changing what the sources emit for a purely visual gain, and the row already says its kind without a glyph (an extension, `.md` or not; a heading by its indent and `H2` marker; an asset by its `embed` detail). `icons: false` removes the element rather than hiding it, so no empty column holds width in a popup that has a vault's worth of paths to fit. What replaces the accent bar is the **sidebar's** language, because that panel is a foot away from this popup: rows at `.bl`'s density and radius in the body font, a selected row at `--bg` on the `--bg-alt` surface with an accent left edge and 600 weight — two non-colour signals, so the selection is not colour alone — a dimmed right-hand `detail` column, and `.cm-completionMatchedText` in the accent, which is the one thing on a vault-length list that says why a row is still in it. **That last one needed code, not CSS**, and driving the live page is what found it: CM matches against `label` and then refuses to guess where those characters landed in a `displayLabel`, handing such an option an *empty* match — so every path row and every heading row in this template (all of which display a form of their own, by MD-14's encoded-insert rule) rendered with no emphasis whatsoever. The mapping is supplied through the result's own `getMatch` hook: the matched characters, found again as a subsequence of what the row shows, with a character the display deliberately drops (a heading's `#`) skipped rather than fudged — so the emphasis is on real matched text or absent, never a range slid sideways over the wrong letters. |

### preview template merged into the built-in listing (2026-07-30)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D185 | **The `preview` directory template is DELETED and its one unique feature — the split preview pane — becomes part of the built-in `_listing` view. A hand-ported copy of a shell view is a fork, and a fork drifts** | `templates/preview/template.html` was 990 lines of vanilla JS re-implementing `frontend/src/views/Listing.tsx`: the same folder listing, the same columns, the same in-folder search, the same per-filetype icons and tints — plus one thing the shell did not have, a **split pane** showing the selected entry's preview beside the list (Finder list view). Everything else about it was already worse, and by inspection rather than by prediction: no live directory watch (LS-1), no file operations (rename/delete/new — §24), no multi-select, no streaming recursive search (SR-7..SR-11 — its search filtered the one loaded page), dark-only by `color-scheme: dark` and a local token block (which is exactly why it sat on AP-12's deferred list), and its own local re-implementation of "is this row a launchable app" against D124's exported `isAppEntry`. That gap set only widens: every listing feature since the port landed in one file and not the other, and nothing made the divergence visible — the two views share no code, no test, and no type. So the direction is the one that ends the duplication rather than paying to close it: fold the **pane** into `Listing.tsx` (FS-9..FS-15), where it inherits watch, file ops, multi-select, streaming search, theming and app rows for free and cannot drift from them again, then delete the template folder and drop `preview` from the built-in `"/"` binding — now `["_listing", "graph", "zarr_aoi"]`. **Rejected: keep `preview` as a peer directory mode** — it is the status quo, and it is a second listing that a user reaches by a switcher click and then finds is missing the features the first one has; two answers to "show me this folder" is a bug report, not a choice. **Rejected: port the shell's listing features INTO the template** — that is re-writing watch, file ops, selection, the streamed search and the theme system in vanilla JS against `fused.runPython`, to arrive at a duplicate of a React view we maintain anyway; the pane is ~200 lines in `Listing.tsx` and the rest of the template is the part we are trying to stop having. Pane default is **OFF**, so a plain folder listing is byte-for-byte what it was; visibility and width persist per-folder through `lib/viewstate.ts` (localStorage) and deliberately **not** the URL — a bookmark captures a view (SB-2), and pane geometry is a workspace habit, not view state worth pinning into a shared link. *(Revised same day, owner: visibility now ALSO rides the URL as a sticky `?preview=true` — authoritative over viewstate on mount, carried across directory navigation, withheld from file targets so it cannot shadow a template's own param via D72's ancestor-climb — because "is the pane open" turned out to be exactly the thing a shared link should reproduce. **Width** stays viewstate-only for the reason stated above, which was always the stronger half of the argument: a pixel drag on one window is not a fact about the view. See FS-13.)* *(Review follow-up, same day: automated review flagged the 50 % default as no longer proportional once measured. Owner: **one-time default is intended** — the pane must not resize itself under the user. The half of the finding that WAS a bug is fixed: the measured width could be persisted to `panew` by a later toggle or a bare divider click, indistinguishable from a drag, which would freeze one window's arithmetic into the folder's saved state. A `paneSized` provenance ref now gates both writes. The same review's second finding was also real and fixed: the pane's file case picked the first unconditional template and showed the metadata card for an **all-conditional** list, while full-screen `Preview` falls back to `templates[0]` once verdicts land — so the pane claimed "no preview" for a file that opens fine full-screen. It now resolves the gates and shows the first allowed entry, failing closed to the card only on an empty list or a broken gate. See FS-11, FS-12.)* *(Second review pass, same day, two more real bugs fixed. **The 220 px minimum was on the wrong element:** FS-12 specifies it as the LIST's floor, but `PANE_MIN_W` and `.listing-pane-slot` applied it to the pane, and the 65 % fraction does not imply it — on a 600 px container 65 % leaves the list 210 px, and the deleted template's list `min-width` had not been carried over. The pane is now capped at `min(65 %, container − 220 px)` through one shared `clampPaneWidth` helper, with a CSS `min-width` on the list as the backstop for a window resize under an already-sized pane. **And a toggle-off threw away the dragged width:** `savePaneState` deleted `panew` whenever the pane went off, so closing the pane and then leaving the folder or refreshing lost a width the user had chosen. The two keys are now written independently. See FS-12, FS-13.)* *(Third review pass, same day, one low-severity bug fixed: the PT-12 sentinel filter kept a `_listing` entry (reachable only via a user registry binding it onto a file type) in the pane's candidate list, but the pane's `show()` only special-cases `_render` — an unhandled `_listing` fell into the ordinary-entry branch and built a `/render` URL from its `null` path, where full-screen `Preview` instead mounts `<Listing>` in place of the iframe. The pane has no slot for a nested `Listing`, so `_listing` is now excluded from the pane's candidates outright — it falls through to the file's next template, or the metadata card if `_listing` was the only entry. See PT-12.)* **Two accepted breaks, and they are different mechanisms** (do not conflate them): a **user registry** that still lists `"preview"` gets the ordinary dangling-name treatment of D95/CT-6 — the name resolves to no folder, is dropped from the rendered mode list, and `template_error` on the stat payload names it, with the entry showing broken (`exists:false`) in the Templates view (§23); an old **`?_mode=preview` URL or bookmark** hits PT-9's unknown-value path instead and falls back to the default mode (`_listing`) **silently, with no error at all** — `activeTemplate()` in `Preview.tsx` is a name lookup over `stat.templates` with a default fallback, and `template_error` is a registry-validation field that has nothing to say about a stale param. The user-visible outcome of the second break is the right one anyway: the URL asked for a folder preview with a pane and gets the listing that now has the pane. Owner approved 2026-07-30. *(Later revision, owner: the "one-time default is intended" call above is **overturned for the geometry, not for the provenance**. The pane's width is no longer a pixel number at all — it is a FRACTION of the split container, rendered as a percentage `flex-basis`, so it rescales with the window instead of freezing one window's arithmetic; the measuring layout effect and `PANE_FALLBACK_W` are deleted, since a percentage needs no measurement. The provenance half stands and is unchanged in spirit: only a **dragged** fraction is written to `panew`, now tracked as `sized` in the pane state rather than a ref. Old pixel values in `panew` are **ignored, not translated** — they were measured against a container this window may not have. See FS-12/FS-13.)* *(Later revision, owner, two passes, and between them **every persisted thing in this row is now gone**. **Pass one — visibility stops being a choice:** the toggle, the sticky `?preview=true` and the `pane` viewstate key are all deleted, and `shouldShowPane(containerW)` — a measurement of the split container above 700 px — decides the split by itself. One bit with three writers that had to agree, standing in for a question the layout can answer: there IS no room for two panes under 700 px, and there always is above it. The drag-to-close gesture goes with the toggle, because closing needs a way back and a width-decided split offers no reopen affordance. **Pass two — width stops being per folder:** `panew` is deleted too. Per-folder width was the "two sibling folders keep independent splits" reasoning of the original row, and it does not survive contact with use — the divider **jumped on ordinary navigation**, out of a folder that had a saved width into one that did not, snapping between the user's drag and the breakpoint default on every hop; the file preview's `_side` sidebar had used one global width all along and never had the problem. The dragged fraction is now a module-level variable in `listing/pane-store.ts` — one width for the whole DOCUMENT, no storage at all — which holds across every client-side navigation (the shell only ever `pushState`s) and is cleared by a **refresh**, deliberately: once dragged, a pane stops adapting to the window forever, and the way back must be something a user can find without being taught a gesture. `sessionStorage` would survive the refresh and defeat exactly that, so it is rejected; so is a double-click-to-reset. Undragged, the fraction now STEPS with the container (30 / 50 / 70 % at 700 / 1000 / 1440 px) instead of sitting at a flat half, so "more room, more preview" holds without anyone touching the divider. Existing `panew` and `pane` values are **purged once** at `listing/pane.ts` module init rather than translated — a width chosen for one folder is not a statement about the session — while the folder's **sort stays per folder**, which is the one thing here that really is a fact about the folder. The `parsePaneFrac` legacy-pixel rule directly above is retired with the key it read. See FS-9/FS-10/FS-12/FS-13.)* **(Revised again by #456 — the pane is not un-closable: a `_side` param on the folder URL records WHICH of its three modes is up (Preview / Claude / Git) or `_side=off`, that the user shut it, and the close chevron in the pane's header plus the reopening button in the listing's search row are exactly the two-place, chosen-by-state affordance the pass above deleted — restored as a MODE control, which is a question no measurement answers, and with the reopening half rendered while the pane is down, which is the "closing needs a way back" condition the old toggle failed. What stays deleted is the on/off toggle, `?preview=true` and the drag-to-close gesture. The width half of both passes is untouched. SPEC FS-10/FS-13.)** |

### git view — repository history scoped to the open path (2026-07-31)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D193 | **The BINDING half of this decision is OVERTURNED — `git` is FOLDER-ONLY (SPEC GT-2/GT-3): the universal `/` key and no file key, its gate refusing anything that is not a directory. Staging, stashing, committing and pushing are repository-level acts, and the working tree a file sits in is its folder's; the per-file question is answered INSIDE this view, not by a second mode — the Commits section is a log SCOPED to the open target, and selecting a commit renders the open file as of that commit through `GET /api/git/show`, resolved on read (the `history` mode that used to own the per-file question was deleted 2026-08-13; see D243). The file keys came back briefly between D235 and now, purely to make the mode reachable while a folder had no mode surface of its own — the preview pane peeks folder rows now, so that is bought properly instead. The rest of this row stands: path scoping, and `git rev-parse` as the authority.** *Original form:* **`git` is a built-in mode for BOTH directories and text-ish files, scoped to the open path, and its repo detection is `git rev-parse` in one bounded subprocess — the CLI is the authority, no marker probe, no walk** | A file explorer that will not tell you what changed in the folder you are standing in is asking you to keep a terminal open beside it. So `git` (SPEC §33) joins the universal `/` directory key and the hand-authored source/config/prose/log file keys as a `condition.py`-gated companion mode, and everything it shows is **scoped to the open path** rather than repo-wide: a repo-wide log is what `git log` is for, and the question a preview pane can answer better than a terminal is the narrow one — *what happened to THIS folder, THIS file.* Four decisions worth recording. **(1) Detection is the CLI, and a `.git` stat fast path was considered and dropped.** The owner's constraint was explicit — detect a repo without backtracking all files — and the tempting cheap answer is wrong twice over: `.git` exists only at the repository ROOT, so a probe has to ASCEND to answer a nested path (`repo/pkg/` has no `.git` of its own, and a nested path is the common case in a file explorer), and the two shapes it would then need to know about are exactly what a hand-rolled probe gets wrong — a `.git` **directory** in a clone, a `.git` **file** in a linked worktree or a submodule. `git rev-parse --is-inside-work-tree` answers all of them from any depth in one fork, and git's own ascent is O(depth) stats, never a descent; the gate itself never calls `listdir`/`scandir`/`glob` and the tests make enumeration **fatal**, so a listing added later fails rather than ships (the CT-12 rule `zarr_aoi/condition.py` documents, doubly binding here because this gate runs on every directory AND every text-ish file opened). Adding the stat probe *in front* of the CLI was rejected on the only ground that matters for a gate: it can turn a correct answer into a guess and cannot turn a wrong one into a right one, and one exec is already cheap. **(2) Mount-backed is refused BEFORE the fork, and refused again in the reader.** Same rule `graph/condition.py` makes (MD-11), through the same `shared/appenv.is_mount_backed` mechanism rather than a second copy, for a worse reason: the reader runs `git status`/`git log` on the path, and git over an rclone-NFS mount stats and lists its way through the work tree. The ordering is part of the requirement — a refusal that still forked git at the mount would have paid the cost the refusal avoids — and the check is repeated in `log.py` because a hand-written `?_mode=git` URL bypasses the switcher entirely. The gate is the UX; the module is the guarantee. **(3) One unscoped `git status`, filtered in Python — not one scoped call plus an unscoped one.** The header's clean/dirty light means the REPOSITORY (that is what the word means) while the list below means the SCOPE, and both fall out of one walk of the index this way. The filter has to reproduce git's pathspec semantics including the case a prefix test gets wrong: `--untracked-files=normal` collapses a wholly-untracked directory to `dir/`, so an entry can be an *ancestor* of the scope rather than a descendant and the scope is dirty because of it. **(4) The user's git config is deliberately left alone.** The reflex hardening is `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null` + `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1`, and it would break real repositories: `safe.directory` lives in the global config, and a repo the user has explicitly marked safe must keep working inside the app. So only the knobs that could corrupt parsing are overridden per command with `-c` (`core.quotepath`, `color.ui`, `log.showSignature`, `diff.noprefix`, `diff.mnemonicPrefix`), the non-interactivity is done with environment instead (`GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`, no pager, no askpass, no LFS smudge), and the external-diff kill is the `--no-ext-diff` **flag** rather than `-c diff.external=` — clearing the value tells git to run the empty program, a hard exit 128, which the tests caught. Everything a URL can carry is treated as hostile data: a `sha` is validated as a hex object name **before any argv is built** (so an option-shaped value cannot cause even one fork), every path rides after `--` wrapped in `:(literal)` so a filename holding `*`/`?`/`[`/a leading `:` matches itself, and argv is always a list. Nothing is unbounded: the log paginates with `limit + 1` as its own `has_more` probe, diffs **stream** through a byte cap and a line cap with a watchdog kill (captured output would buffer a hundred-megabyte diff into memory before it could be trimmed, and a manual read loop has no `timeout=`), truncation is reported in the UI, and the change list is capped too. Refusal is a **payload**, not an exception, so a non-repo / missing git / mount / empty repo renders a calm empty state and never the red traceback overlay — that overlay is a debugging affordance for a view's own bug, and "this folder has no git" is not one. File-key placement is a judgement call stated for the record: `git` goes on code, config, prose and logs, and NOT on record streams, tabular data, geo, media, 3D, archives or generated tool files — a scoped commit log over a data blob says nothing a diff can render, and `.json` in / `.jsonl` out is that same line drawn twice (`.json` is the dominant hand-authored config format; `.jsonl` is an append-only stream). The mode is **read-only forever**: nothing stages, commits, checks out or fetches, and `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0` says as much to git. Not reusing the name `history` — `templates/history/` is the app's own sidecar/view-history inspector (§24, D96) and has nothing to do with git. *(Owner ruling, same day: **a template has no header at all** — "a template is not the same as app, it does not require any headers." A template renders inside the shell's chrome, so the shell owns identity and the template ships content; the header band is deleted and the only facts that survive are the ones that are DATA rather than chrome, folded onto the section labels — the branch (and a detached/unborn HEAD) on `UNCOMMITTED`, with the dirty light dropped because `UNCOMMITTED n` already says it and a count beats a lamp. An intermediate "one compact status line, not a display title" step was the same argument stopped halfway and is superseded. See GT-10.)* *(Review pass, same day, three real defects found by Cursor Bugbot and confirmed by the reviewer, each a case of two facts sharing one signal. **(1)** The page grows its log WINDOW rather than paging, so the server's clamp on that window made `has_more` permanently true and "load more" refetched the same rows forever; the clamp is now its own `capped` field, and it is deliberately independent of the malformed-record guard, whose own fix (count the records git emitted, not the ones that survived parsing) had the OPPOSITE failure — a premature "end of history". Two defects on one expression, opposite in direction; neither signal may be derived from the other. **(2)** Only the NEW path of a rename was scope-tested, so a file moved OUT of the open folder vanished from Uncommitted — the exact change a scoped view exists to show. Both sides are now tested and the direction is reported, which is also the concrete justification for filtering an unscoped status in Python rather than delegating to a pathspec: `git status --porcelain -- pkg` was VERIFIED to omit that rename, so git's own scoping has the same defect. **(3)** An untracked DIRECTORY (git collapses one to a single `dir/` row) has no patch, so it fell through to the vs-HEAD branch and opened a blank pane wearing the commit-oriented sentence — wrong twice, since it is not a commit and the path IS in scope. It now answers with its contents via `ls-files --others --exclude-standard` (so .gitignore is honoured rather than reimplemented), streamed under the same byte cap and entry-capped, each file clicking through to its own diff. See GT-7, GT-8, GT-9.)* |

### Revert from Claude Code's own file-history store, not from git (2026-07-31, SPEC §34)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D194 | **The undo for an agent's edits reads Claude Code's `file-history` checkpoint store — deliberately NOT git — and "revert the last change" means the newest version whose content DIFFERS from disk, not the highest `@vN`** | Claude Code already writes a full copy of every file it is about to change, at `<claude-config-dir>/file-history/<sessionId>/<sha256(abspath)[:16]>@v<N>`, and the filename key being a pure function of the absolute path is the fact the whole design rests on: a file's entire timeline is a **glob**, so enumeration needs no transcript parsing and is cheap enough to run on a view boot. **Rejected: git as the authority.** Git answers "what did the last commit say", which is a different question from "what did this file look like before the agent touched it" — and it has no answer at all in a directory that is not a repository, which is where a lot of this app's files live. The two are complementary; a git-backed template is separate work and this does not touch it. **Rejected: parse the session transcript to build the timeline.** Those `.jsonl` files reach **5 MB+** and would be read on every render — a performance trap for a panel that is often collapsed. They are consulted only for the one fact the filesystem cannot carry (FH-5), opt-in, line-prefiltered before any `json.loads`, byte-capped, and wrapped so corruption degrades to "no extra rows". **The selection rule is the load-bearing decision, it was measured rather than assumed, and it took TWO tries.** First: on a real session 6 of 13 files matched their highest `@vN` and 7 did not, because disk moved on after the last checkpoint — so "restore the highest N" is wrong about half the time and would frequently be a silent no-op that reads as a broken button. That conclusion still holds. Its replacement, *"the newest version whose content differs from disk"*, is the more instructive failure: it is right whether a checkpoint is a pre- or a post-image, it sidesteps that ambiguity cleanly, it survived TWO independent reviews that audited it for bugs rather than questioning the premise — and **it oscillates on the second press.** With `disk == v3`, v3 does not differ so the target is v2; once `disk == v2`, v3 differs and is the newest such entry, so the target is v3 again. A two-state ping-pong, for ever, with v1 unreachable at any point: "Revert last change" undid itself on the second click and the start of the chain could not be reached at all. The user found it by pressing the button twice.
