The fault is in what the rule ANSWERS — "which checkpoint is most recent and isn't what I have" — which is not what undo means. **Undo is POSITIONAL:** locate where disk sits in the chain, then step backwards. Over the newest-first list: position = index of the *newest* entry whose content equals disk (newest, not oldest — with duplicate content on both sides of a real checkpoint, walking from the oldest match steps straight over it); target = the first *older* entry that still differs (the differs-check stays inside the walk rather than taking `position + 1`, because identical adjacent versions are common and restoring one writes the same bytes back); `position == -1` means disk is in no checkpoint at all, so the first step back is "discard to the most recent checkpoint" — the same index that yields `unique_current`, derived once so the two cannot disagree; and no older differing entry is `at_earliest`, a terminal state that DISABLES the button rather than falling back to something newer, since falling back is precisely what made the old rule a toggle. Now `v3 → v2 → v1 → delete → disabled`: monotonic, whole chain reachable. **Rejected: add a Redo button.** None is needed — the timeline already lets the user click a newer row explicitly, and that asymmetry is correct for a control labelled "Revert last change". *(One more instance of the same class, found by pressing the button four times in the running app: `at_earliest` may only be claimed by an ENRICHED scan. The boot timeline skips the 5 MB transcripts, so it cannot see the creation boundary and reported the terminal state one step early — and the view believed it and disabled the button on a file whose remaining step back was a delete `revert_plan` would have offered. The unenriched answer is now explicitly provisional (`enriched: false`) and the click asks the plan, which always enriches and is the authority.)* Two more empirical semantics get their own handling rather than a guess: **chains are per-session and N restarts**, so two sessions both holding a `@v2` for one path is ordinary and the merged timeline orders on **mtime**, never on N (a version is the pair, surfaced as one opaque id); and a **null `backupFileName` means the file did not exist**, so reverting across that boundary is a **delete**, never a restore of empty content that would leave a zero-byte file the agent never created. **That third semantic also produced the worst bug in the review, and the fix is an identity test rather than a tighter heuristic.** The boundary rows exist only in the transcript, and they were attributed by `trackingPath` being a path-boundary SUFFIX of the target — with this module having, by its own admission, no idea what the repo root is. `src/main.py`, `README.md` and `index.ts` recur across every checkout on a disk, so an unrelated project that CREATED its own `src/main.py` injected a boundary row into this file's timeline; because those rows sort by the transcript's own timestamp it was typically the NEWEST entry, so it became the revert target and turned "Revert last change" into a **DELETE of a file the agent had never touched**, behind a confirm sheet asserting "Claude created it" about it. Reproduced against the module. The record already carries `realParentDir`, an ABSOLUTE directory, on exactly these null-backup records — so the rule is now `join(realParentDir, basename(trackingPath)) == abspath(target)`: no suffix match, no cwd guess, no cross-project collision possible. **Rejected: narrow the suffix match, or add a cwd-prefix guard** — both are heuristics over a fact that is already available exactly. A record with no usable `realParentDir` is **refused rather than guessed**: the cost of refusing is one missing row, the cost of guessing is deleting the wrong file. This also let the slug cross-product go, replaced by a glob on `projects/*/<session>.jsonl` — the slug is the cwd with separators replaced, which is lossy and was never evidence of anything. |
| D195 | **The store is touched read-only, forever; the single write is to the target, behind a mandatory confirm step, an atomic replace, and a sidecar stash — because the bytes being overwritten are frequently the only copy** | Read-only-ness is asserted as a **whole-tree byte snapshot** across every action rather than per call, so a write added under the config dir later trips a test instead of shipping: that directory is the user's live edit history and this feature is a guest in it. One accepted corollary, chosen deliberately: the user's own annotate saves are not checkpointed by anybody, so they are **not revertible** — this reverts the agent's edits and only those. The sharp hazard is FH-3's other half: since disk often matches no checkpoint, a naive restore vaporizes work with no undo at all. Three independent defences, in order. **(1)** The plan payload computes `unique_current` — "no version holds what is on disk" — and the view gates an explicit warning on it, next to byte counts, line counts and the line delta, before any write; gating rather than always showing it is what keeps it a signal instead of noise people click past. The delta is stated as *what the restore does* (lines it introduces / takes away), because that is the number a confirm step has to show; the reverse framing reads identically on symmetric edits and lies on every asymmetric one, and above a byte cap or for non-text content it degrades to net counts and **says it is inexact** rather than implying a diff nobody computed (difflib is quadratic and a timeline renders every version). **(2)** The pre-restore content is stashed into the target's own `<file>.json` sidecar under `revertStash`, through the read-merge-write `annotate.py` already owns so every unowned key round-trips — never into the Claude dir. Bounded and skipped above a byte cap or for binary, because that sidecar is a small JSON file three writers rewrite constantly and `file_history` already is a version store; a revert the user confirmed is never blocked by a stash that could not be written. Two review findings landed here, both about the stash being *trustworthy* rather than merely present. It was read in TEXT mode, so universal-newline translation rewrote CRLF and lone-CR files: `b"line one\r\nline two\r\n"` stashed as `'line one\nline two\n'` while the sibling `size` recorded 20 against 18 characters — the sidecar contradicted itself and a hand-recovery would restore bytes that were never there. Binary read + explicit decode, `size` = byte count; this package ships a `windows/` dir, so CRLF is a live case. And the SKIP DECISION was computed inside the revert, after the click, surfacing only post-hoc — so the sheet carried a permanent hedge ("a copy is kept ... unless too large or not text") under which the one genuinely unrecoverable combination, `unique_current` AND no stash, read exactly like the safe case; `os.replace` then destroyed the only copy and the user found out in the past tense, beside "Reverted to v3". The predicate (a stat, a decode, an access) now runs in the PLAN, so the sheet states the truth and escalates that combination with stronger wording plus a button that names the act ("Overwrite permanently") rather than merely "Revert". *(It briefly also required a tick-to-confirm, gated on the irreversible case only; the owner removed that as friction after using the panel, so the escalated warning is now the ONLY thing between the click and unrecoverable loss and carries real visual weight in that case. The bridge's `confirm_unique` token is a separate mechanism, derived from the plan and never from widget state — removing the tick did not touch the programmatic guard.)* Each refusal is also reported truthfully: folding an EACCES into "not UTF-8 text", or a `getsize` failure into "nothing on disk to stash" (which reads as "the file is absent"), describes a fixable machine problem as a fact about the content. **(3)** The write itself: `file_writable` reuses `_sidecar_writable`'s gate — read-only mount FIRST via `shared/appenv`, because `os.access(W_OK)` **lies** under CacheMode=full (the write lands in the VFS cache and only 403s at the async upload) — plus W_OK on the directory, which the sidecar's gate does not need and an `os.replace` does, then mkstemp + `os.replace` in the target's own directory carrying the existing mode. **Path confinement is a matching problem, not a sanitizing one:** the client's selector is an opaque id matched against the enumerated timeline and never joined into a path, so `..`, separators and absolute prefixes have nothing to traverse, and a restore may only touch a path the store already has a version for — the only target guard a module that cannot see the view can offer. **Placement:** `templates/shared/file_history.py`, stdlib-only, reached by `sys.path` — **not** `fused_render/file_history.py`, which would be unreachable from the only place that needs it, since a template child under the fused engine has no PYTHONPATH (the same reason `appenv` exists). **Every failure crosses as data**, because anything raised out of a template's `main` becomes the red traceback overlay and "no store on this machine" is not an error — though "as data" is not the same as "as one undifferentiated message": an unreadable store or session dir is reported with its path and errno rather than as "no versions for this file", since `chmod` is actionable and the collapsed form sends the user looking at the wrong thing, and an ImportError (the folder copied without `shared/`) is distinguished from any other import-time failure, which used to be reported as "helper is not available" and read as "you copied the folder wrong". **Four more findings, each on the destructive path.** *The confirm gate lived only in the page:* `revert` chose its own target from `version_id=None`, with no plan echo and no confirmation token, and the sole guard was a source grep asserting one `action: "revert"` call site — which pins today's template, not the bridge, so any future second caller inherited an unguarded file-destroying entry point. The bridge now requires the plan's `id` back (also a freshness check: a plan built against one disk state and applied against another is how a user confirms one diff and gets another) plus `confirm_unique` when the plan set `unique_current`. *`enrich` changed what the button DID, not just what it showed:* it was a parameter of `revert_plan`/`apply_revert` and the view passed its History-panel state, so the same button performed a RESTORE before the panel was expanded and a DELETE after — one click, two destructive outcomes, decided by a disclosure widget. The parameter is gone; the plan and the write always enrich, and only the boot timeline stays cheap. *A symlinked target reported success and restored nothing:* `os.replace` swaps the LINK for a regular file rather than writing through it, so the real file kept its pre-revert content while the stash captured a file that was never overwritten — refused now, deliberately rather than `realpath`-ing first, because the store's key is the sha256 of the path the VIEW opened and following the link would revert a different chain. *A read-only-mount probe that RAISED fell through to `os.access`:* the blanket `except Exception` wrapped the call as well as the import, re-opening exactly the incident the probe exists for — a malformed `FUSED_RENDER_RO_MOUNTS` would let a doomed revert report `ok: true` and surface the 403 later at an async upload this UI never sees. Narrowed to `except ImportError` for the import (a missing `appenv` still degrades to `os.access`, since there is no flag to consult) with the call itself failing CLOSED. *(The identical shape in `_sidecar_writable` is pre-existing and deliberately left alone.)* *(Three bugs found by driving the running app rather than by reading it, all fixed: the collapsed row count read the unenriched list and said "(2)" over 3 rows — it is now the row loop's own counter, absent while collapsed, with enrichment landing before the open state paints so it cannot jump; an unparseable transcript stamp made a row claim 1970, and both label and tooltip now fall silent instead; and `renderHistory()` rewrites the note element, so setting an error message and THEN re-rendering wiped it — every **failed** revert was a silent no-op, the worst possible error path for a destructive action.)* *(Review follow-up: that write-order bug was fixed as an INSTANCE, not as a class, and a reviewer found its sibling one path over — the post-revert timeline reload can fail and write its own error into the same element, which the success message then erased, so a revert that landed but left the panel stale reported unqualified success. `loadHistory` now returns its error to the caller, which composes one honest sentence. Also fixed in the same pass: a dropped version was a silent `continue`, which under the positional rule moves both where the walk starts and where it lands — every drop is now recorded with its path, reason and errno, named in the payload's note, and a drop at or newer than the chosen target makes `revert_plan` REFUSE rather than walk to a different point in history and present it as "the last change"; older drops stay harmless, because one unreadable ancient checkpoint must not cost the user their undo, and an explicitly clicked row is never blocked since the user named it. And the confirm sheet no longer renders an unparseable timestamp as "1/1/1970", mirroring the guard the row list already had.)* *(Three owner-reported UI findings from using the panel, all in the template. The dot column had **three** glyphs over three colours carrying two unrelated meanings — collapsed to two states (`●` here, `○` elsewhere) with the revert target moved onto ROW treatment, an accent stripe plus wash, since `◉` and `●` are barely distinguishable at 11px and "where are you" and "what does the button do" belong in different visual channels; `at_earliest` consequently needs no fourth marker, because no striped row already reads as "nothing to go back to". The tick-to-confirm was **removed** as friction (see D194). And the post-revert reload collapsed the panel and threw away the outcome — which I had earlier written off as pre-existing shell behaviour rather than fixing; it now persists through `sessionStorage` keyed by the target path rather than a URL param, because the URL here is explicitly the SHARING channel for a review and a disclosure state is a workspace habit, per the D185 argument for pane geometry, with the outcome read-and-clear so it cannot replay on later reloads.)* *(Fourth review round, automated — four findings, all real, all live. **(1)** `writable` is a field on a SUCCESSFUL plan rather than an error, so a read-only target still opened a destructive confirm sheet from a timeline row while the main button had correctly gone dead; closed at BOTH layers, the plan-level check being the one that closes the class, and `writable_reason` now travels with the verdict because a read-only mount, a chmod'd file and an unwritable directory are three different things to do about it. **(2)** The History toggle flipped `histOpen` and painted the open label BEFORE awaiting the enrich, so a failed fetch left an expanded label over an empty list — the third instance of the optimistic-then-await trap in this one panel, fixed by having `loadHistory` always repaint from the last good timeline so its error path resyncs every label. **(3)** `_unsafe_skips`' `-inf` floor with no target was reported as contradicting the "older skips are harmless" docstring, with the suggested remedy being to treat them as harmless. **Not implemented, and I argued against it**: with no target, a version we failed to read is exactly a candidate for the older differing entry we did not find, so every skip genuinely matters and `-inf` is the correct floor — treating them as harmless would claim terminality from a scan with a hole in it, the same class of error as the oscillating rule. What WAS broken was the message (it blamed an unidentifiable last change, which describes the other case) and that a stable refusal was only discoverable by clicking; it is now an `unconfirmed` STATE, distinct from and mutually exclusive with `at_earliest`, that disables the button and shows its reason, with one `_selection` helper feeding both the timeline and the plan so they cannot disagree. **(4)** The stored outcome was cleared only on boot, so a reload that never arrived left it to reappear as fresh news on the next open, and a failing refresh on a dying page could write it back after a newer boot had consumed it — restructured into a one-writer/two-clearing-readers carry slot rather than given a fourth guard, since this mechanism had by then produced three separate defects.)* *(Fifth round, two findings, and both were the SAME root cause as B1 — a guard living below the layer that decides whether to OFFER the action — which is why this one is a seam and not a third point fix. **N1:** with blocking skips but a live target, `timeline` kept publishing that target, the panel struck the row as "this is what Revert does" and left the button enabled, and every press hit `revert_plan`'s refusal; a reload left the same live control, so it repeated. This is the half of B3 that the earlier scoping missed — only the no-target branch had been converted to a visible state. **N2:** a symlink was refused in `apply_revert` alone, so the sheet opened on a target that could not succeed and `_stash` ran first, reading THROUGH the link — a failed revert that had already put the wrong file's content into `revertStash`. **The fix is one authority**, `offer_reason`, answering "may this be offered and if not why", consulted by the panel, the rows, the sheet and the bridge: `timeline` now publishes `offer` and `offer_reason`, `revert` names a row only when that row is actionable, `writable_reason` absorbs the symlink and directory refusals (they are answers to "can this be reverted", and keeping them elsewhere is exactly what hid them), and the bridge re-checks before `_stash` — which has to be GATED rather than followed by a raise, since it runs first by design. Two asymmetries kept deliberately: an explicitly chosen version bypasses the automatic refusals but not writability, and FH-3's unenriched terminality stays provisional, which is why `offer` is its own field rather than inferred from `revert`.)* |

### app cloning — a deployed page's source, back on the viewer's machine (2026-07-30)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D196 | **A clone of a deployed page is a DOWNLOAD, not a git clone: no identity, so no update-in-place — and the publisher's consent is mount state, stated explicitly on every deploy** | Two shapes were available for the viewer flow. Reusing §26's GitHub clone path would have inherited its update-in-place branch, which is only safe because `.git` gives it identity: it can prove a folder is a clone of *this* repo before writing into it. An export archive carries no provenance we can verify, so the same code with a zip in place of a remote would happily overwrite a user's edited copy on a second clone. Hence a separate module and separate routes, every clone landing in a fresh `unique_dir` folder, and the two flows sharing only the confirm-step shape. On the publisher side the flag lives on the MOUNT, not the artifact, so turning cloning off is one record write and never needs the bundle a publisher may no longer be able to reproduce; the dialog therefore states the posture explicitly in both directions, because the CLI's preserve-on-omit (right for a CLI) would have made unticking the box silently do nothing. The unpack hardening moved to `zip_import.py` rather than being re-written for clones: the template import already had staging, symlink refusal, path-escape refusal and decompressed-size caps, and a second extractor is how a hardened path and an unhardened one end up side by side. |
| D197 | **A clone keeps the page's files and nothing else, and the app it clones is named after the page — not after the temp dir the export happened to use** | Two names leaked out of implementation details and into what the user sees. The bundle's `manifest.json` was written into the cloned folder as a `.fused-render-bundle.json` dotfile, on the argument that a later `export` could then reproduce the same bundle — but nothing ever read it back (`export_page` recomputes the manifest from the page's own files), so it was write-only clutter in the user's folder, one more path the confirm step had to predict, and a *second* move on the commit path that needed its own rollback to avoid reporting failure with a clone already on disk. Dropping it makes the commit a single rename and the preview's list exactly the page's files. Separately, `share create`/`repoint` derive the app name from the source directory they are handed, and what this module hands them is `tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="fused-render-deploy-")` — so pages published as `fused-render-deploy-pabxq903`, which is the name the deployments list showed, the name the clone inventory reported, and therefore the name a viewer's clone folder inherited. `--name` is now stated explicitly from the page (its stem, or its folder when the stem names nothing, sanitized), on repoint as well as create, since repoint re-derives it too. The clone's FOLDER is likewise named after the **link** now, falling back to the app name: a deployed page's URL ends in its token, and when the publisher chose that token (`share create --token my-solar-map`) it is the best name available — it is what the link the user followed says, and it survives redeploys, whereas the app name describes the source file and two pages can share it. An opaque CSPRNG token (26+ chars of lowercased base32) names nothing, so the app name wins there; the shape check that distinguishes them mirrors fused's `mounts.is_opaque_token` and is copied rather than imported, since `fused` is an optional extra and cloning must work with no CLI installed. It decides a folder name and nothing else — never a gate. The confirm button lost the folder name for the same reason: "Clone to local" names the action, and the body right above it already says where the files land. |
| D199 | **Pasted and dropped media go through a new multipart upload endpoint, land in a shared `assets/` the template creates, and are named by timestamp — with paste and drop sharing one helper** | Four choices, one feature (SPEC MD-23). **(a) Multipart, not base64 in JSON.** Base64 inflates a payload by a third: irrelevant for a screenshot, very relevant for a pasted video. `python-multipart` is already a core dependency and `templates_api.api_import_templates` is an existing `UploadFile` precedent, so this adds neither a dependency nor a pattern. **(b) A new `POST /api/fs/upload`, not a binary mode on `/api/fs/write`.** `_fs_write`'s contract is "content is a string", and its callers depend on the optimistic mtime lock and the create-exclusive 409 — neither of which means anything for a freshly pasted blob. Overloading it would fork that function down the middle; a sibling route reuses the guard sequence verbatim (X-Fused, the mount-backed/`mount_read_only` branch before any kernel probe, `_writable`, the RO-2 `readonly` 403) without touching the text path. It also needed `fused.uploadFile` **and** `fused.mkdir` on the runtime surface: the runtime had no directory-create at all, so a template could not make the folder it was about to upload into. **(c) The template creates `assets/`, the endpoint does not.** Keeps the upload route's contract identical to `_fs_write`, which deliberately never creates intermediate directories. The cost is one extra `mkdir` round-trip per paste whose `exists` 409 is expected and ignored. **(d) Timestamp names in a shared `assets/`, and one helper for both gestures.** Timestamps never collide, so a paste needs no directory scan and no prompt; a shared folder keeps note directories readable, and the trade-off — deleting a note leaves its media behind — matches Obsidian and belongs to an unreferenced-media sweep, not here. Paste and drop differ only in where the files come from and where the text lands, so `insertMediaFiles(view, files, pos)` takes the position as a parameter rather than reading the selection; two copies of ensure-dir/upload/insert would drift the moment either gained error handling. |
| D200 | **Bare URLs are decorated where they stand — a mark that becomes an anchor — rather than rewritten into `[url](url)`** | Three choices, one gap (SPEC MD-24). **(a) Display-only, never a document rewrite.** The obvious alternative — convert a pasted or typed URL into `[url](url)` — changes what the file says in order to change how it looks, and it only ever helps URLs pasted *after* the feature ships. Decorating at render time instead leaves every note byte-identical and repairs the URLs already sitting in notes people wrote months ago. It also keeps the one rewrite this template does do (⌘K, and paste-over-a-selection producing `[selected](url)`) as the only one, so "does typing change my file?" has a single answer. **(b) A mark, not a replacement widget.** Every other link surface here is a widget because it has markup to hide and a display text that differs from its source. A bare URL has neither: its display text already *is* its target, so a widget would swap those characters for an element rendering the identical characters — no visual gain, and the caret can no longer land inside the URL to edit it. `Decoration.mark` takes `tagName` and `attributes`, so the range becomes a real `<a class="lp-link">` without replacing anything. The angle form `<https://…>` does have markup, and its `<`/`>` therefore hide and reveal under the ordinary caret rule — the two shapes differ only in that. **(c) The grammar answers "is this a URL?", not a regex.** `URL` and `Autolink` are already nodes (the vendored GFM extension emits them), so this is two branches in the existing tree walk rather than a third regex pass with its own idea of where a URL ends and its own collision surface against the wikilink pass. The code guard rides along free — the grammar emits no `URL` node inside a fence or a code span — and where a guard is still written it reuses the narrow `CODE_NODES` set, because the last bespoke "is this code?" list silently stopped every wikilink from rendering (MD-18a). |
| D201 | **Ordered-list renumbering is a transaction filter over text, scoped to the edited list, anchored on its first item** | Four choices, one gap (SPEC MD-25). **(a) A filter, not more key bindings.** `markdownKeymap` renumbers on Enter because `insertNewlineContinueMarkup` calls the grammar package's own `renumberList` — which is not exported, so it cannot be reused, and every other gesture (Backspace, select-and-delete, ⌘X, ⌘⇧K, paste) was left stale. Binding those individually means enumerating gestures forever and still missing the next one; `EditorState.transactionFilter` sees every edit whatever produced it. **(b) Text, not the syntax tree.** A filter runs before the new state exists, and `tr.state` would force one to be built and then possibly discarded on every keystroke. The shape being matched — a marker at the start of a line — is one the tree adds nothing to. The single thing text alone gets wrong is a fenced block, where digits-then-dot is not a list, so fences are tracked in an explicit pass. **(c) Scoped to the list the edit landed in, not the document.** Renumbering every list on every keystroke would sweep a deliberately odd list two paragraphs away into an undo step the user never made. **(d) The first item's number anchors the sequence.** `3.` `4.` `5.` — a list resumed after a paragraph — is textually identical to `1.` `2.` `3.` with its head deleted, so preserving a deliberate start and normalising to 1 cannot both be had. Anchoring is what `markdownKeymap`'s Enter path already does, which makes it the only rule under which Enter and Backspace agree with each other. Undo, redo and programmatic dispatches are excluded through a user-event allow-list (`input`/`delete`/`move`) rather than by naming undo, and the renumbering is appended to the *same* transaction so one undo takes back the edit and its numbering together. |
| D202 | **Vertical spacing is carried by unconditional line decorations, padded at a block's two edges** | Three choices, one gap (SPEC MD-26). **(a) Line decorations, because inline marks cannot hold a margin.** The heading scale was already there as `.lp-h1`…`.lp-h6`, but those are `Decoration.mark` over a range of the source, and a vertical margin on an inline span does nothing. `Decoration.line` is the only place it can live, and `lp-fence-line` was already the precedent for reaching it. **(b) Unconditional, never gated on the caret.** This is the constraint that shapes the whole feature. The editor un-renders the line the caret is on, so if spacing differed between a line's rendered and its caret-revealed state, the document would visibly jump under the cursor on every arrow-down — the worst possible bug in a typography change, because it only appears while editing and never in a screenshot. The heading *size* mark is already applied regardless of the caret for exactly this reason, so the spacing simply matches it. **(c) Edges, not every line.** A multi-line block gets `-top`/`-bot` classes on its first and last line only. Padding every line would render a fence as a stack of separated tinted panels, and margin on every list row would space the rows apart from each other — the opposite of a list reading as one block. A nested list is skipped because it lies inside its parent's range and would put a gap in the middle. Bottom margins are kept small throughout: markdown's blank separator line is already a full line-height, so these rules add only what markdown has no way to express — the space above a heading, and the gap around a block. |
| D203 | **The OS clipboard is bridged through the LOCAL BACKEND, not the web Clipboard API — copy-out and paste-in both carry absolute paths, cut is excluded, and the Linux write serves one file-manager family** | A webview cannot read or write the native file flavors Finder/Explorer/Nautilus use; `clipboardData.files` would upload every byte and drops folders entirely. The Python server already runs on the user's own machine (it binds `127.0.0.1`, D2/D3), so it can be the bridge the webview lacks — and once real absolute paths cross, the *existing* server-side `_fs_copy` does the work: instant at any file size, folders included, no bytes through the browser. **The OS clipboard is a second writer of the EXISTING module clipboard store** rather than a parallel "external clipboard" state, so all four Paste sites, the `disabled: !clipboard` checks and the cut-dimming keep working untouched; rejected threading an `external` flag through `Clipboard` that every consumer must then branch on. **The change token is a content fingerprint, not a native change counter:** macOS has `changeCount` and Windows `GetClipboardSequenceNumber`, but Linux has no analog, so hashing the ordered path list is the only token all three platforms can produce — and it is fully testable with no OS involved. It tracks **last seen**, not last written, which is what stops a stale system clipboard from clobbering a pending in-app cut on every focus change; the cost is that re-copying an identical selection in the file manager is not seen as a new event, which is harmless because adopting the same paths is idempotent. **Reconcile on focus, not on a poll** — returning to the app is the only moment the clipboard can have changed from the user's point of view, and `useRefreshOnReturn` exists for exactly this posture; a copy made while the app is already focused is missed until the next focus change, and a read on Paste-menu-open can be added later if that bites. **Cut is excluded**, in both directions: no platform exposes a reliable cut-vs-copy flag on read, so honouring one would mean deleting the user's source files on a guess. **The Linux write covers one file-manager family and that is not engineerable away** — there is no OS-owned clipboard on X11/Wayland, GNOME and KDE disagree on the format, and `xclip`/`wl-copy` publish one target per invocation with a second call replacing the first; dispatching on `XDG_CURRENT_DESKTOP` serves the session actually in use, reading tries both so pasting *in* works everywhere, and a resident GTK/Qt owner process offering both targets at once is the documented upgrade. **No new dependencies** (pyobjc is already in the `[app]` extra, `ctypes` is stdlib, Linux detects its tools rather than requiring them), and **every failure mode is the same one**: absent pyobjc, a hardened sandbox, no `xclip`, a dead request — all `supported: false`, a normal 200, leaving today's in-app-only behaviour intact rather than erroring in the UI. Cross-platform *testability* was a design constraint, not an afterthought: the Windows `DROPFILES` construction and slash normalisation, and the whole Linux backend behind faked `which`/`subprocess`, are pure functions tested on every platform, so only the real OS round-trips are platform-gated. |

### macOS mount stability — rclone NFS handle cache (2026-08-03)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D198 | **The macOS mount's fragility was rclone's NFS handle cache, not the kernel client: the daemon now runs `RCLONE_NFS_CACHE_TYPE=disk` (env, NOT argv), and the kernel options gain `intr`/`nolocks` while `retrans` is dropped** | Reported symptom, long-standing: *any* recursive walk of `~/.fused-render` — a global editor/agent search, ripgrep, Spotlight, or one flat million-key S3 prefix — permanently killed every mount, healed only by a force-unmount. Every previous mitigation treated it as a kernel-NFS-client or S3-latency problem (D102's rc-routed listings, D156's walk refusals, the HTTP serves) and each helped only by keeping *our* code off the mount; external walkers still killed it. The actual cause is server-side and has nothing to do with latency: go-nfs's default in-memory `CachingHandler` (`helpers/cachinghandler.go`, v0.0.4 = what rclone v1.74.4 pins) scans EVERY cached handle on every handle resolution — `for _, k := range c.activeHandles.Keys()` with a `Peek`+`hasPrefix` per key, under the handler lock, on essentially every NFS RPC. `ToHandle` mints one handle per directory entry and READDIRPLUS calls it per entry, so a walk drives N toward `--nfs-cache-handle-limit` (**default 1_000_000**); the LRU has no TTL and shrinks only by eviction at the limit, so the mount NEVER recovers — exactly the observed signature (permanent, not time-dependent). Measured on a LOCAL 50k-file tree so no S3 latency was involved: per-stat 0.12ms at 0 handles -> 3.58ms at 50k (linear, 29x), and re-listing one unchanged 500-entry directory 80ms -> 1801ms; under `disk` both stayed FLAT (0.12ms, ~63ms). Extrapolated to the 1M limit, `memory` reaches ~70ms per stat, i.e. ~36s for one 500-entry listing. rclone's own `diskHandler.FromHandle` is a sha256 of the path plus one small file read, no scan. Must be set in the ENVIRONMENT: `--nfs-cache-type`/`--nfs-cache-dir` are registered on the `serve nfs`/`nfsmount` command flag sets, so `rcd` rejects them as an unknown flag and refuses to start — pinned by a test, since getting this wrong breaks mounts entirely. Bonus: disk handles derive from the path, so they survive `restart_rcd` instead of handing live clients stale handles. The cache is reset per spawn (the disk handler never evicts, and a new daemon cannot honour handles it did not mint). Kernel side, `intr` makes a process stuck on a slow mount KILLABLE (macOS defaults to nointr — the unkillable beachballed ripgrep) and `nolocks` closes a latent hang: go-nfs registers no NLM program, yet macOS enables remote locks by default, and DuckDB/SQLite opening a mounted file takes a real lock. **Rejected:** `soft` — it converts a stall into a mid-read EIO that DuckDB/rasterio surface as file corruption on a merely-slow read, and on a read-only mount it silently implies `deadtimeout=60`, force-unmounting the mount out from under the app; the stalls it appeared to fix were this handle cache. `retrans=2` was **inert** (mount_nfs(8) defines it for soft mounts; ours is hard) and is removed rather than left reading as live tuning. `timeo=600` kept, with the units recorded: tenths of a second, so 60s. **Also rejected — FUSE-T** (evaluated same day): technically viable (the bundled rclone already ships `cmount` and probes `/usr/local/lib/libfuse-t.dylib`, and is signed with `disable-library-validation`; the rc `mountType` is `cmount`, not `mount`), but its licence is free for NON-COMMERCIAL use only and reserves bundling with commercial software, so it could only ever be user-installed-and-detected like WinFsp (D132). It is also not clearly a fix: its default backend is still an NFSv4 loopback mount, fuse-t issue #105 reports our exact symptom shape (every syscall ~30ms after a 300k-file walk, avoided only with `-o noattrcache`), the FSKit backend that would escape NFS is Experimental + needs a manual per-user System Settings enable + reports 0 bytes free (blocking writes), and for the eventual rw-S3 goal it is actively worse than nfsmount (silent write failures under `-o ro`, mtime-on-read triggering full re-uploads, AppleDouble `._*` sidecars uploaded into the bucket) |

### execution engine — the pref's default flips to fused (2026-08-03)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D204 | **The `engine` pref's default flips from `builtin` to fused-when-available — knowingly re-accepting the install-order-dependent default D70 and D80 both rejected** | `prefs.selected_engine()`'s unset/unknown value reads as `"fused"` instead of `"builtin"`. Nothing else in the resolution changes, and that is what makes it safe: `effective_engine()` already ANDs the selection with a live availability probe, so "fused by default" resolves to fused *iff* the package is importable and to builtin otherwise — a default that depends on the environment, never one that breaks in it. A stored `"builtin"` still **pins** builtin: the default flipped, the choice did not, and an importable `fused` overriding a user who chose builtin is exactly D70's surprise. `FUSED_RENDER_ENGINE` semantics are untouched — still the process override that beats the pref, still validated once at startup by `server._forced_engine` (`=fused` still fails loudly when missing). The Preferences radio stays **two-way**; the availability AND already is what an `auto` value would have meant, so a third stored value would only be a second spelling of the default. The page states the new default and, for the now-common unset-pref-without-the-package case, keeps saying "Currently running: Local (built-in) — falling back to Local while the fused package is unavailable" beside the checked-but-disabled Fused radio. `tests/conftest.py` pins `FUSED_RENDER_ENGINE=builtin` ambiently, so the suite's incidental `/api/run` coverage does not change runner depending on whether the optional extra is installed. | Owner call 2026-08-03: the fused engine's UX has improved substantially since the default was set, so it should be what a fresh install gets. This **reverses** two recorded calls and is recorded as a reversal rather than left to look like drift: **D70** flipped `FUSED_RENDER_ENGINE`'s default from `auto` to `builtin`, and **D80** then chose `builtin` for this pref, both on the same reasoning — an install-order-dependent default was surprising, because the same fused-render install could run pages under two different contracts depending on what else shared the environment. That cost is re-accepted, not forgotten: the owner's judgement is that with the fused engine's UX where it now is, and with **both engines running locally** (PF-3 — neither touches a named Fused environment), the remaining contract difference is a much smaller surprise than it was in July, and the better default is worth it. Rejected an `"auto"` pref value explicitly (the owner's call): the resolver's availability AND already provides that behaviour, and a stored `auto` would make two values mean the same thing while giving the radio a state that is not a choice. |

### Sidecar relocated under home_dir() (2026-07-31)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D205 | **The per-file sidecar (`claudeSessions`/`bookmarkHistory`/`lastSession`/`comments`/`docs`/`excel`/`usd`/`latex`/`slides` keys) moves from a sibling of the target file to `home_dir()/sidecar/<mapped path>.json` — REVERSING D83's explicit "not under FUSED_RENDER_HOME" invariant** | `shell/storage.py`'s new `sidecar_path(file)` (mirrored, stdlib-only, in `templates/shared/appenv.py` for template subprocesses per PY-15) maps an absolute path into a subtree under `home_dir()/sidecar/`: a POSIX path just drops its leading `/`, a Windows drive letter becomes its own single-letter folder (`C:\Users\...` → `sidecar/C/Users/...`), a UNC share nests under `sidecar/unc/<server>/<share>/...`. Built on `ntpath.splitdrive` (not a live `os.path`) so the classifier is pure and testable on any host, the same discipline `_view_url_codec.py` uses. Case is preserved exactly — never folded — so two paths that differ only in case can't collide on a case-sensitive filesystem. All 6 Python `_sidecar_path` definitions (`server/session.py`, `shell/bookmarks.py`, `templates/claude/agent.py`, `templates/annotate/annotate.py`, `templates/slides/slides.py`, `templates/usd/reader.py`) now delegate to it; the JS side gets one shared implementation in `static/runtime.js` (`fused.sidecarPath`/`fused.targetPathFromSidecarPath`, async — the mapping needs a server-provided `sidecar_root` fetched once via `/api/config`) instead of five independent `file + ".json"` copies. `history/template.html`'s sidecar↔target role resolution (HV-3) is now exact (`targetPathFromSidecarPath` either resolves or doesn't) instead of a same-directory `<name>.<ext>.json` shape guess. **Structural side effect, not a separate fix:** every mount-read-only gate that existed ONLY because the sidecar sat on the source file's own (possibly remote, possibly read-only) filesystem — `session.py`'s `_session_put`, `bookmarks.py`'s history mirror, `agent.py`'s `_mount_read_only`, `annotate.py`/`slides.py`/`usd/reader.py`'s `_sidecar_writable` — is now unreachable and removed: the sidecar is always under the app's own writable home dir, so the "sidecar-write incident" (CacheMode=full 403-looping a doomed PutObject) this repo names repeatedly can no longer happen, and reading/writing a sidecar for a mounted file no longer costs a network stat. `_sidecar_writable` (annotate/slides/usd) now answers via a new `appenv.nearest_existing_dir` (walk up to the nearest ancestor that exists, since the sidecar's subtree usually doesn't exist yet) rather than checking the target's own parent dir | Owner call 2026-07-31, no migration (old co-located `<file>.json` sidecars are simply abandoned — "don't care if existing sidecars stop working"). Explicitly accepted trade-offs, discussed and decided before implementation: (1) **no GC for orphaned sidecars** — today, deleting/moving a project folder frees its sidecars for free (co-located); after this, a sidecar survives under `home_dir()/sidecar/` forever with nothing to reclaim it, unbounded growth over time — owner chose to skip building cleanup for now rather than block on it. (2) **read-only source files now get sidecars** — previously an explicit UX gate; now a structural side effect of the mount fix, and the owner took it (recommended default). (3) **exact case preservation over folding to lowercase** — the owner's own example used lowercase (`sidecar/users/vasu/...`), but exact-case was chosen to avoid case-fold collisions on case-sensitive filesystems; not implemented as literally typed. (4) **portability regression accepted implicitly**: zipping/copying a folder no longer carries its sidecar data along, a reversal of the co-location rationale in `docs/CLAUDE-TEMPLATE-POC.md`'s original design notes — not re-litigated, since the owner's stated goal was specifically to stop sidecars from living in the user's own directories. Rejected: server-side templating of the sidecar root into each template's HTML at render time (adds a second injection mechanism where `/api/config` — already fetched once at boot for `mounts_root`/`calls_dir` — covers it for free); storing a `_source` field inside the sidecar JSON for the history view's reverse lookup (unnecessary — the forward mapping is mechanically invertible per-host, so no writer needs to remember to stamp it) *(Follow-up, 2026-08-04: `templates/claude_split/agent.py` — a fork of `templates/claude/agent.py` added on `main` in parallel with this decision, so it never got the migration — moves onto `appenv.sidecar_path` too, making it 7 Python `_sidecar_path` definitions, not 6. Its folder-target special case (a `.claude-split.json` dotfile written INSIDE the target directory, to avoid colliding with a sibling `<folder>.json` under the old co-located scheme) is dropped: that collision risk doesn't exist once the sidecar lives in its own tree under `home_dir()`, so a directory target now resolves through the exact same function as a file target. Its `_mount_read_only` gate is removed for the same structural reason as the other five. `app_git.py`'s `_GITIGNORE` (`*.html.json`/`.claude-split.json`) and claude_split's own mirrored `.git/info/exclude` injection are deliberately left in place, now purely as legacy defense — a repo from before this decision may still carry an old co-located sidecar file, and `git add -A` must still not sweep it into history; no fresh repo will ever grow one of these files again.)* *(Follow-up, 2026-08-04: `fused.sidecarPath`/`fused.targetPathFromSidecarPath` are pulled OFF `window.fused` entirely — owner's call, on review. `window.fused` is `docs/EXPORT.md`'s documented portable-subset contract, kept forever and mirrored by the separately-maintained hosted stub (the `fused` wheel's own copy of `runtime.js`) — every function added there is a promise to user-authored templates, not just to the 6 built-in ones that actually call it. Worse than an undocumented hosted gap: the sidecar is the exact JSON file five other built-in features read-merge-write into (`claudeSessions`/`bookmarkHistory`/`comments`/...), so a third-party template calling `writeFile` on a path it got from a public `sidecarPath()` would clobber that file wholesale instead of merging — a footgun the function's signature gives no hint of. Now `window._fusedSidecarPath`/`window._fusedTargetPathFromSidecarPath` — plain globals, not nested under `fused` — reachable by built-in templates only because they execute in the same window this script is injected into; absent from the hosted stub, and not meant to be added there. Every built-in caller (`docs`/`excel`/`latex`/`usd`/`annotate`/`history` templates) updated to the new names.)* |
| D206 | **claude_split's chat gets two live-app-state channels — a `<live-app-state>` block pushed with every message and an `app_state` MCP tool it can pull mid-turn — and the DOM outline reuses the ANNOTATION layer's element identifiers rather than minting its own** | `templates/claude_split/template.html` snapshots the framed app at send time (`appStateSnapshot`): entry path, title, url, the app's own params, a depth-4 / 60-node structural outline of `<body>` (tag, id, class, own text ≤120 chars — never `outerHTML`), and a 50-entry parent-side ring buffer of `console.error`/`warn`, uncaught errors, unhandled rejections and `reload` markers. `appStateBlock` wraps it in a `<live-app-state>` block; `agent.py`'s `_strip_app_state` peels that back out of everything user-facing (meta.json, the re-attach probe, a restored transcript) so the block is model-only. The pull half is a second tool on the existing approval MCP server, parked in a SIBLING `appstate/` directory (never `perm/` — a snapshot answer is not a verdict), answered from the poll loop with a snapshot taken THEN, and self-bounding on a timeout so an absent page never wedges `claude`. Both blocks that can ride a message (this one and #372's annotation preamble) are composed in ONE function (`composeOutgoing`) and removed by ONE function (`stripBlocks`), because `stripAnnBlock` recognises its preamble only at position zero: an app-state block in front of it silently no-ops that strip and leaks annotation JSON into the transcript, and one placed after the annotation fence leaks itself instead — neither order works with a strip that knows only one block. Outline nodes carry `path` from **`annPathOf`**, the annotation layer's own `tag:nth-of-type` builder, plus `id` — one implementation, called from both places, with a test asserting an outline path round-trips through `annResolve` back to the same element. The `stop · esc` control (button on the working line + Escape, `runEnding` as a pure decision) is ported from the `claude` template; Escape's precedence is a pure `escapeAction(composerOpen, runId)` — an open annotation composer wins over killing the run. | The agent was editing a page it could not look at: whether its change rendered, whether the console filled with errors, whether the page went blank were all unanswerable without asking the user to describe their own screen. **The frame-descent layer this feature originally needed is DROPPED, not merely unused** — it existed because the pane framed `/embed/<app>` (fused-render's React shell, with the app nested one iframe deeper), so reaching `leftframe.contentDocument` described the viewer's chrome and captured none of the app's logging; **#372** (element-level annotations) reframed the pane as `/render?path=<entry>`, the raw rendered document, which makes the frame's own `contentDocument` the app's — so `reachableFrames`/`hasRuntime`/`appFrameOf`/`resolveAppWindow` and the 100 ms `setInterval` they needed are deleted and the instrumentation hangs off the same `load` event #372 already uses to rewire annotations (recorded explicitly so the deletion does not read as drift). Sharing #372's identifier scheme is the deliberate half of that inheritance: the element a user pinned and the node the agent reads in the outline are then literally the same name, so it can act on a pin without guessing, and a duplicated path builder is what D146 forbids. Snapshot is TEXT ONLY for #372's stated reason — structured anchors, no screenshots or raw coordinates, so Claude can map each note straight back to the app's HTML source. `--allowed-tools mcp__fused_approvals__app_state` pre-approves the pull channel and nothing else: the card would read "may Claude look at the screen you are looking at", which is a non-decision the user would answer once per edit, and a prompt with no decision in it trains people to click through prompts that do have one — transparency comes instead from a one-line `read app state` note in the log (and `app state attached` under each pushed turn), and the tool call is genuinely BLOCKED until the page answers, so nothing happens silently. **Accepted costs, stated plainly:** (1) the rendered page's own text — up to ~60 nodes × 120 chars, plus title and 50 console lines — now flows into the prompt on **every turn**; it is local-only (the page is already on screen, no file is read to get it) and capped, but it is a real, permanent increase in what each message carries, and a snapshot with nothing in it returns `null` so a turn with nothing to say looks exactly like a pre-feature turn. (2) A residual logging gap: the `load` hook cannot beat the app's FIRST inline script, so logging from there is still missed — the DOM outline shows what that script did or failed to do, and everything from the next tick onwards is captured. Rejected: a permission card for `app_state` (see above); clearing the console buffer on reload ("these three errors were there before, this one appeared after your edit" is the buffer's whole value, and clearing destroys it); wrapping `console.log` (50 ring slots spent on an app's chatter would push out the errors they exist for); quoting `script`/`style` bodies in the outline (source the agent can read properly, and a clipped 120 chars of it is a misleading fragment). |
| D207 | **claude_split annotations gain a PNG crop per note, attached BY FILE PATH — REVISING, not reverting, #372's "element-level, never screenshots or raw coordinates"** | On send, `templates/claude_split/template.html` rasterises the visible left pane ONCE (`shotPane`: clone the framed `<body>`, copy every `getComputedStyle` property onto the clone's inline `style` because `<style>` rules do not travel with a serialized element, replace each `<canvas>` with an `<img>` of its own `toDataURL` pixels because a `<canvas>` serializes as an empty element, `XMLSerializer` → `<svg><foreignObject>` → `Image` → `drawImage`), then cuts each pending note's crop out of that one bitmap at the rect its pin already uses (`annStageRect` → `shotCropRect`). Each crop is uploaded via `fused.uploadFile` (the binary-safe path — `fused.writeFile` is UTF-8 only and mangles a PNG) into `agent.py`'s new `SHOTS` directory, a SIBLING of `runs` under the template's own 0700 temp root, and the annotation JSON gains `shot` (absolute path, or `null`) and `shotNote` (why it is null). `agent.py`'s spawn line pre-approves exactly that directory with `Read(//<shots>/**)` beside the existing `app_state` tool. Caps mirror the `APP_STATE_MAX_*` style: `SHOT_MAX_EDGE` 640px longest edge, `SHOT_MAX_BYTES` 300KB (halved and re-encoded while over — PNG has no quality dial), `SHOT_MAX_COUNT` 6 per send, `SHOT_MIN_AREA` 64px², `SHOT_TIMEOUT_MS` 6s for the whole capture; crops are pruned at 12h / 200 files. The pull channel's `null`-snapshot handling is fixed in the same work (`appStatePull` + a bounded retry): the push channel's "nothing worth a block" was being handed to `agent.py` as `"null"` and recorded as a permanent "could not read the app's state", which a live-reload — the thing an edit triggers — provoked routinely. | **This REVISES D206/#372 rather than reverting it.** The structured anchors stay primary and authoritative: `anchorId`/`anchorPath`/`tag`/`text` are still what Claude edits FROM and still map onto the app's HTML source, `iu`/`iv` stay FRACTIONAL (never raw pixels), and the crop is an *attachment to* an anchor, never a substitute for one — it exists for the claims a path cannot carry ("this is misaligned", "this chart drew nothing", "the wrong colour"), which #372's text-only model left the user describing in prose. **A file path with a lazy `Read` beats an inline image** because it costs ZERO tokens until the agent decides the visual matters; an inline base64 block (or an MCP image content block through `app_state`) is paid on every turn that carries an annotation whether or not the pixels were ever the question, and most annotations are answerable from the anchor alone. Pre-approving `Read` of one directory is what keeps "choose to look" free of a permission card the user would answer once per screenshot — the same argument D206 made for `app_state`, and the double-slash rule form was verified against claude 2.1.221 rather than assumed (a single-slash `Read(/abs/**)` matches nothing, silently). **One pane capture cropped N times, never N standalone subtree serializations:** a flex child serialized alone renders without its parent's layout, collapsing to its content width, so it stops looking like the thing the user pointed at — the ancestor context IS the picture. It also makes the intrinsic (canvas/img) case fall out of the same code path instead of needing a second one. **Known gap, stated rather than hidden: blank WebGL.** maplibre and deck.gl create their context with `preserveDrawingBuffer: false`, so `toDataURL` on their canvas reads back fully transparent; any annotation that is or contains such a canvas gets `shot: null` and a `shotNote` naming the cause and saying the app is very likely drawing fine, because an agent shown a blank image concludes the app rendered nothing and spends a debugging loop on a bug that does not exist. Forcing `preserveDrawingBuffer: true` by patching `HTMLCanvasElement.prototype.getContext` inside the framed document was considered and NOT done: the parent's `load` listener fires after the app's inline scripts (the same gap D206 records for console logging), so it needs a document-start injection point in `/render` that does not exist, and adding one for every template to serve this one is the wrong shape of change. **Accepted cost: send latency.** The send path is now genuinely async — inlining computed styles is O(elements × ~340 properties) and the `Image` load plus per-crop encode are real work, so a busy app can put a noticeable pause between pressing Enter and the run starting. Mitigated, not eliminated: the user's bubble is appended BEFORE the capture so their words appear instantly, and every failure mode — a throw, a `null` blob, an over-budget crop, the 6s timeout — degrades to sending the annotations WITHOUT shots, because a user losing their typed message to a failed screenshot is unacceptable. Rejected: writing crops into the user's project directory (pollutes their files, and `app_git.py` would sweep binaries into history); a per-run directory (the run dir does not exist until `_start`, strictly after the crops are captured, so it would mean writing them elsewhere and moving them); returning the crop as an MCP image content block through `app_state` (would change that tool's text-only contract for a payload the agent pays for whether it wanted it or not). |
| D208 | **claude_split gains an OPT-IN, per-message screenshot of the WHOLE visible pane, riding its own `<pane-shot>` block as a `view`/`viewNote` pair — EXTENDING D207 rather than replacing it** | A pill in both composers (`viewShotWanted`, `🖼`) marks the NEXT send only; `sendMessage` reads it into a local and clears it before any await. `annCaptureShots(pending, deadline, wantView)` encodes the whole `shotPane` bitmap — `{left:0, top:0, width, height}` — as ONE extra encode of a bitmap that already exists, uploads it as `<stamp>-view.<ext>` into the same `SHOTS` directory (so the same `Read(//<shots>/**)` pre-approval and the same 12h/200-file pruning cover it), and returns `{view: {view, viewNote}, viewThumb}`. It has its OWN caps — `SHOT_VIEW_EDGE` 1600, `SHOT_VIEW_BYTES` 900KB — and explicitly does NOT inherit `SHOT_MAX_EDGE`. `paneShotBlock` wraps it in a `<pane-shot>` block that `composeOutgoing` places after the app-state block and before the annotations, and `stripPaneBlock` removes position-independently BEFORE `stripAnnBlock` (whose preamble is only recognised at position zero). The pane shot is captured before any crop, does not count toward `SHOT_MAX_COUNT`, shares the crops' one `SHOT_TIMEOUT_MS`, and gets a thumbnail row in the send receipt. | **A crop cannot answer the question this is for.** "The whole layout is wrong", "these two panels overlap", "everything shifted down" have no single element as their subject, and a crop of one element actively HIDES the answer by cutting away the relationship that IS the bug — a case D207 left unserved, and one the user was left describing in prose exactly as #372 left the misalignment case. It is cheap because `shotPane` already rasterises the entire pane for the crops: one more encode, no second capture. **Deliberately not gated on `pending.length`:** "the whole layout is broken" often has nothing to click, so a message with no annotations at all must be able to carry one. **A sibling block, not a per-annotation field:** `shot`/`shotNote` are per-anchor, so hanging a pane picture off one annotation is a category error and repeating it on all of them is a lie — and a pane shot can ride a message that has no annotation block to live in. Tag-delimited like `<live-app-state>` so its strip is position-independent; the annotation block's prefix-matched strip is the one fragile one and a second was not affordable. **Never suppressed for blank WebGL, only annotated** (`shotBlankRegions` names the unreadable rectangles): suppressing a blank CROP leaves the other crops, while suppressing the pane leaves nothing at all — so this reuses the `shotNote`-beside-a-real-`shot` contract instead of inventing a second convention. **Rejected: sticky.** A screenshot toggle that stays on quietly becomes an always-on cost — a rasterise, an encode and a file per turn — for a picture the user asked for once. **Rejected: exposing it to `app_state`.** Letting the model request pixels every turn is the very cost D206/D207 avoided; `app_state` already answers "did my edit land" symbolically and keeps its single-TEXT-block contract, and a picture of the user's screen is theirs to offer rather than the agent's to take. **Rejected: inheriting `SHOT_MAX_EDGE`** — a whole pane squeezed to 640px is unreadable, which defeats the only reason to send one. Accepted cost: 1600px is ~6x the pixels of a crop, so the byte cap actually bites here, which is what makes D209's quality dial matter. |
| D209 | **A shot tries WebP and falls back to PNG, and its filename is taken from `blob.type` — never from the format requested — because WKWebView cannot encode WebP and fails SILENTLY** | `shotEncode` tries `image/webp` at `SHOT_WEBP_QUALITY` (0.8 then 0.6) at full size, then `image/png`, and only then halves the resolution and repeats. `shotExt(blob)` returns `.webp` only when `blob.type` is `image/webp`, and BOTH crop and pane-shot naming go through it. Whether the engine really encodes WebP is probed once per page (`shotWebpOk`), not once per shot. | **Measured, not assumed.** Claude Code's `Read` accepts `.webp`: verified with the real CLI reading a webp out of the real shots dir under the real `Read(//<abs>/**)` rule — no permission card, and small text stayed legible down to quality 60. **WKWebView — which the desktop app ships — cannot encode WebP and does not say so:** `canvas.toBlob(cb, "image/webp", q)` yields a blob whose `type` is `"image/png"` at byte-identical size, with no throw and no null. Naming the file from the REQUEST would therefore produce a `.webp` holding PNG bytes, which is worse than never trying, because that path is what the agent `Read`s. Hence the one rule: the name follows the content. **The quality dial belongs before the resolution knob.** `shotEncode`'s halve-and-retry existed only because PNG has no quality parameter, and dropping 640px → 320px → 160px destroys exactly the legibility a picture is sent for; with a real WebP encoder every quality step is spent at full size first. Both formats are tried at each size and the first that FITS wins, because lossy WebP usually beats PNG but PNG genuinely wins on flat UI. **Rejected: JPEG.** Measured on this very UI it LOST to PNG — 13477 → 13945 bytes on a card, 18173 → 91579 on a canvas raster — because flat colour is PNG's strong case. **Stated plainly: a large share of real usage gets no benefit.** The desktop app is WKWebView, so those users get exactly today's PNG behaviour; Chrome/Edge/Firefox users get the smaller file. This is cheap and correct, not transformative. |
| D210 | **Escape in `claude_split` resolves to the least destructive undo available, and is bound on the framed app's document as well as the page's own** | `escapeAction(composerOpen, annotating, runId)` returns `close-composer` → `exit-annotate` → `stop-run` → `""`, and the single handler `onEscape` is registered on BOTH `document` and, on every frame load, the app's `doc` (beside the existing `mousedown`/`mousemove`/`click` handlers). Leaving annotate mode goes through `annSetMode(false)`, the one function that owns the mode. | **The control promises "Esc or click to stop" and nothing implemented it.** `escapeAction` knew only the composer and a live run, so an armed pane with no popover and no run returned `""` and the key did nothing. **Ordering is by cost of being wrong:** dismissing a popover is repeatable, leaving annotate mode is one click to undo, ending a turn loses the turn — so an Escape pressed while annotating must not reach the run killer, even when a run is live. **Two documents, because a keydown does not cross a frame boundary.** Annotate mode is driven with the pointer over the iframe, so that is where the key lands, and binding only the parent is precisely why Escape looked broken while annotating — the same boundary that already forced `mousedown`/`mousemove`/`click` onto `doc`. Focus is therefore covered in both places it actually sits while annotating: the control button (page document) and the app (frame document). **`annSetMode` extracted rather than duplicated:** a second copy of the toggle is a second chance for the control's label to disagree with `annOn`. Accepted limit: an Escape pressed with focus in the surrounding shell chrome reaches neither document, which is correct — Escape belongs to the pane that has focus. Verified by synthetic dispatch on the frame's document (`cmux browser press` injects into the top document only and cannot route into a subframe), which is the wiring that was wrong. |
| D211 | **The annotate control goes back to a small floating icon — 26px, unlabelled, pushed OUTSIDE the pane — after a strip and then a full-width banner were both tried and both cost too much** | `#lefttools` is deleted; `#annbtn` is `position: absolute; top: 10px; right: -40px` inside `#leftview`, a 26px circle holding one inline-SVG comment bubble. `-30px` = its own 26px plus the 4px divider, so it starts just past the divider in the chat pane's left margin and overlaps the app by zero pixels. Accent-tinted (icon + ring on `--panel`) while idle, accent-FILLED (`--on-accent` icon) while armed. The icon is an inline SVG stroked in `currentColor`, like the composer's pane-shot icon. No label in either state; the instruction and both exits live in the `title`. | **Every version of this control trades occlusion against layout, and the earlier two paid too much.** The original labelled pill floated on the app's top-right corner and GREW into a sentence when armed, covering more of the app exactly when the user was aiming at it. The strip (and then the banner) fixed occlusion by converting it into layout — but that charged ~28px of app height plus a line of chrome to **every** project, including the ones that never annotate, which is the wrong default for a feature used occasionally. The negative offset escapes the trade entirely: outside the pane there is nothing to occlude and no height to spend. Nothing clips it — neither `#left` nor `#leftview` sets `overflow`, and `#chat`'s `overflow: hidden` cannot clip a non-descendant — and the space is guaranteed by `#chat`'s `min-width: 340px` with a layout that never stacks vertically. **Accent while IDLE, not just when armed:** a 26px unlabelled glyph parked outside the pane is easy to miss altogether, so being noticed is the one job the idle state has. That makes both states accent, so the invariant that keeps them apart is asserted: idle tints, armed fills. **Size and position identical in both states** — the armed rule may only repaint. A control that grew when armed would cover more at the worst moment, and as an overlay it cannot move the iframe's box at all, so pins and `annStageRect` (frame viewport coordinates) are untouched by toggling; the strip needed a same-height rule to get the same property. **Still a real `<button>` inside `#leftview`, a SIBLING of the iframe:** Enter/Space and `aria-pressed` come free, `aria-label` names an icon with no text, and living in the parent document keeps it structurally unreachable by `shotPane` (which rasterises the framed document), so it can never appear in a capture. **A comment bubble, not a pencil:** a pencil means "edit this", which is what CLAUDE does to the app — this control lets the user leave a note ON it, and what it produces are numbered comment pins. Drawn as an inline SVG rather than a text glyph for the same reason the pane-shot pill was converted: only `currentColor` can follow the accent tint AND invert to `--on-accent` when armed, and a glyph renders at a different weight on every platform. Accepted cost: the icon sits in the chat pane's margin, so it reads as furniture on the seam rather than as part of either pane, and it is 26px of the chat pane's background. |
| D216 | **The canonical skills are also assembled into a Claude Code PLUGIN root under `home_dir()/skill-plugin/` (`skill_plugin.py`), and every chat fused-render spawns is handed it with `claude --plugin-dir <root>` — D185's user-level sync stays, demoted to convenience for sessions we did not launch** (superseded in part by D228: the `claude --help` capability probe described below is deleted and the flag is passed unconditionally — it blocked the pre-bind path long enough for the desktop supervisor to kill the server) | D185 delivered the skills by writing into `<CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR or ~/.claude>/skills/`, which is a *guess about the machine*, and every way it can miss is silent: the dir we resolved may not be the one the CLI reads (the user set `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` after startup), marker ownership correctly makes us skip a same-named user-authored skill, the user can delete one mid-session, and on a fresh install nothing orders the sync before the first session. In each case the session starts *without* `fused-render-authoring` and says nothing — the model then writes an app against a `fused` bridge it is guessing at, which is the failure this skill exists to prevent. The sessions in question are ones WE spawn, so the guess is removable: `--plugin-dir` loads a directory plugin for that session only, from a path we wrote moments earlier, additive to and independent of the user's own skills/plugins/settings/CLAUDE.md. Rejected: `--plugin-url` / telling the model to fetch the skill from GitHub (needs network, against the no-network-at-runtime stance, and tracks `main` rather than the installed runtime the skill documents — the same reasoning D185 recorded), and asking the user to `claude plugin install fused-render@fused-render` (opt-in, same version skew; the published marketplace entry stays, for people who want the skills in their own sessions). Shape: the loader wants `<root>/.claude-plugin/plugin.json` + `<root>/skills/<name>/`, so the repo root already IS a plugin root (that is what makes the marketplace entry work) — but the end user almost never has the repo, so `sync_skill_plugin()` reassembles the same tree from whatever the install shipped. Source resolution is D106's, same order as `user_skills.py`: repo `skills/` + `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` first (editable installs), else the packaged copies under `fused_render/skills/`. **The packaged manifest is a FLAT `fused_render/skills/plugin.json`, not a packaged `.claude-plugin/` dir**: nothing in the wheel may live under a dot-prefixed path, because whether a hidden path survives the build backend's `artifacts` globs is precisely the kind of thing that fails in a built wheel on a user's machine and nowhere else — the dotted dir is mkdir'd in the *output*, where we control it. Sync is stamped (a fingerprint of source paths/sizes/mtimes beside the root, not inside it) so the common case — every server start — rewrites nothing; a change deletes and renames the staged tree in, because there is no atomic directory swap and a merge would let a deleted skill linger. Staging is a `mkdtemp` beside the root, not a fixed `<root>.new`: `export_skill_plugin_env` is called from the create-app and create-template routes, which FastAPI runs on a threadpool, so a shared name let two concurrent scaffolds each delete the other's half-copied tree and publish whichever fragment won (caught in review). `_is_loadable` takes the skills that must ALSO be present, and the stamp gate passes them — the manifest alone is not evidence of a complete tree, and treating it as such let a gutted root be blessed by a matching stamp and served to every session until the sources next changed, which is the silent-failure mode this whole decision exists to remove. The failure paths deliberately check the manifest only: there a partial tree competes with no tree at all. Best-effort throughout, returning a stale-but-loadable root over none. Whether to pass the flag at all is decided ONCE, server-side, in `export_skill_plugin_env()` (called from `server.export_app_env`, beside the other `FUSED_RENDER_*` exports): it syncs, probes the local `claude --help` for `--plugin-dir` (probe deleted by D228), and publishes the root as `FUSED_RENDER_SKILL_PLUGIN_DIR` — or clears the var. BOTH claude templates (`claude/agent.py` and its `claude_split/` fork) then just read `appenv.skill_plugin_dir()` and append the flag if it is set; they may not import the package (SPEC PY-15) and they deliberately do not re-derive the path from `home_dir()` either, because `home_dir()` branch-nests and only the server knows the resolved answer. The first build DID probe inside `_start` and that was wrong twice over: `subprocess.run` goes through the `Popen` that a dozen existing tests fake (12 failures, all real), and in production it added a subprocess to every turn — `test_the_spawn_path_never_shells_out_to_the_cli` is the scar. The probe fails CLOSED (an unknown option makes the CLI exit before the turn starts, so an older install loses the skills rather than every chat) with one deliberate exception: a `claude` the server cannot FIND still gets the flag, because "not on the server's PATH" is the normal state on Windows — the templates search install locations the server does not — and reading that as unsupported would withhold the skills from exactly the users least likely to notice. The system prompts are deliberately untouched: the starter `CLAUDE.md`s already name the skills, and `_split_system_prompt`'s one job stays naming the app-state tool. Tests: `test_skill_plugin.py` — assembled shape, stamp short-circuit, stale file eviction, packaged fallback, missing-manifest synthesis, no-raise on failure, the no-dotted-path packaging invariant, and the seams pinned (the `SKILLS` tuple and source roots against `user_skills.py`; both templates reading only the appenv helper, with no second spelling of the subdir name); plus the export half in `test_template_appenv.py`. |

| D217 | **The pushed app-state DOM outline travels as a FILE PATH (`dom_path`), not as bytes in the message** | `appStateBlock` stringified the whole outline into the outgoing message, and the message is what the CLI records in its own session transcript — so an N-message conversation carried N full DOM trees in context permanently, re-read on every later turn, and all but the newest already stale. The block's own preamble says it goes stale the moment anything is edited and that `app_state` is the fresh-read channel, which makes the retained copies pure cost. The outline is also by far the largest thing the page sends (deeply repetitive `nth-of-type` ancestor chains, which tokenize badly). Screenshots already solved this exact problem the same way: capture, `fused.uploadFile`, send the path. Rejected: keeping a small inline digest (title/entry/landmarks) beside the path — the hybrid preserves zero-latency awareness and was the recommendation, but the call was made for the pure path, accepting that the model must spend a `Read` before it knows the app's structure and may sometimes act without looking. Also rejected: shortening `path` to be parent-relative, which would halve the blob even inline but changes the anchorPath contract the annotations share. The file goes in the SHOTS directory rather than a new one: same class of artifact under the same privacy argument (a private, short-lived record of what was on the user's screen), and that directory is already 0700-enforced, already TTL+count pruned, and already the one path `--allowed-tools` lets `Read` touch without raising a card — a second directory would need its own copy of all three, and `_shots_dir`'s hardening is ~80 lines of security-critical code not worth duplicating. The write happens in the send path, NOT inside `composeOutgoing`, which is sync and pure because it must stay the exact inverse of `stripBlocks`. Falls back to the inline outline on any failure (no directory, failed write) rather than dropping it: degrading must not leave the agent knowing *less* about the screen than before this existed, and the preamble is built from whichever shape actually arrived so it never promises a `dom_path` that is not there. Names carry a sequence number as well as `Date.now()` — two sends in one millisecond would otherwise overwrite one another's outline while the first was still being read. The tag is unchanged, so both strippers (`_strip_app_state` and the page's `stripAppStateBlock`) keep working untouched. The PULL channel (`app_state` tool) still answers inline: the model asked for it, once, and the answer is fresh. |
| D218 | **The split view shows Skill invocations as log rows and live API retries on the status line** | `_poll` only ever read `system`, `stream_event` and `result` rows, so two things the CLI already reports were discarded. (a) A skill silently changes how a reply was produced — without a row in the log the only visible difference between a session that loaded `fused-render-authoring` and one that did not is that the answers are better, which is exactly the kind of thing that makes D216 unverifiable in the field. Read off the FINALIZED `assistant` row, whose `tool_use` block carries `input.skill` whole; the streamed `content_block_start` for the same call arrives with `input: {}` and the name only as `input_json_delta` fragments needing reassembly, so reading one row and not the other is also what stops a call being counted twice. Keyed on the `tool_use` id because `_poll` replays the entire file every 400 ms — the same reason `notedStates` exists for app-state reads. (b) An overloaded API was indistinguishable from a hang: the status line sat on "Thinking…" with a frozen token count for the whole backoff, which is how a 529 looked to a user for the entire life of this template. `system`/`api_retry` rows carry `attempt`, `max_retries`, `retry_delay_ms` and `error_status`. The live state is deliberately TRANSIENT — anything the model produces after a retry ends it, since rows are in file order — because a banner left up for the rest of the turn is a lie for far longer than it was true; the tally is kept, because "retried four times" is what makes a final failure explainable. Shown on the status line and not as one note per retry: ten attempts would mean ten log rows. 529 and 429 travel as the status rather than a baked-in message (the API is swamped and waiting helps, versus this account is being throttled — calling one the other sends the user to the wrong place), and `max_retries` is read from the row rather than assumed, since the budget is the CLI's. Terminal errors are rewritten in `_poll` rather than in `runEnding`, which is pure and pinned against the claude template's copy so the two keep deciding identically; it never string-sniffs the CLI's error text and keeps the original in parentheses as the string a bug report can be matched on. **The rewrite keys off the retry still IN FLIGHT when the run ended, not the tally.** Keying it off the tally was a bug (caught in review): the tally survives a mid-turn retry that succeeded, so a later unrelated failure — a crashed tool, a bad edit, an auth error — was presented as an API overload with the real cause buried.
