Because a `result` row clears the live retry like any other post-retry row, the cleared value is held for one row and captured only when that `result` is an error; the abnormal-exit path (killed mid-backoff, no `result` row) reads the still-live retry instead. The scar is `test_a_recovered_retry_does_not_explain_a_later_unrelated_failure` — the adjacent case to one the original tests did cover ("retried, recovered, no error") and which they missed. |

### macOS bundle — the interpreter locates itself, and a ready marker is a claim (2026-08-04)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D212 | **The .app gains one relative symlink, `Contents/lib -> Resources/lib`, so the bundled interpreter self-locates with NO environment variables — and `envinstall.is_installed()` stops treating `.openfused-ready` as proof, verifying it once per venv per process and DELETING it when the venv's own python cannot run** | Reported symptom: on a DMG install, every HTML page whose `.py` carries a PEP 723 `# /// script` header failed permanently with "python execution" errors, and the only workaround was flipping the engine pref from `fused` to `local` (D204 having just made `fused` the default, so this was the out-of-the-box experience). Two linked causes. **(1) The interpreter could not self-locate.** py2app puts the runtime (stdlib zip + `lib/python3.12/`) under `Contents/Resources/lib` but the interpreter at `Contents/MacOS/python`, so CPython's landmark search — `<prefix>/lib/python3.12/…` relative to the executable, i.e. `Contents/lib/python3.12` — misses and falls back to the prefix compiled in at build time: the BUILD MACHINE's Homebrew framework (`/opt/homebrew/opt/python@3.12/Frameworks/…`), measured on a real installed bundle with the env stripped. The app worked anyway because py2app's launcher stub exports `PYTHONHOME=…/Contents/Resources`; what did not work is everything downstream, because `fused`'s `python_compute` STRIPS `PYTHONHOME`/`PYTHONPATH`/`VIRTUAL_ENV`/`PYTHONSTARTUP` from its children. So a `uv venv --python Contents/MacOS/python` (how the install loader builds a header's venv — PY-18, D173, D176) recorded that nonexistent Homebrew path as its base prefix, and every child of that venv died (`ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'pandas'`, encodings failures). `ln -s Resources/lib Contents/lib` fixes it outright: verified on a copy of an installed bundle, `sys.prefix` becomes `<App>/Contents`, `import fused_render, duckdb, rasterio` works with `PYTHONHOME`/`PYTHONPATH` unset, and a `uv venv` built from that interpreter records a base prefix INSIDE the app and imports a freshly installed package with the env stripped. Zero per-request cost — no probe, no wrapper, no extra subprocess; the link is resolved by the interpreter's own startup path search. Placed after the prune/staging steps that rewrite `Contents/Resources/lib/python3.12` and before both the smokes and the codesign sweep, so it is sealed into the signature; both magic-byte sweeps use `find <dir> -type f`, which does not follow symlinks without `-L`, so the link is neither signed as nested code nor traversed twice, and a re-signed bundle carrying it passes `codesign --verify --strict` ("valid on disk / satisfies its Designated Requirement"). Relative, never absolute — the .app is copied to /Applications, mounted from a DMG, and run from CI temp dirs. **(2) A bad venv was permanent.** "Installed" was solely `os.path.exists(<venv>/.openfused-ready)`, and upstream's `ensure_requirements_venv`/`ensure_bare_venv` short-circuit on `if marker.exists(): return` — a rebuild happens only when the marker is ABSENT. The venv cache key is `sha256("<python path>|<version>|reqs:…")`, and inside the .app both the path and the version are constants, so an app upgrade does not change the key: nothing revalidated, and there was no repair action in the UI. So the marker is now the **index** of installed venvs (its absence is still final, and still a single stat — the common case) but a **claim** that is verified once: one `<venv>/bin/python -c ""` with the env `python_compute` gives children, borrowed from `engine._child_env()` so the probe cannot drift from the real child env. Memoized per venv directory per process (`_VALIDATED`) — `/api/run`'s pre-flight calls `is_installed` on every run of every header script, so a probe per request would put a subprocess on the hottest path. **Because the probe is a subprocess, two placement rules follow, and both were got wrong first (found on review — the second bug in this row's history caused by copying `app_interpreter`'s shape without copying its reasoning):** (i) the pre-flight runs `await asyncio.to_thread(envinstall.is_installed, …)`, never inline — `routers/run.py` awaits `run_python` directly, so an inline `subprocess.run(..., timeout=5)` stalls the whole server (websockets, the file watcher, every other request) for the full budget, which is exactly why the header-LESS branch of the same `if` already does `await asyncio.to_thread(app_interpreter)`. `to_thread` re-raises in the awaiting frame so the existing `except (ImportError, RuntimeError)` containment is unchanged, pinned by a test rather than assumed. (ii) `_venv_is_usable` is **double-checked**: read the cache under the lock, probe OUTSIDE it, re-acquire and `setdefault`. Holding the module-global lock across the probe (which it first did, copying `engine._app_interpreter_lock`) serialized the first probes of UNRELATED venvs, so one venv on a wedged mount blocked every `is_installed` in the process for up to 5s — cached hits for healthy venvs included, and combined with (i) that froze the entire app on one stuck venv. The accepted cost is that two threads racing on the same venv may both probe: it is a read-only `-c ""`, so a duplicate is one extra ~15ms spawn in a rare race, and `setdefault` makes the racers agree on one verdict. Rejected per-`venv_dir` locks — they need their own eviction story for a benefit the duplicate-probe trade already buys. (iii) Because the probe is outside the lock, the venv can be DESTROYED mid-probe, so a **generation** is read before the probe and re-checked after — `_generation_of` = (`_EPOCH`, `_GENERATION[venv_dir]`), bumped by `_discard_verdict`, which is now the only way an entry leaves `_VALIDATED` so that "the verdict is gone" and "the generation moved" cannot disagree. The invariant is absolute and there is no tolerated staleness window: **a verdict is only ever cached against the generation it judged**; if it moved, the prober returns its own measurement to its own caller and stores nothing, and the next call re-probes. Recorded as an invariant rather than a mitigation deliberately: the first cut instead let `_REBUILD_ATTEMPTED` absorb a stale verdict's cost, which quietly made a tunable repair *policy* double as the staleness backstop — so a later tuning of the budget would resurface staleness far from the change that caused it. The `_EPOCH` half exists because a per-directory counter cannot express "invalidate everything": a probe on its FIRST look at a venv has no entry to bump, and that is most in-flight probes, so `reset_venv_validation_cache()` bumps the epoch instead of only clearing the dicts — otherwise the reset is undone by exactly the calls it means to invalidate. The steady-state ceiling is untouched (no discards means no generation changes), pinned by the 8x20-thread test. On failure the marker is **deleted before returning False**, which is the load-bearing subtlety: without it the pre-flight answers `needs_install`, the worker calls upstream, upstream sees the marker and returns immediately without rebuilding, and the loader loops forever against an unchanged `fused`. The cached verdict is dropped alongside the marker (and again whenever the marker is found absent), so a rebuilt venv is judged on its own merits instead of inheriting a permanently negative answer — the mirror image of the bug being fixed. This half is also what HEALS users who are broken today: their venv key does not change when they upgrade, so the symlink alone would leave them sitting on the bad venv. **Two contracts bound what the validation costs when it is wrong (both added on review, both pinned by tests, and neither is defensive padding a future reader should "simplify" away).** (a) **The probe is THREE-valued.** `_venv_runs` answers True (completed, exit 0), False (DEFINITE evidence: completed with a non-zero exit, or the spawn failed on the exe itself — `FileNotFoundError`/`PermissionError`/`NotADirectoryError`), or None (INCONCLUSIVE — a `TimeoutExpired`, or any other `OSError`: `EAGAIN` when the server cannot fork under load, `EMFILE` when it is out of descriptors, `EINTR`). Only False reaches the deletion. The two-valued first cut was a live hazard, not a hypothetical: `TimeoutExpired` IS a `SubprocessError`, so with a 5s budget a few concurrent `/api/run` calls on a loaded machine were enough to unlink the marker of a perfectly healthy venv and charge the user a full uv re-download. `None` is cached nowhere (it says nothing worth remembering, and caching it would make one unlucky fork permanent), touches no state, and answers **True** so the run proceeds and reports whatever really happens — the policy `engine._probe` already had, which reports a failed probe and destroys nothing. The inconclusive log line is worded distinctly from the corruption one so an operator can tell "state was destroyed, a rebuild is coming" from "we never got an answer". (b) **The repair is attempted at most ONCE per venv dir per process** (`_REBUILD_ATTEMPTED`, under the same lock). `runtime.js`'s `installed` flag bounds the retry WITHIN one `runPython` call, but nothing bounded it across calls, and the state that would have — the marker and the memo — is exactly what the repair destroys. For the cohort this decision exists for the rebuild is guaranteed futile: the property that failed is the bundled interpreter's own base prefix, invariant under rebuild, and the key folds in only that interpreter's path and version. So unbounded, every page reload, every LR-2 `watchPath` auto-reload and every param change would pay another multi-hundred-MB download, where before D212 that user got one instant permanent error — a straight regression. The second and later definite failures therefore leave the marker in place and report **installed**, so the script runs and the user sees the interpreter's own stderr, which is the truthful outcome. Per PROCESS deliberately: installing a fixed DMG means a fresh server, an empty set, and one rebuild that works. The bound is NOT cleared on the marker-absent branch (the marker is absent by construction right after we unlink it, so clearing it there would mean it could never engage), and `reset_venv_validation_cache()` clears all three sets together — resetting only the verdicts would leave a bound that silently suppresses the repair the re-probe just asked for, and leaving `_BOUND_LOGGED` would silence a bound that can engage again. **One rule governs every branch that acts on a verdict, and it is stated once rather than per-branch: a verdict may only be acted on while the generation it judged is still current, and a "ready" answer requires the marker to still be there at the moment we answer.** `_GENERATION`/`_EPOCH` enforce the first half for the *cache*; `_marker_stamp` — `(st_ino, st_mtime_ns)`, captured before the probe and re-checked under the lock, named to match openfused's copy so the two read alike — extends it to the marker on disk, and both mechanisms are necessary because of an asymmetry: the counters only know what THIS process did, while the install worker is another PROCESS that can un-mark, rebuild and re-mark without touching them. Identity, not existence, for exactly that reason — the un-mark-and-re-mark case leaves a marker present the whole time this side can observe, so `os.path.exists` sees nothing at all. Three concrete failures this closes, all found on review and all pinned by tests: (1) "stat the marker -> probe -> decide -> unlink" is not atomic between THREADS (the endpoints run in FastAPI's threadpool), so A could record the attempt and unlink while B, which stamped before A's unlink, arrived after A's add, read `already_tried`, announced a rebuild that had not happened and returned True — running a venv known to be broken instead of joining A's install; (2) a *replaced* marker (the worker finishing a rebuild mid-probe) would let the definite-failure branch unlink the marker of a freshly rebuilt, possibly healthy venv and force another multi-hundred-MB download — the same high-severity shape Bugbot found in openfused; (3) a `True` verdict answered after the marker had VANISHED would hand `/api/run` a directory being rebuilt underneath it, a confusing mid-rebuild failure instead of the loader. All three resolve identically — discard the verdict, answer not-installed, let the caller join the install already in flight (`start()` joins rather than duplicating) — so they share one gate rather than three copies of an identity comparison. The INCONCLUSIVE branch is deliberately NOT gated: it answers from no verdict at all, and "I learned nothing" is true whatever the marker did. Deliberately no cross-process lock: this module's only mutual exclusion is `threading.Lock`, a file lock would need a stale-lock policy, and the thread-level race is what needs atomicity; the stat under the lock is the same syscall the function already makes unguarded on entry, so if that one returned the filesystem was answering microseconds ago. And the bound's warning is emitted **once per venv per process** (`_BOUND_LOGGED`, warning on the transition and `logger.debug` after), because past that point every call answers from the cached verdict — every page reload, every `watchPath` auto-reload, every param change — and repeating a multi-line warning per request buries the one occurrence that matters in the log of exactly the incident this code exists to diagnose | The build-time assertion is the real lesson and is why this is recorded rather than just fixed: the property was invisible because **every** existing smoke in `build_dmg.sh` ran the bundled python WITH `PYTHONHOME=Contents/Resources` set, and a bundle whose landmark search resolves to a Homebrew prefix passes all of them. The new smoke runs it with `env -u PYTHONHOME -u PYTHONPATH -u VIRTUAL_ENV` and FATALs unless `sys.prefix` is inside `$APP_DIR` *and* `import fused_render, duckdb` succeeds — the prefix check alone would accept a right-looking prefix over an incomplete runtime, and the import alone would pass on a machine that happens to have the build interpreter installed (i.e. every dev machine, which is the whole reason this reached users). **Rejected:** removing py2app's launcher-stub `PYTHONHOME` export — it is not ours to remove (the stub sets it), the app keeps working with it, and it is now merely redundant rather than harmful. **Rejected:** deleting `engine.app_interpreter()`'s `_wrapper_interpreter` machinery (D172/D175), which exists to give a header-LESS script a `PYTHONHOME`-restoring wrapper when the raw interpreter fails its probe. On a correctly built bundle the probe now succeeds and the wrapper is never generated, so the machinery is self-disabling; it stays because it also covers a bundle built before this change and any future interpreter that genuinely needs an env, and its cost when unneeded is zero. **Rejected:** revalidating from a persisted record (a stamped build id, a version file beside the venv) instead of a probe — it would need its own invalidation story to avoid becoming exactly the stale-forever fact the marker already was, and it cannot see the failure mode that actually occurred (a base prefix that vanished for reasons having nothing to do with our version). **Rejected:** validating by importing the requirements rather than `-c ""` — that is upstream's job, costs a real import, and the property that broke is more basic than any import: the interpreter never reached the point of executing a program. **Left alone knowingly:** `engine._needs_install_dict` still says the script "declares dependencies that are not installed yet", which is misleading in the one call where the real cause is a dead interpreter. A truthful message needs a *reason* plumbed out of `is_installed` — whose contract is a bool — into the wire dict, i.e. new API plus per-venv reason state, which is the cross-cutting refactor this change is not. It also self-corrects within one user action now that the rebuild is bounded: the misleading message appears once, and the very next call runs the script and shows the interpreter's own stderr |

### install progress — liveness instead of a fake percentage (2026-08-04)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D213 | **The install record gets a ~2s heartbeat that refreshes elapsed time and `ts` while `pct` STAYS at 25, the client renders that stage as an indeterminate bar, and a joined install is never painted as 0% — with the worker's terminal write latched so a late beat can never put `done: false` back on the wire** | Reported symptom: the dependency-install splash "sits stuck for a long time" when switching between apps. Not a perception problem — the UI had no mechanism by which it *could* move. `_env_install_worker.install()` wrote exactly three records (`create` 10, `install` 25, `done` 100) and did the entire download inside one `ensure_requirements_venv` call, which runs uv behind `capture_output=True`; so for the only slow phase — minutes on a cold cache with imagecodecs/pyproj — nothing was emitted at all, while the client polled every 500ms and repainted an identical record. Three changes. **(1) A heartbeat** (`_HEARTBEAT_S = 2`) re-writes the `install` record with elapsed time appended (`downloading and installing 3 package(s): pandas, pyproj, s3fs (2m14s)`) and a fresh `ts`, which is the only evidence of liveness that reaches the wire. **`pct` deliberately does not move, and neither does the stage** — the obvious "fix" a future maintainer will reach for is animating the number upward, and it must not be done: there is genuinely nothing to measure behind captured output, the percentage is the one thing a waiting user trusts as fact, and a bar that creeps to 90% and then sits there is a worse lie than one that admits it does not know. **(2) An indeterminate bar** in `runtime.js` for `stage === "install"`: a narrow travelling fill (`bar.dataset.indeterminate = "1"` plus one `<style>` injected on first use and found by id afterwards — inline styles cannot express keyframes, and a per-install copy would pile up in `head`), switching back to a real width for any stage that carries a genuine value so a finished install stops animating. **(3) `showInstall` no longer asserts `0%`.** `start()` JOINS an install already in flight rather than duplicating it, so nothing was ever lost — but re-opening a page four minutes into a download painted 0% and then jumped to 25% on the first poll, and a user switching back and forth saw 0% → 25% → freeze repeatedly and concluded it was looping. The initial state now claims no percentage at all and the first real paint comes from the server's own record, which the `/api/env/install` response already carries. `paint` moved to a module-scope `paintInstall(ui, prog, notice)` so the stage-to-bar rule has one definition and can be driven directly under node | **The ordering is the whole risk, and it is enforced rather than asserted (the D181 rule).** The heartbeat thread and the terminal `done`/`error` write both `os.replace` onto `progress.json`; a beat landing afterwards puts `done: false` back on the wire and the client then polls a finished install forever — the reported symptom, made permanent. Three layers, only the last of which is a guarantee: `Event.wait(_HEARTBEAT_S)` rather than `sleep` so shutdown is immediate; `stop.set()` + `join` in a `finally`, which runs as an exception propagates and therefore precedes the `except`'s error write as well as the success write; and — because `join` takes a timeout and a beat can be parked inside its own `_write` on a slow filesystem — one lock plus a latch inside `install`, so once a terminal record exists nothing may write again. The latch closes AFTER `_write` returns, never before: a terminal write that fails (a full disk) must not shut the file, or the `except` path's error record — the only one carrying the reason — no-ops and `done: false` stays on the wire forever, which is this decision's own symptom reached from the other side. The lock makes write-then-latch atomic, so no beat can slip between them. The heartbeat thread is a daemon: one wedged in a write must never keep the worker process alive after its record says done, or `_pid_alive` reads the installer as still running and the page polls a corpse. `_write`'s temp name gains the thread id (matching `envinstall._write`): pid alone stopped being unique the moment a second writer lived in this process, and a shared temp name means the first `os.replace` consumes the other's file and the loser dies — a crashed installer whose venv was actually fine. The module stays stdlib + `fused` only (D152), so `threading` not anything of ours. **Explicitly out of scope, both deliberate:** real per-package percentages, which would require upstream's `venvs._run_step` to stop capturing output — a `fused` change, not ours; and a cross-key concurrency cap, even though switching apps really does start N detached workers competing for bandwidth (each distinct requirement set is its own key and its own worker, with no cap), because a cap needs a queue, a fairness rule and a cancellation story of its own. **Testing gap, stated rather than papered over:** the indeterminate bar's DOM contract is asserted under node (`dataset.indeterminate` set for `install`, cleared for a real percentage; the keyframes injected exactly once; a joined mid-flight record painted with no 0% frame — via a recording setter on the memoised bar's `style.width`), but no headless test can tell whether the animation LOOKS right, and this repo has been burned by exactly that before (see the CM line-margin note in the memory index). It needs a human glance |
| D214 | **Script venvs are pinned to Python 3.12: the server's own interpreter when it is already 3.12, else a uv-MANAGED 3.12 — downloaded first, as a visible `python` progress stage, under its own progress key. `scripts/dev.sh` pins its `.venv` to 3.12 and rebuilds one that is not** | Reported symptom: a `# /// script` app declaring `tensorflow` failed permanently with uv's "no wheels with a matching Python ABI tag (e.g. `cp314`)", and the user worked around it by switching the engine off `fused` entirely. Cause: nothing ever set the backend's `python_executable`, so `_python_executable()` returned None, upstream resolved that to `sys.executable`, and the base interpreter for every script venv was an accident of how the server had been launched — a `.venv` that a bare `uv venv` had built on uv's newest known CPython. The error named the real cause and no rebuild could repair it, because the missing wheels do not exist. 3.12 rather than 3.13 because all three installers already ship it (`python@3.12` in build_dmg.sh, `uv python install 3.12` in the Linux and Windows scripts), so the pin needs NO packaging change and both run paths — script venvs and the header-less app interpreter — land on one version instead of two | **Preferring our own 3.12 over a managed one is what makes this cheap, and is not a shortcut.** `uv python find --managed-python` matches only uv's own registry, and every bundled 3.12 is copied into the payload rather than registered there — so forcing a managed interpreter would have made every packaged user download a second CPython to reach a version they already had, AND re-keyed every venv they own, since `python_identity` folds the interpreter path into the key. Resolving to None instead is byte-identical to the pre-D214 value, so their keys do not move. Rejected `--prerelease=allow`, which was the other candidate fix: it is not "only when nothing else resolves" (that is `if-necessary`) but "consider prereleases for every package", and since uv prefers the highest compatible version it would resolve tensorflow 2.21.0rc1 over stable 2.20.0 — shipping release-candidate scientific libraries to every user of every app to work around an interpreter choice. The interpreter download reports under a fixed, key-SHAPED `PYTHON_BOOTSTRAP_KEY` because this is the one state where a venv key cannot be computed: the key folds in the base interpreter, and that is precisely what is missing, so any key derived here would name a venv nobody will build. For the same reason the worker acquires and STOPS, and the client's loop guard becomes the SET of keys installed rather than a boolean — two rounds are now legitimate. A not-ready resolution is deliberately never cached, since the download lands in another process that cannot notify us. **Accepted costs:** a source checkout moving off a non-3.12 interpreter re-keys its venvs once and the orphans are never collected (there is no GC), and a `uv python upgrade` of the managed 3.12 re-keys again because the patch version is in `python_identity` and the managed path is not — upstream owns that function. A machine with no uv degrades to the old unpinned behaviour rather than refusing to serve: it can honour no pin, and a working server beats a correct one nobody can start |
| D215 | **The generated wrapper defines `__file__` (the script's real absolute path) and `__name__` (`"__fused_module__"`) in the exec globals, so a page's `.py` gets the same module globals under either engine** | Reported symptom: a working local-tools app failed on first paint with `NameError: name '__file__' is not defined` at its own module level — from `CATALOG_PATH = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)), "settings_catalog.json")`, i.e. the ordinary way to find a data file sitting next to your script. The built-in executor's worker loads the file with `spec_from_file_location("__fused_module__", path)`, so CPython's import machinery defines both dunders; `engine.build_code`'s preamble `exec(compile(src, abs_path, 'exec'), globals())` defines neither. The trap is that it *looks* like it does: the real path was already being passed as `compile()`'s filename, which is why tracebacks correctly named the user's file and line — but that argument only LABELS code objects, it does not bind the name `__file__`. So the two engines handed user code different globals, and the difference was invisible in every error message the failure produced. `__name__` is set in the same breath, for parity rather than a reported failure: it was inherited from the backend's runner namespace and arrived as `"builtins"`, which no script can sensibly branch on and which differs from the other engine. Fixed in fused-render alone — `fused` receives a STRING of code and has no path to point `__file__` at, so it is correct for its runner not to set one; the path is known only here | **The cost of not fixing this at the source was already visible in the tree and had been paid twenty times.** Fifteen templates — `bundle/reader.py`, `docs/docs.py`, `claude/agent.py`, `claude_split/agent.py`, `markdown/graph.py`, `latex/engine.py`, `excel/reader.py`, `history/history.py`, `annotate/annotate.py`, `slides/slides.py`, `pano/pano.py`, `usd/reader.py`, `map/map_render.py`, `zarr_aoi/tile_server.py` and `git/log.py` — each carry a hand-written `if "__file__" not in globals(): __file__ = os.path.join(sys.path[0], "<name>.py")` — several with a copy of the same three-line comment explaining the engine's behaviour; five more (`geotiff/tile_server.py`, `geotiff/_tiff_core.py`, `netcdf/grid_tile_server.py`, `netcdf/_zarr_core.py`, `notebook/kernel.py`) instead branch on `if "__file__" in globals()` at the point of use and fall back to `sys.path[0]`, which now simply always takes the first arm. Shipped templates were therefore immunised one at a time while every user-authored page rediscovered the defect from scratch, which is the asymmetry that makes this a source bug rather than a template idiom. Those guards are left in place: they are `not in globals()` tests, so after this change they never fire, and deleting twenty files' worth of dead branches is a separate mechanical cleanup, not something to bury in this PR (their comments are now stale, and that is the flagged cost of deferring it). The reconstructed value was already identical to the real one — `sys.path[0]` is the `script_dir` this same preamble inserts — so nothing changes behaviour for them. **The parity is pinned behaviourally, not structurally:** two rows added to `test_engine_parity.py`'s table, which runs every case through the built-in worker AND the fused wrapper AND the real backend and compares, so a future rewrite of either preamble cannot drop a dunder silently. Asserted on basenames because each engine writes its target under its own tmp subdir. **`__name__` was the one judgement call, and it is safe for a specific measured reason:** neither engine makes it `"__main__"` (`"__fused_module__"` vs the old `"builtins"`), so no `if __name__ == "__main__":` block that was dormant starts running — which matters because `geotiff/tile_server.py`, `latex/install_worker.py` and both `permission_server.py` files use exactly that guard for the SUBPROCESS they spawn of themselves, where `__name__` really is `"__main__"`. A test pins that a script's `__main__` block stays dormant, so the property is enforced rather than reasoned about. **Not extended further:** `spec_from_file_location` also sets `__spec__`, `__loader__` and `__package__`, which are left undefined — `__package__` would only matter for relative imports, which fail under both engines anyway, and inventing a fake spec/loader would make introspection lie about how the module was loaded rather than close a real gap |

### Google Drive mounts — in-app sign-in (2026-08-04)

| # | Decision | Choice | Rationale / rejected alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| D219 | **Drive sign-in is handled in-app by spawning `rclone authorize "drive"` and creating the remote over rcd — the same child-process-plus-poll shape the Fused account login already uses — and the user must supply their own Google OAuth client, because rclone's shared one is being retired** | `POST /api/mounts/remotes` (`shell/mounts/endpoints.py`) hardcodes `"type": "s3"` and its docstring sends OAuth backends to a terminal; `views/Mounts.tsx` told the user to run `rclone config`. That instruction is the whole gap — nothing about Drive is architecturally blocked, the endpoint simply had no way to obtain a token. It now spawns `rclone authorize "drive"`, which runs its own loopback callback server on `127.0.0.1:53682`, opens the system browser, and prints the token JSON on success; the server parses that blob and creates the remote through the **rc daemon**, never `rclone config create` on argv — the identical `ps`-visibility reasoning already recorded for the S3 key path. Completion is **polled, not pushed** (a status endpoint reporting `in_flight`), and cancel escalates SIGTERM→SIGKILL — all three lifted from `account.py`'s `_ActiveLogin`/`_pump`/`_watch_exit`/`_ensure_dead`, so this adds no new concurrency pattern. **Scope is full `drive`, not `drive.readonly`:** read-write was the requirement, and `drive.file` — the one non-restricted alternative — only ever sees files the app itself created, which is useless behind a mount. **The remote sets `--drive-skip-gdocs`:** Docs/Sheets have no byte representation, and without the flag they surface as ordinary-looking files whose saves silently fail to round-trip. **Nothing downstream needed changing, which was verified rather than assumed:** `_detect_read_only` (`access.py`) sees Drive advertise `Put`/`PutStream`/`Copy` via `operations/fsinfo`, falls past the S3/GCS-anonymous branches and reports writable, so the mount is read-write with no flag set and `mount_read_only` stays false; `VFS_OPT`'s `CacheMode: "full"` is already exactly what Drive read-write requires, since Drive cannot do partial or random writes; and the S3/GCS fast paths (`signing.py`, `direct_listing.py`) are each guarded by an explicit `type ==` check, so Drive falls through to the generic VFS mount path untouched. `_mount_credential_status` **did** need widening — it returned `"n/a"` for anything that is not `env_auth=true`, so a revoked Drive token surfaced as the generic "reconnect" message that cannot fix it; the gate now includes every OAuth backend type (read off the provider registry — see D224 — rather than a `type == "drive"` special case), and `_BAD_CRED_MARKERS` already carried Google's `"has been expired or revoked"` string, so the classifier itself was left alone. Drive is deliberately **not** added to `_credential_suggestions`: that list is credential-file-driven (it scans `~/.aws`, ADC, env vars) and an OAuth backend has nothing to detect, so it is a distinct provider card in the "Add storage" picker (D224) rather than a detected suggestion. No `drive://` scheme in `resolve_cloud_url` — that resolves bucket URLs and Drive has no equivalent. | Owner call 2026-08-04. **Rejected an embedded webview**, the obvious way to keep the user inside the app: GNOME Online Accounts shipped exactly that for years (a WebKitGTK view scraping the auth code out of the page title via Google's `urn:ietf:wg:oauth:2.0:oob` flow) and was forced to tear it out — Google now rejects embedded user-agents with `disallowed_useragent` and killed OOB outright in late 2022. System browser + loopback + PKCE is where GOA landed, and it is what `rclone authorize` already does, so the correct flow comes free. **Rejected driving rclone's rc config state machine** (`config/create` with `opt.nonInteractive`, then `config/continue`), which would surface the auth URL in-band: more moving parts and more sensitive to rclone version, for no user-visible gain over one subprocess. **On the OAuth client, the original premise is dead and this entry is rewritten: bring-your-own client ID is now MANDATORY for Drive.** The first version of D219 shipped no client ID and rode rclone's built-in shared one, on the reasoning that it is already verified by Google for the full `drive` scope, so an annual third-party security assessment was not our problem. That is no longer available. rclone has been notified that Google will begin **charging for API requests made on rclone's default client ID**, starting later in 2026 after 90 days' notice, and rclone's stated plan is warn → disable → remove it. So every Drive user must supply their own `client_id`/`client_secret`, and a remote that does not carry one is on a countdown rather than merely rate-limited. This is why the sign-in endpoint **refuses a Drive attempt without a client** (a 400 whose message explains the retirement, before the browser ever opens) and why the setup step exists at all in the UI. Drive is the **only** backend affected — rclone's config prompts still say "Leave blank normally" for OneDrive, Dropbox and Box — so those providers are deliberately never shown client fields (D224). **Rejected shipping one bundled fused-render client ID**, the obvious way to keep it one click: under Google's new per-client-ID charging *we* would be the payer for every user's traffic, and the full `drive` scope is a **restricted** scope, so publishing it means a CASA security assessment — roughly $540–1,000, four to twelve weeks, and re-verified annually. **Rejected narrowing to `drive.file`** to escape that: it carries no verification burden precisely because it can only ever see files the app itself created, which behind a mount means an empty folder — the same reason the scope choice above rejected it. **Rejected automating the Google Cloud setup** so the user never sees the console: there is no public API for creating a standard OAuth client at all, and the one programmatic path that exists (`gcloud iap oauth-clients create`) produces clients locked to IAP with an unmodifiable redirect URI and forced-internal brands — it cannot serve the loopback desktop flow `rclone authorize` needs. What is left is to make the manual trip as short as possible, which is what the stepper does: a button per console page, the downloaded `client_secret_*.json` as the primary input, and the pair remembered per machine so it is a one-time cost rather than a per-remote one. **Async upload failure is surfaced, not left to the log** (D221): with `CacheMode: "full"` a write returns success as soon as it lands in the local cache, and a quota or permission rejection would otherwise appear only in the rcd log. That is the most likely source of "I saved it and it vanished" on a read-write mount, so it is in scope here rather than deferred. |
| D221 | **Pending and failed VFS uploads are read from rcd's `vfs/queue` and surfaced on the mount, rather than inferred from the log** | The write path returns success at the cache, so the only authority on whether bytes actually reached the remote is the VFS upload queue — rcd exposes it as `vfs/queue` (per-item `name`, `size`, `tries`, `err`, and the retry delay), which is the same daemon-over-loopback call every other mount fact already comes from. A failing item carries a non-empty `err` and a climbing `tries`, so "queued" and "failing" are distinguishable without guessing from a message string, and the existing per-mount probe in `GET /api/mounts` is where it lands — that handler already fans out one worker per mount and joins with a timeout, so this adds a field to a payload the Mounts page is polling anyway rather than a second poll loop. **Rejected scraping the rcd log**, which was the obvious cheap route: the log is free-form, rotates, interleaves every mount's lines, and would tie a UI state to rclone's message wording — the structured queue is a stable contract that already distinguishes the two states. **Rejected blocking or erroring the write itself**: the whole point of `CacheMode: "full"` is that a write completes at local speed, and a Drive mount that made every save wait on an upload round-trip would be unusable; the honest model is that the save succeeded locally and the sync is a separate, observable thing. | Owner call 2026-08-04: build it with the Drive work rather than after it. A read-write cloud mount whose failures are invisible is worse than a read-only one that says so, and the failure mode is silent data loss from the user's point of view — they saw a successful save. |
| D220 | **`nolocks` stays as-is; `localocks` was evaluated and rejected, and database files on a mount stay unsupported on macOS** | D198 added `nolocks` to the macOS kernel mount options because go-nfs registers no NLM program while macOS enables remote locking by default, so a real `fcntl` lock — which SQLite and DuckDB take on **every** database-file open, including read-only opens — hung against an absent lockd. `nolocks` converts that hang into a fast failure, which is strictly better, but it *is* a failure: database files on a mount are unopenable on macOS, not merely slow. `mount_nfs`'s third setting, **`localocks`**, would handle lock requests on the client instead of forwarding them, which fits the topology exactly — this is a loopback mount, so the only processes needing to coordinate are on the one machine — and would keep the no-hang property while letting those opens succeed. It is a one-line change to `NFS_MOUNT_OPT`'s `ExtraOptions` in `shell/mounts/config.py`. It is **not** being made. | Owner call 2026-08-04: reading a database out of Google Drive is a very unlikely scenario and is not worth optimising for. The supporting reasons are worth recording so this is not revisited on a hunch. The blast radius is far smaller than it first appears: this is about opening a **database file**, and DuckDB reading Parquet or GeoTIFF off a mount takes no `fcntl` lock and is completely unaffected — which is essentially every template in the built-in set. Read-write is separately a bad idea on Drive whatever the lock mode, for reasons that have nothing to do with locking: `CacheMode: "full"` plus Drive's lack of partial writes makes every commit a whole-file re-upload of the entire database, uploads are async and non-atomic so a mid-upload failure leaves a torn file at the remote, and WAL mode needs `-wal`/`-shm` siblings kept consistent while `-shm` is a shared-memory mapping that does not behave over a network filesystem. So `localocks` would only ever have unlocked *reading* database files — a genuine but narrow win, affecting all backends rather than Drive specifically. Recorded as available and deliberately unspent, not as an oversight; it also could not be verified from this project's Linux dev machines, where FUSE handles POSIX locks locally and none of this reproduces, so taking it would have meant a macOS-only change validated only on the DMG. |
| D222 | **A mount probe that TIMES OUT no longer reads as "disconnected": the fast verdict is written before the slow `os.listdir`, so only a listdir that actually FAILS can downgrade it** | `mount_state` (`shell/mounts/lifecycle.py`) ran its whole probe on a thread, `join(timeout)`'d it, and fell back to `"disconnected"` when nothing had been written — so "we didn't hear back in 3 seconds" and "the mount is broken" were the same answer. That is fine for S3 and wrong for Drive. Measured on a real Drive mount on this machine: a cold subdirectory listing takes **18.27s**, a root listing once `VFS_OPT`'s `DirCacheTime: "30s"` (`shell/mounts/config.py`) expires takes **35.45s**, and a warm root takes 0.00s — against `PROBE_TIMEOUT = 3.0`. So a perfectly healthy Drive mount reported disconnected, which the fs-listing endpoint turns into a **503**, and because the dir cache expires every 30 seconds it recurred forever rather than settling. Confirmed live. The fix restructures `probe()`: `_is_mounted`, `_mount_wedged` and rcd membership already classify every genuine failure mode (wedged, stale, unmounted, not-served) and are all cheap, so `out["state"] = "mounted"` is published from those alone and the `os.listdir` runs afterwards, able only to *downgrade* via the existing `except OSError` branch. A probe that outruns the timeout therefore degrades to the fast verdict; a listdir that raises still reports disconnected; the `probe_io=False` health-monitor path is unchanged. | Owner call 2026-08-04. **Rejected simply raising `PROBE_TIMEOUT`**, which is where everyone reaches first: it is paid per **healthy** mount inside `GET /api/mounts`' join budget, so buying Drive its 35 seconds would make the mounts list hang for every S3 user behind one unresponsive daemon — trading a false red dot for a slow page. **Rejected a per-backend timeout** (a longer budget for `type == "drive"`): it still pays real wall-clock on the slow case, it needs a backend lookup inside the probe that the probe currently does not need, and it only moves the cliff — a cold Drive listing has no upper bound we know, so any number picked is a guess that eventually reproduces this. The principle worth keeping is the general one and it is not Drive-specific: **a timeout is the absence of evidence, not evidence of failure.** The probe's job is to catch the states that positively indicate breakage, and it already does that without any I/O. **Amended after review — the first version of this was wrong in a way worth recording.** It claimed the listdir "can only downgrade via the `except`"; a readdir that never returns never reaches any `except`, so the join expired with `"mounted"` already committed. The exposure was real: with rcd alive and listing, `ismount` true, and `_mount_wedged`'s `lstat` served from the dentry cache, **every** fast check passes while the only call that touches the network blocks forever (laptop suspend, VPN drop, dead DNS) — a green dot for the whole outage, with `broken_mount_error`'s Reconnect advice never firing, where the pre-D222 code at least said disconnected. Second-order, each poll (~30s per mount) stranded another probe thread in an uninterruptible syscall that never exits: unbounded accumulation, nothing logged. The fix is to distinguish "listdir finished" from "listdir never answered" — `_claim_listdir`/`_release_listdir` record per mountpoint whether one is outstanding and since when, a probe that finds one already out reports `mounted` under `LISTDIR_STUCK_AFTER` and `disconnected` past it (logging once per episode), and it never starts a second listdir into the same blackhole, which caps the thread leak at one per mountpoint. **The residual cost is accepted, not eliminated:** for up to `LISTDIR_STUCK_AFTER` a genuinely unreachable backend still reads as healthy. That window is the price of the whole decision and it is bought deliberately — a slow listing is *far* more common than a blackhole (every Drive mount pays the slow case every 30 seconds as the dir cache turns over, while a blackhole needs the network to actually die), so optimising the common case and bounding the rare one is the right trade. The threshold is what bounds the exposure, and it is set by measurement rather than taste: 60s is ~1.7x the 35.5s worst-case healthy listing, low enough that a real outage surfaces within two polls and high enough that no measured healthy listing trips it. Lowering it trades false reds for faster detection; that dial is the whole tuning surface and it is deliberately the only one. | 
| D223 | **The user's OAuth `client_id`/`client_secret` reach `rclone authorize` through the ENVIRONMENT (`RCLONE_<BACKEND>_CLIENT_ID`/`_SECRET`), never on argv** | `rclone authorize <backend> <client_id> <client_secret>` accepts them **only positionally** — there is no flag form — which would put a live OAuth client secret on the child's command line. On Linux `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` is mode `-r--r--r--`: readable by **every** local user for the whole life of the child, which is however long the user takes over a consent screen. That is exactly the invariant `_authorize_argv`'s docstring exists to hold, and the same one the S3 secret key and the rcd auth secret are already kept off argv for (they go over rcd's loopback HTTP body instead). `/proc/<pid>/environ` is mode `-r--------` — owner only — so the environment preserves the property while still reaching the child. Verified empirically against rclone v1.74.4: with `RCLONE_DRIVE_CLIENT_ID=TESTID12345` exported, the OAuth redirect carried `client_id=TESTID12345`; with nothing exported it fell back to rclone's shared `202264815644.apps.googleusercontent.com` — the client ID being retired (D219). Passed via `subprocess.Popen(env=...)` as an **overlay** on `os.environ`, since the child still needs `PATH`/`HOME` to run at all. | Owner call 2026-08-04. **Rejected argv**, which is what a reader who has not checked the `/proc` modes will "simplify" this back into — hence the comment at `_client_credential_env` citing the two modes explicitly, and a test asserting the secret is absent from `_authorize_argv`'s output while present in the spawn env. **Rejected driving rclone's `config/create` interactive state machine** to hand the client over in-band: it is the same rejection D219 already made for the token, for the same reasons (more moving parts, more sensitive to rclone version) and it would buy nothing here. The secret is also **cleared off the long-lived `_ActiveAuthorize` record** as soon as the attempt finishes, in a `finally` so every branch clears it — the identical bounded-lifetime discipline already applied to the token in `job.out`, and for the identical reason: that record deliberately outlives the attempt so the status endpoint can report the last outcome, and a credential left reachable for the life of the process is what turns a later crash dump or an added diagnostic into a real leak. | 
| D224 | **The Mounts page presents ONE provider-driven "Add storage" picker over six providers — Drive, Dropbox, Box, AWS S3, S3-compatible, public buckets — and OneDrive and iCloud are deliberately not among them** | The page had three disconnected entry points stacked at the bottom: the Add-mount form, a bare "Sign in to Google Drive" link, and an "Add a custom S3 remote" link. Both links read as footnotes, so the honest description of the old surface is that a user could not tell what was **possible** — and any provider added to it would have been invisible however well it worked. It is replaced by a grid of provider cards, each stating its setup cost up front ("One-click browser sign-in" versus "Needs a Google API client"), each opening its flow in the existing `Modal`. **Dropbox and Box are included because they are genuinely free to add:** the server-side work is one registry entry each (backend type, label, no extra params), they reuse the same authorize child, poll, cancel and rollback path as Drive, and — the actual reason they are cheap — rclone still says "Leave blank normally" for their client id/secret, so neither inherits Drive's console trip. They are explicitly never shown client fields; making the UI "consistent" there would invent setup work that does not exist. | Owner call 2026-08-04. **Rejected OneDrive**, which looks like the obvious fourth: `rclone authorize onedrive` does not finish the job, because OneDrive's config asks a **post-consent drive-picker question** (which drive/site of the several an account may have), and answering it programmatically means driving rclone's `config/create` → `config/continue` interactive state machine with `opt.nonInteractive` — precisely the machinery D219 rejected for the token path, now with a multi-step conversation to maintain against rclone version changes. It is not architecturally blocked; it is a different and much larger piece of work, and it is recorded as deferred rather than impossible. **Rejected iCloud** on three independent grounds, any one of which is disqualifying: rclone rates the backend **Tier 4 (experimental)**; it requires the user's **real Apple ID password** typed into our UI rather than an OAuth consent, which is a posture this project should not take whatever the storage; and its trust token **expires every 30 days**, so the mount would break monthly with the only remedy being to re-enter that password. **Rejected keeping the old links alongside the picker** as a migration courtesy: two ways to reach the same flow is the ambiguity this entry exists to remove. The Add-mount form stays exactly as it was — this is about the **setup** surface, not the mount list, and conflating "connect a provider" with "mount a folder from one" is what made the old page hard to read. | 
| D225 | **Cancel on a browser sign-in does not resolve until the server's `in_flight` drops, and a start refused because one is already running offers a Cancel of its own** | Two halves of the same defect: the client treated a cancel as DONE the moment the kill was accepted. `terminate()` only starts the teardown — rclone's callback server holds `127.0.0.1:53682` until the watcher has reaped the child — so restoring the sign-in button there handed the user a retry that 409s on the sign-in they had just cancelled. An accepted cancel now returns a THIRD outcome, `standdown`, distinct from `cancelled`: the modal stays up with Cancel disabled ("Canceling…") and a second, shorter poll (`oauthStandDownTick`) waits for `in_flight` to clear. That poll deliberately reads a settled failure as `cancelled` rather than surfacing it — the server's own error string there is "the … sign-in was canceled", i.e. the user's own click, and banner-ing it tells them off for it — but still reports `ok`, because consent can succeed and the remote be created inside the gap before the kill lands, and dropping that would leave a real remote invisible until a reload. The mirror case: a `start` refused with "a sign-in is already in progress" cleared `connecting`, which took the Cancel button away with it, so the message told the user to cancel something nothing on screen could cancel — the only exit was the 300s server timeout. The form now ASKS the status endpoint whether one is genuinely in flight (never matching on the error text) and, if so, offers "Cancel that sign-in" — a deliberately separate path from `cancel`, since there is no local poll to stop and no outcome to report: whatever that attempt creates belongs to the name IT was started with. **Rejected** killing the child on unmount, which would abort a sign-in the user may be mid-consent on in another window. |
| D226 | **`replacing` is derived from whether the remote actually exists, never from the client's `replace` flag** | `replace` is PERMISSION to overwrite; `replacing` is what the rollback reads to decide "this remote was the user's, do not delete it on a failed sign-in" (D219). Conflating them broke on an ordinary UI sequence: the Replace checkbox stays ticked while the user edits the name to a free one — the checkbox only stops being RENDERED — so `replace: true` arrived for a name that did not exist, the brand-new remote was marked `replacing`, and a failed `config/create` skipped `_delete_remote`, leaving exactly the half-created remote the rollback was added to prevent. Fixed on the SERVER, which already reads the remote list one line earlier for the collision 409, so the authoritative answer was already in hand and no non-UI caller can get this wrong either; resetting the checkbox client-side would have left the same trap open for any other caller. |
| D227 | **The Sessions sub-app (Claude Code session inbox) ships as bundled html+py content on a second builtin zip mount, not as a native React sub-app** | The fourth sub-app (after apps/explorer/learn) is the Claude Sessions triage inbox — ~2.2k lines of already-working fused-render content (`core_apps/sessions/` in the repo: inbox.html + runPython .py targets scanning `~/.claude/projects`; the learn content moved beside it to `core_apps/learn/` at the same time). It reuses learn's entire shipping mechanism (D123), generalized: `BUILTIN_MOUNTS` in `shell/mounts/automount.py` now drives N builtin zips (`learn.zip`, `sessions.zip` → `ensure_builtin_mounts()`), each mounted read-only via rclone `:archive:` and gated in the UI by a per-mount `*_mount_ready` config flag. The shell renders `${mounts_root}/sessions/inbox.html` at `/sessions` chrome-free, exactly like learn (StatView `variant="learn"`). **The one thing learn's precedent didn't cover: writes.** The app persists triage state (`triage.json`, `session_names.json`); the shipped copies write them to `~/.fused-render/claude-sessions/` (honoring `FUSED_RENDER_HOME`), never next to the scripts — the mount is read-only, and state must survive app upgrades that replace the zip. | Rebuilding in React was rejected for v1: it re-implements a polished working UI for no user-visible gain, and shipping the fused project dogfoods the platform's own authoring story (runPython bridge, params URL sync). Known accepted cost: the inbox polls via `fused.runPython` every 5s, a fresh subprocess per poll — fine at ~300 sessions; a native FastAPI route + React rebuild is the v2 path if this becomes core or the transcript count makes polling hurt. `analyze.py` deliberately keeps its fire-and-forget `claude -p` spawn (its cwd fallback moved off the read-only mount to `~`). fcntl locking is guarded for the Windows payload (no-op there). |
| D228 | **`--plugin-dir` is passed unconditionally — the `claude --help` capability probe D216 introduced is deleted, not moved or cached** | The probe ran inside `export_app_env`, i.e. on the main thread BEFORE uvicorn binds its socket, and it allowed the call 30s (`subprocess.run(..., timeout=30)`) while the desktop supervisor gives the entire child 20s to become ready (`supervisor/core.py:_READY_TIMEOUT_S`, set two weeks earlier in an unrelated refactor, never cross-checked) and then kills it, retries three times, and shows a startup-failure dialog. Two independently-tuned constants: the child's allowance for one subprocess was 1.5× the parent's allowance for the whole server. In the field this is not hypothetical — `claude.exe` is a ~279MB self-contained binary that auto-updates roughly weekly, and the first run after each update pays a Windows Defender first-touch scan: a measured 53.4s startup, confirmed with py-spy sitting in exactly this call, and a fake-shim experiment where a 25s shim made `wait_until_ready(20s)` give up at 20.2s while the same child answered at 28.9s. **The dialog fired for a server 8.7s from healthy.** Considered and rejected: (a) moving the probe off the readiness path fire-and-forget like `_AiSession.prewarm_default` — correct in shape, but it leaves the var unset during the cold window, silently degrading early sessions to D185's fallback, which is the exact silent miss D216 exists to remove; (b) persisting the answer keyed on binary path+size+mtime — pays the cold cost once per CLI update rather than once per process, but adds a cache-invalidation surface to keep right forever; (c) raising either timeout — picks a new arbitrary number for a call whose real distribution is bimodal (0.5s warm, ~1min cold). All three keep a probe whose answer is a foregone conclusion: `--plugin-dir` has shipped since the plugin system itself (CLI **2.0.12, 9 Oct 2025**; its only changelog entries, at 2.1.74/2.1.76 in March 2026, are *modifications* to an existing flag), on a binary that auto-updates weekly, ten months before D216 added a probe for it. Deleting it removes `_claude_bin`, `_supports_plugin_dir`, `_PLUGIN_DIR_SUPPORT` and the `subprocess` import from `skill_plugin.py`, leaves `export_skill_plugin_env` filesystem-only (so its position in `export_app_env` stops mattering), and takes with it a real bug: a probe TIMEOUT was caught by `except subprocess.SubprocessError` and cached as `False` — read downstream as positive evidence of non-support — silently stripping the skills from every session that server spawned, contradicting both docstrings, which said a run failure means "cannot tell". The accepted trade: an install old enough to reject the flag now loses every chat rather than just the skills (an unknown option makes the CLI exit before the turn starts). That population is ~nil and shrinks weekly; the fallback worth building IF it is ever seen in the field is a spawn-time retry-without-the-flag, which tests the real thing instead of a help-text substring and costs nothing on the happy path. Tests: `test_the_export_never_spawns_a_subprocess` pins the pre-bind path as filesystem-only (the scar), and `test_the_real_cli_still_takes_the_flag` shells out to the actual CLI — now the only thing standing behind the assumption, so it fails in CI rather than in a user's session if the flag is ever renamed or dropped. |
| D229 | **The `git` template becomes a VSCode-style Source Control GUI — branch management, staging, stashing, committing and pull/push — rebuilt IN PLACE, which overturns the locked GT-11 "Read-only, always"** | **GT-11 is overturned, on the owner's decision.** Read-only kept the view honest and left it a dead end: every question it answered — "this file is modified", "this is staged", "you are 3 commits behind" — ended in *"now go somewhere else"*, which is the one thing a view that already knows the answer should not say. The reads are unchanged and still never write, never fetch and never take an optional lock; what changed is that `ops.py` sits beside `log.py`. **In place, one mode**: a second `git_scm` template was rejected because two modes for one repository is a switcher the user has to learn a rule for, and history is not a different subject from staging — it became a *section* of the reorganized view. **Two modules, not one op-switch**: a reader that also mutates has no honest place to draw its validation line, and "what can this template do to my repository" must be one file to audit rather than a grep for verbs across an 800-line reader. **Scope**: stage/unstage/discard/stash stay restricted to the opened path by the same `:(literal)<rel>` pathspec the reads use, and the write rule is deliberately *stricter* than the read rule — the reader lists a rename with one side in the scope (GT-7, because "this file left the folder you are looking at" is the change the view exists to show) and lists a collapsed ancestor `dir/`, and neither may be *changed* from here, because listing a change and changing it are different acts. Rejected: repo-wide mutations with a warning (the scope is the whole premise of the view, and a warning is not a boundary). `branch_checkout` is exempt and repo-wide by nature — a branch IS a repository concept — which is expected, not a hole. **Commit is index-based and the UI is honest about it**: `git commit -m` with no pathspec, ever. The rejected alternative `git commit -- <paths>` looks like the scoped answer and is a different operation — it bypasses the index and records the *working tree* for those paths, so a file deliberately staged in one state gets committed in another; a silent data surprise is worse than a visible limitation. The price is that a commit from a `pkg/`-scoped view carries whatever is staged elsewhere, so the reader reports `staged_outside {count, paths}` (collected inside the *existing* unscoped `_status` walk rather than by a second `git status` — GT-7 measured that call once and it should not be paid twice) and the view warns *"N staged change(s) outside this scope will also be committed"* and lists them. Empty-message and nothing-staged are the module's own refusals, because git's are a malfunction-sounding sentence and a wall of status text respectively. **No history rewriting**: no `--amend`, no `reset --hard`, no rebase, no force push, `branch -d` and never `-D`, `pull --ff-only` with a non-fast-forward as a *refusal pointing at a terminal*. Rejected: offering merge/rebase buttons on divergence — both take a decision on the user's behalf that has no undo (a merge writes a commit they did not ask for, a rebase rewrites commits they already have). `git clean` never gets `-x`: an ignored path is where `.env`, virtualenvs and build trees live, a scale of loss unlike "throw away the edit I just made" and one no confirmation can warn about, since those files are by construction invisible in this view. A test greps the module for the forbidden spellings so a future change that needs one is a conversation. **Confirm before anything destructive**: exactly three ops can lose uncommitted work (`discard`, `discard_all`, `stash_drop`), the module names them in one constant the view mirrors so an op added later without being classified ships without a confirmation *loudly*, and each is its own addressable call — never a side effect of a safe one. The confirmation is **inline in the view, not `window.confirm`**: the template renders in an iframe where a native dialog is at best chrome the view cannot place and at worst suppressed, and inline puts the question next to the row it is about. Its pending state is a URL param, so a refresh reproduces the question instead of dropping it. Discarding a *staged* change is two explicit calls (`unstage` then `discard`) composed by the view behind one confirmation, because `restore --worktree` restores from the index and a single call would put back the very content being discarded. `branch_delete` is deliberately unconfirmed — `-d` cannot lose work, git's own refusal is the safety step, and a second one trains people to click through. **Non-interactive by environment stays** (`GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` + askpass knobs, plus `GIT_EDITOR=false` so a conflicted `stash pop` is an error rather than a wait with no end): pull/push work with a credential helper or ssh-agent and otherwise fail fast, because a prompt nobody in an iframe can answer is a hang. **`GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0` is DROPPED for mutating commands** and kept for reads — it only ever suppresses locks git takes *optionally*, so carrying it on a write would state a promise the module cannot keep and would suppress exactly the opportunistic index refresh that makes the `git status` right after a mutation accurate. **A push from a branch with no upstream sets one** (`--set-upstream <remote> <branch>`, labelled "Publish branch") rather than surfacing git's "use --set-upstream" advice, which is a sentence no GUI button can act on; it is never a force — it can only create a ref that does not exist, and a remote branch that does exist with commits we lack is refused as any other non-fast-forward. Rejected: failing with git's advice (a dead end in a GUI) and silently pushing to a guessed remote when several exist with no `origin` (there is no defensible guess, so the answer is "no remote" and no button). **Mount-backed repos stay refused for the write path too** (GT-4/MD-11): the gate is the UX, the module is the guarantee, and a hand-written `?_mode=git` URL must never reach a *mutating* git call across an rclone/NFS mount. Branch names are validated by `git check-ref-format --branch` rather than a hand-rolled regex (the rules are git's and change with git), with the one rule that cannot be delegated — a leading `-`, since `check-ref-format` has no `--` terminator — checked first in Python. **Amended after review, four findings that all had the same shape — a value carrying less information than the decision made from it.** (a) **A name GIT produced is untrusted input too.** git echoes refnames and remote names verbatim out of files inside `.git`, and those files are content: a hand-written `.git/HEAD` makes `symbolic-ref --short HEAD` print `--upload-pack=cmd`, a hand-written `[remote "--receive-pack=cmd"]` makes `git remote` print that, and both flow into `fetch`/`pull`/`push` argv — one click of Push, local command execution, and the view reporting success. Neither is reachable through git's porcelain, so such a repository arrived as a tarball or a shared drive (`clone` does not copy config). The leading-dash rule is now applied centrally to repo-derived names and the whole repository is refused, not the bad name filtered out (using the *other* remote silently would hide that it is malformed); `--` was also added before the `push --set-upstream` positionals, the one command that lacked it. Both guards are kept rather than either — depending on one alone is exactly how a single missing `--` becomes that bug. (b) **A stash is addressed by commit id, not only by index.** `stash@{n}` means "the nth entry right now" and any `stash push` — this view, another tab, a terminal — renumbers every entry, while a confirmation can sit in the URL indefinitely; a bounds-checked index would therefore irreversibly drop a different, still-wanted stash. `log.py` now emits `%H` per entry and `ops.py` verifies it, refusing with "that stash moved, check the list again" — deliberately NOT repairing by finding the sha's real index, because the list the user read is stale and silently acting on a redrawn one is the same surprise in a new costume. (c) **A discard confirmation names its section** (`discard:staged:<path>` vs `discard:worktree:<path>`): an `MM` file is in both lists, both rows built the same key, and the CHANGES row's ↩ — whose question mentioned only the unstaged edit — was composing the staged form and destroying the staged version too. Rejected: keeping one key and disambiguating by entry state, which is what produced the bug. (d) **Every network command names its remote and refspec**: a bare `git push` means whatever `push.default`/`remote.pushDefault` say, which can be every matching branch, or a different remote than the success message names. Also from the review: `ask` is cleared after every mutation; the composed staged-discard says so when its second half fails ("the change was unstaged, but discarding it failed: …"); the view mirrors ops' scope rule so a row the module would refuse ships no buttons; both stream-matched git sentinels read stdout AND stderr; and `_brief` picks git's `fatal:`/`error:` line rather than the first three, after a first-attempt `pull` pushed "Not possible to fast-forward" past the cut and downgraded a specific refusal to a generic one. SPEC §33 GT-11..GT-17. |
| D230 | **A `.py`'s environment is the FOLDER's, declared in `pyproject.toml`, built by `uv sync`, and stored centrally under the home dir keyed on the folder's path — per-file PEP 723 headers are no longer read, with no migration path** | A breaking change, taken deliberately and in one step. Under D172 every `.py` carried its own complete dependency list and got its own venv, so two scripts in one folder wanting `altair` built the same environment twice, a page calling five scripts showed up to five progress rows and five cancel buttons, and nothing about the arrangement was reproducible on another machine. The declaration now lives once per project root (the app folder, a template folder, else the **topmost** ancestor holding a manifest — topmost, not nearest, so a stray `readers/pyproject.toml` cannot shadow the app's), every `.py` beneath it shares one venv however deep it sits, and `uv.lock` makes the resolution reproducible. **Central storage, keyed on the folder's absolute path.** Follows MD-7 — derived state goes to `~/.fused-render/venvs/<sha256(abs path)[:16]>`, user data travels with the file — and it is also the only way to guarantee the venv and the uv cache sit on one filesystem, which is what makes uv's hardlinks dedupe instead of silently falling back to full copies. An in-folder `.venv` was rejected outright: for a core template it would be destroyed on every release by `core_templates.py`'s rmtree+os.replace swap, costing a full re-download of numpy/pyproj/imagecodecs/pypandoc-binary each upgrade — and rmtree over a 30k-file venv is slow and fails on Windows while the geotiff and zarr daemons hold files open. `.venv/` is nonetheless added to `app_git._GITIGNORE`, because a user running `uv` in their own terminal will create one and `commit()`'s `git add -A` would sweep it into app history. **The path is hashed as given, not `realpath`'d** — a deliberate divergence from MD-7's canonicalisation. Move-or-rename → a fresh, clean environment is a requested feature, and collapsing symlinks would defeat it; the dangerous direction (two folders colliding on one key) stays impossible either way. That makes orphaned venvs a normal consequence rather than a leak, so `projectenv.gc()` runs once at server startup, off the event loop, and reclaims any venv whose sidecar names a path that no longer exists. A venv with no readable sidecar is left alone: it may be an install in flight, and deleting one out from under a running worker is worse than leaking it. **Staleness is a digest, never an mtime chain.** Upstream's `pyproject → uv.lock → pyvenv.cfg` mtime comparison is simply wrong for templates: `copytree` uses `copy2`, so every release stamps a template's manifest newer than its venv and would force a resync of byte-identical dependencies at each upgrade. A `.fused-source.json` sidecar inside the venv records the sha256 of the `pyproject.toml` it was built from; a mismatch is the signal. The MANIFEST only: `uv.lock` is an OUTPUT of `uv sync`, so folding it in would make the environment's own side effect a reason to rebuild the environment — and, worse, hashing the lock *instead* (on the reasoning that it is the resolved truth) meant a dependency ADDED to `pyproject.toml` changed nothing, the venv read as fresh, no install was offered, and the run failed later on an ImportError with no loader and no explanation. A user must never have to run `uv sync` by hand to fix that, since doing so writes an in-folder `.venv` and diverges from the home-dir store. The cost in the other direction is a resync for a comment edit, which is a fast no-op through uv's cache. For the same reason the worker syncs BARE rather than `--frozen`: uv honours a current lock as-is and re-resolves only what a manifest edit moved, whereas `--frozen` turns that edit into a lockfile error instead of reconciling it. It is deliberately NOT routed through D212's repair machinery — an edit is not corruption, so it must not spend the one-rebuild-per-process budget nor be bounded by it. D212's probe, marker-stamp identity check and repair discipline are otherwise **untouched**; only the key that names the venv directory changed. **The app-satisfies fast path survives, but only for UNLOCKED projects, and only when the declaration is non-empty** (an empty `[project]` table is PY-17: nothing to install means the app's own interpreter, not an empty venv without the bundled stack). A `uv.lock` is a request for exact resolution — the user committed specific versions so the folder resolves the same way elsewhere — and satisfying it "near enough" from whatever the app happens to ship is the one thing a lock exists to rule out. Without a lock the trade is the same one it always was: skip a multi-hundred-MB download the app has already paid for. **No migration tooling, on purpose.** A `fused-render migrate-env` command that unioned every header under a folder into a manifest was built and then deliberately removed: the union is a *guess* at a dependency set the user never wrote (two headers declaring `numpy` and `numpy>=2` have no correct automatic merge), it would have to be trusted without review on the one occasion it runs, and it buys a one-time convenience at the cost of a permanent code path and a permanent expectation that headers are still half-supported. Recorded here for the reason D177 exists: an unwritten deliberate omission is indistinguishable from a bug, and the next reader would otherwise "fix" it by adding the tool back. What replaced it was the error itself — a file carrying a header was REFUSED, the refusal naming the `pyproject.toml` to create and quoting the dead block's packages. **OVERTURNED BY D233:** that refusal is gone and a leftover header is now silently ignored, which this row argued was never an option. The argument was right about the failure mode and wrong about the stakes — see D233 for why a pre-release product with no installed base does not buy that protection. | **Supersedes** the parts of D172/D173/D174 that describe per-file headers: D172's "no baseline is unioned in" survives verbatim (a folder's manifest is its complete list), D173's "sharing one venv across a folder is deliberately not built" is exactly what this reverses, and D174's self-managed `DAEMON_VENV` is unchanged — the geotiff and zarr_aoi daemons still own their own environments, because the built-in executor honors no declaration of ours at all. **Known accepted risk:** a user who runs `uv run` themselves gets an in-folder `.venv` that VS Code will auto-select, so their editor resolves imports against a different environment than the app uses; there is no clean cross-platform answer, the gitignore entry limits the blast radius, and the inspector reports which interpreter the app actually chose. **Deferred:** a UI "Reset environment" action, and a "this folder moved from X" note in the loader. **Every existing user app with a header stops working until its folder gains a `pyproject.toml`.** This repo has no changelog file — GitHub releases are cut with `gh release --generate-notes` (`.github/workflows/release.yml`), so the release notes ARE the merged PR titles. The user-facing description therefore lives in `docs/usage.md` § Dependencies (with the breaking-change callout), and the PR that ships this must carry `BREAKING` in its title so the generated notes say so on the release page. |
| D231 | **Without `uv`, a folder that declares dependencies gets a clear refusal — no `python -m venv` + pip fallback** | D230 makes `uv sync` the builder, and the builder it replaced (`ensure_requirements_venv`) degraded to `<python> -m venv` + pip when uv was absent. That fallback is deliberately NOT restored. It cannot honour a `uv.lock` — pip has no notion of one — so on exactly the machines with the least tooling it would resolve its own versions and produce a DIFFERENT environment than the one the user committed, silently, while PY-16 promises the opposite. It would also be a second builder to keep correct: its own marker discipline, its own sidecar digest, its own answer to the half-built-directory case, all exercised by nobody in CI and by almost nobody in the field. A wrong environment that looks right is worse than an error that names its own fix. **Scope of the loss is small and stated in the error:** every script whose folder has no `pyproject.toml` still runs on the app's own interpreter with the whole `[bundled]` stack (PY-17), which is most of them; only folders that declare their own dependencies are affected. All three packaged builds ship uv (`build_dmg.sh`, `build_linux_appimage.sh`, `build_windows_installer.ps1`), so the population this can reach is a source checkout without uv — which also cannot run `uv lock` to produce the lock the templates ship. `_resolve_script_python` case 4 still answers READY without uv, on purpose: readiness is a question about the INTERPRETER, and refusing there would take the PY-17 path down for a capability most runs never use. The refusal happens in the worker, at the point it is actually true. | Considered and rejected: (a) failing at startup when uv is absent — punishes every PY-17-only user for a capability they may never touch; (b) a pip fallback gated on "only when there is no uv.lock" — the lockless case is precisely where uv would have WRITTEN a lock, so the fallback would leave the folder permanently unreproducible while looking like it worked. |
| D232 | **The `fused` pin moves off the S3 wheel URL to the `fused==2.9.3b3` PyPI pre-release** | The direct-URL pin existed for one reason: the clone flags `deploy.py` sends (`--allow-clone` / `--no-allow-clone`, `fusedio/fused` #351) had landed on main with no release carrying them, and `2.9.3b2` — the newest thing on PyPI — exposed neither, so cloning deploys and *every* repoint failed. 2.9.3b3 carries them, and also carries the `workflow_venv` work (`fusedio/fused` #354) that this branch's upstream half went in as, so the stopgap has served its purpose. The string stays duplicated in three places (`deploy.PINNED_FUSED_REQUIREMENT`, pyproject's `[fused]` extra, its `[bundled]` list) with tests holding all three equal — D78's arrangement, unchanged. | A version pin beats a URL on every axis that matters here: it resolves from any index rather than one S3 bucket, it is cacheable and legible in `pip freeze` and in a `uv.lock`, and it survives the bucket being reorganized. Still a `bNN`, so the exact `==` is load-bearing — a range specifier would exclude the pre-release outright. `allow-direct-references` stays enabled in `[tool.hatch.metadata]`: it costs nothing and the next unreleased upstream landing will want it. Move the pin to `2.9.3` when the final ships. |
| D233 | **A leftover `# /// script` header is an ordinary comment — not read, not refused, not reported** | Overturns the refusal D230 shipped with. `engine.run_python` no longer detects headers at all: `projectenv.has_script_header`, `header_dependencies`, the PEP 723 regex and the `ScriptHeaderNoLongerRead` error are deleted. A file carrying a block runs exactly as it would without one — the folder's `pyproject.toml` is the only declaration (PY-16), and a folder with none runs on the app interpreter (PY-17). | The refusal existed to turn a confusing failure (an ImportError for a package the user can see they declared) into guidance, and it was not worth its cost.
