A DMG install replaces the .app under a still-running process, so plist ≠ running `__version__` means only an app restart helps — the card says so and never offers a refresh. Restart outranks refresh when both drift (disk 0.5.0, server 0.4.9, tab 0.4.8): a refresh there still leaves a stale server, and auto-reload is suppressed for the same reason. The decision table is a pure module (`server-status.ts`, bun-tested); the component keeps the polling and timers it already had. **The restart card's button is a new `fused-render://relaunch` deep link** — payload-free under D128's strictness rules — because the page cannot be allowed to kill the app over HTTP: `/api/desktop/shutdown` is token-guarded precisely so a web page can't, and handing the token to the shell would undo that. The OS routes the link to the running app, which parks a detached `/bin/sh` relauncher on its own pid (a dying app cannot `open` its own successor — `open` on a live bundle only foregrounds it — so the wait happens in a process that survives us), then quits through the normal teardown (D-quit path); the relauncher sees the pid die and runs `open -a <bundle> fused-render://launch` — the launch action so the successor opens no home tab and steals no focus (a plain `open` is a normal launch, D128), `-a` so the deep link can't resolve to some other registered install — the down-card covers the gap, and the probe auto-reloads the page onto the new version. Relaunch no-ops when unpackaged, when a quit is already in flight (it rides begin_quit's locked claim bool, not an unlocked `state["quitting"]` read — respawning an app the user is quitting would be worse than ignoring the link), and when the process already RUNS the disk version or the plist is unreadable: the OS may launch a fresh instance just to deliver the link (app crashed, or a second click after the old pid died), and that instance quitting itself to boot itself again would be a pointless cycle that can race a second instance past the pidfile the teardown already removed. On Windows/Linux (winopen, supervisor) the link degrades to launch semantics — ensure running, no tab — instead of falling through to a /clone error page. **Accepted exposure, stated rather than implied:** any web page can carry `<a href="fused-render://relaunch">`, so the gate is the browser/OS "open FusedRender?" confirmation — weaker than the shutdown token, and a bigger blast radius than `launch` (a full quit-and-respawn vs. a foreground). Accepted because the action is the user's own Quit+reopen: the same graceful teardown every quit surface funnels through, followed by a respawn — a nuisance if tricked, never a loss, and materially different from `/api/desktop/shutdown`, which kills the server and brings nothing back. |
| D274 | **Before deferring a read behind a click, MEASURE which part of it is slow — and when the answer is "a cosmetic sub-step", make that step optional instead of making the whole feature optional. The `claude` snapshots panel lists on arrival; `file_history.timeline` grows `deltas=`; the file index is explicitly NOT the enumerator** | The panel was collapsed behind a "Show version history" row on the theory that a timeline is a worker round trip most file opens never want (FH-18). The theory named the wrong cost. Profiled on `claude`'s own `template.html` — 453 KB, 12 checkpoints — the read is 292 ms, and **`difflib` inside `_delta` is 290 of them** (4.66M calls in `find_longest_match`), against **7 ms** to read all twelve versions off disk and **0.2 ms** to enumerate them out of the store. The expensive thing was the `+9 −52` on each row; the thing being deferred was the entire feature. So `_delta` takes `exact=`, threaded out as `timeline(deltas=…)`, the page asks for `deltas: "0"`, the read lands in ~6 ms — **~49×** — and the list is simply there when the panel mounts. The exact diff still exists where it is read: `snapshot_plan`, one version, on the click that opens a row.<br><br>**What makes the cheap read safe to act on** is that `differs` is a BYTE comparison, so `_locate`, `position`, `revert`, `offer` and `unique_current` are bit-identical either way — the softened pair is decoration, and the revert semantics (FH-3) never see the flag. The knob defaults to EXACT, the opposite direction from `enrich`, because a hand-written call that did not think about it should get the whole truth; and it only ever narrows, so `deltas=True` cannot buy back the quadratic diff `DIFF_BYTE_CAP` exists to refuse. **One rendering trap fell out, and only the running app caught it:** a net is a SINGLE number by construction (`max(0, ver − cur)` against `max(0, cur − ver)` — at most one side is ever non-zero), so printing it in the exact delta's PAIR shape put `~+0 −43` down every row of a file that has only grown. Every test passed and the panel looked worse than before the change; a screenshot of the real thing is what showed it. It now renders one signed term (`~−43`), or the word "changed" when both sides are zero — an edit that replaced as many lines as it removed, which is most of them.<br><br>***Rejected: answering the enumeration from the file index* (§30).** It was the owner's suggestion and the measurement inverted it. The `listdir` walk it would replace is 0.2 ms — 0.07% of the read — while the same answer from the index measured **8.6 ms** cold and 3.2 ms warm *in-process*, before the HTTP hop and the `/api/index/status` probe the JS bridge adds. And it would import a staleness this panel specifically cannot tolerate: the index is a background scan (no `fsevents.json` on Linux), `snapInvalidate` refetches the moment a turn ends, and the checkpoint Claude wrote four seconds ago is precisely the row the user came to find. **The generalisation: the file index wins for machine-wide questions over a large corpus, and loses to a 44-entry `listdir` on a keyed lookup in a small directory that changes under the user's hands.**<br><br>**Showing the list is what exposed FH-4**, and the fix is the same lesson twice: the number was fine, the framing was wrong. Version numbers restart per session, so five chats over one file give five chains each starting at v1, and the flat merged list showed "v2" three times — *"why do we have multiple snapshots of the same version"*. Rather than dropping `vN` (it is real, and it says where in a run of edits a checkpoint sits) or globally renumbering it (inventing an ordering the store never wrote), the panel now draws the chain: contiguous per-session runs, each with a heading. **Contiguous runs, not a group-by** — the row order is load-bearing, since `_locate` walks the merged timeline positionally to pick a revert target, so the grouping may only insert boundaries; a session that came back to the file later gets two headings, which is what happened. The heading is **named from the sidecar's `preview`**, the same string the "Recent chats" rows above it use, so the two sections agree; a miss is ordinary (the store sees terminal sessions this file's sidecar never had) and falls back to "chat" plus the short session id. *Rejected: labelling the fallback "a chat outside this app"* — a chat started from another file's page in the app is also absent from this file's sidecar, so the claim would be wrong.<br><br>**Two controls died with the deferral** — the placeholder `.snap-row` and its `hide ▾` — which is the third revision of this heading and the last, since there is now nothing to open. `snapInvalidate` had to grow a `snapMounted` gate on the way: it fires when every turn ends, including in a FOLDER chat where the panel never mounted, and a hidden section would have gone on spending a round trip per turn for an answer `_snapshots` refuses. **Also recorded rather than fixed:** the store keys on `sha256(abspath)`, so the same file in a git worktree has its own empty chain — dogfooding inside a worktree makes a working panel look broken. SPEC FH-2, FH-18, FH-19. |
| D275 | **Model weights are fetched by US, not by `snapshot_download`: up to 4 `Range` segments per file, with segments across ALL files as the units of work in one pool capped at 8 connections, and per-segment offsets fsynced to a `.fusedpart` sidecar as the bytes land so a cancel, a crash or a quit resumes instead of restarting. Any failure or incapability falls back to `snapshot_download` / `hf_hub_download` exactly as they were** | Two properties of the thing being downloaded make the library's shape the wrong one. A modern model's bytes are **one shard**, not many — `snapshot_download` opens one connection per file and one file at a time, so a 4.6GB safetensors moved at exactly one connection's speed no matter how much link the machine had. And the fetch is **killed on quit** by design (AI-5e), so every interruption cost the user the whole download; the on-disk state is what makes the supervisor's kill cheap rather than destructive, which is why resumability shipped in the same change rather than as a follow-up. It is stdlib-only `urllib.request` because `worker_base` is imported by every runner's interpreter and is the only part of the worker contract CI can run at all (AI-9a). **The correctness argument is one ordering:** snapshot the cursors, fsync the DATA, then write the sidecar — so a recorded offset is always a durable byte, never a byte still in flight that a kill would lose and a resume would then skip. `os.pwrite` on a shared fd (no userspace buffering) is what makes that claim true at the syscall level, and its absence on Windows is one of the fallback conditions rather than a buffered approximation.<br><br>***Rejected: hf's own `max_workers`.*** It parallelises across FILES, which is the axis that does not matter here — the one big shard that dominates a model download stays on a single connection, so the case that motivated this is exactly the case it does not improve. ***Rejected: writing into hf's `.incomplete` file*** so the two downloaders could share resume state. hf resumes one of those by seeking to its current length; our segments write out of ORDER, so a partial file of length N does not mean the first N bytes are present, and hf would append onto a hole and produce a blob of exactly the right size that is silently wrong — the worst available failure, since nothing downstream would notice until a load. A suffix of our own also means the fallback path is clean: hf never sees our state at all. ***Rejected: verifying a hash after download.*** `huggingface_hub` does not either — it relies on TLS plus `Content-Length` — and re-reading every gigabyte off disk to answer a question the transport already answers would give back a chunk of the speed this was for. What IS checked is completeness, and the first cut checked it the wrong way — against the part file's length, which `ftruncate` makes correct before a single byte arrives, so the check was dead code and a sparse file of pure holes would have published as a blob. It is the per-segment CURSORS, the same durable-byte accounting the sidecar records; the code says so, so it does not read as an oversight. ***Rejected (scope): a bandwidth limit, a detached daemon that survives app quit, per-segment UI.*** The first two are the download-manager prototype's requirements, not this one's; quitting the app stopping the download is a decision (AI-5e), not a gap.<br><br>**What it costs.** A second downloader to maintain beside the fallback, and a class of bug the library was insulating us from — a lying `Content-Range`, an expiring presigned CDN URL, a server that answers the probe with a 206 and then ignores `Range`. That last one is why a 200 at a non-zero offset is refused outright: written where it was asked for, one body's bytes land at four different offsets and the file is the right LENGTH and entirely wrong, the one failure a size check cannot catch. **Review found three more members of that family and they are the reason to state it as a family rather than as three bugs:** a *206* whose `Content-Range` starts somewhere else (a clamping proxy — the same scattering wearing a legal status code), a re-resolve that swallowed a whole new metadata answer when a repo was updated mid-download (new bytes at old offsets, published under the old etag), and the dead length check above. All three end in the same place — a plausible blob under a correct etag, which hf then serves from cache forever — so anything that could put bytes somewhere other than where they were asked for now aborts to the fallback rather than being retried into. Each of those is a test in `tests/test_ai_hub_fetch.py` driving a real `http.server` that misbehaves on purpose, because a stubbed reader cannot lie about a header. **The most expensive finding was not a corruption but an invisible non-event:** we sent the Hub token on to the presigned CDN URL, which S3 refuses as two authentication mechanisms — so for any user with a token set, which is everyone pulling a gated or large model, every probe and every segment failed and the download quietly fell back to the thing this replaced, SLOWER than before and with nothing on screen to say so. A fallback designed to be invisible is a fallback that can hide the feature never running at all, and the lesson generalises past this PR: anything that degrades silently needs a test asserting the fast path was TAKEN, not merely that the result was right. **A third round found four more defects and all four were in the RETRY LOOP** — worth recording as a shape rather than as four fixes. That loop is the only place here where two things can disagree, the connection and the cursor, and every one of the four was that disagreement: a budget counting BYTES ARRIVING rather than the cursor MOVING, which is an infinite loop in one direction (a server that ignores `Range` and truncates re-sends the same prefix, and the safe reading of such a body rewinds the cursor, so the budget never expires and the bar oscillates 0%→50% until someone kills the process) and an abort in the other (the flag was read from a drain that a raising `read()` never returns from, so half a gigabyte on disk under a connection reset counted as a failed attempt); a `finish()` outside the guard that sets the stop flag, so a failure while publishing let the pool's own shutdown wait out every remaining segment of every remaining file; and a resume that skipped the range probe on principle, where only a probe could tell it the server had stopped honouring ranges — the refusal that follows takes down the whole repo and the fallback then deletes the progress the un-probed resume existed to protect. **A loop whose exit condition is DERIVED rather than observed earns more review than the code around it**, and three of the four fixes were the same move: observe the thing that matters instead of a proxy for it. A fifth round (a bot, on the merge commit) found the last one: the file list came from the repo's DEFAULT branch and the fetch was hardcoded to `main` — two sources of truth agreeing by coincidence, and where they differ we fetch a different revision than the list described, write a ref for it, and stay internally consistent while doing it, because every etag still matches its content. One listing now yields the total, the names AND the commit, so the pinning is structural rather than a matching default; `_segmented_fetch` takes the revision with no default at all. **The through-line of all four rounds: every defect was one fact represented twice** — a cursor and a flag, a layout and a probe, a listing and a revision — which is the same failure this module's own docstring was written about. It is also why the probe answer is three-valued — "no" and "could not ask" drive opposite decisions about a recorded layout. Progress needed one repair on the way in: the part file is created at its final size and filled sparsely, so `bytes_on_disk` counts a `.fusedpart` by allocated BLOCKS — by length it reported a 4.6GB download as complete before a byte arrived. That in turn makes **sparse files a requirement, not an optimisation**, so a cache filesystem that allocates on `ftruncate` (where pre-sizing every file would also reserve the repo's full 25GB before a byte downloads) is a fallback condition, detected once per download with a 4MB throwaway file. ***Rejected: an hf-style cache lock.*** The etag names the CONTENT, so two app instances fetching one repo write identical bytes at identical offsets; the only race is a rename pulling a part file out from under the other, whose next syscall fails and whose download falls back — slower, never corrupt. Inside one app the deterministic job id already joins the second Download onto the first (AI-5a). SPEC AI-5b, AI-5i. |
| D276 | **`[bundled]` is a size budget, not a wish list: five groups (556 MB, 58% of the installed set) leave the extra and move into the manifests of the templates that use them** | Measured on macOS/arm64/py3.12 with `importlib.metadata`, taking each group's dependency closure minus the survivors' closure, and summing the real files on disk: **polars 197 MB** (`polars` + `polars-runtime-32`, which carries the actual engine), **scipy 70 MB**, **matplotlib 25 MB** (+contourpy/cycler/kiwisolver), the **PDF stack 70 MB** (pymupdf 56, pikepdf 13, fpdf2 1.5 + defusedxml), and the **geo stack 180 MB** (geopandas/rasterio/rio-tiler/shapely/zarr, whose exclusive transitives are the bulk of it: pyogrio 77, pyproj 18, numcodecs, morecantile, numexpr, affine and the pystac-ext-* fan-out). Together with fonttools and pyparsing, shared only between matplotlib and fpdf2, that is **556 MB**: the installed `[bundled]` + core set goes from **954 MB to 398 MB**. What justified each removal individually, because "it's big" is not a reason on its own: **polars is imported by NOTHING** — no template, no core app, no server code; it was advertised to user `runPython` code and nothing else, and a fifth of the download for everyone is not what an advertisement is worth. **scipy's only consumer, `netcdf/grid_tile_server.py`, already installs it into the venv it manages itself** (DAEMON_DEPS, D174), so the packaged copy was downloaded a second time and the shipped one was never imported — the same was true of `zarr` for `zarr_aoi` and `rasterio` for `geotiff` and `pyramid`. **matplotlib** had two consumers, `map/geo_classify.py` (now declared there) and `notebook/kernel_body.py`, which only harvests figures the user's own cell created and does nothing when it is absent. **pymupdf/pikepdf serve `pdf_studio` alone**; fpdf2 is the PDF *export* path of `excel` and `slides`. The **geo stack** genuinely serves `map`, `vector` and `geometry_editor` — the deepest per-user cost in the extra, paid by everyone who never opens a raster. Six folders gained a `pyproject.toml` + committed `uv.lock` (SPEC PY-16): `excel`, `geometry_editor`, `map`, `pdf_studio`, `slides`, `vector`. `geotiff`, `netcdf`, `zarr_aoi` and `pyramid` gained nothing, deliberately — every heavy import there is behind a self-managed venv, and a folder manifest would download the same wheels twice (D174). Two manifests name `pyarrow` for a reason no import scan can see: geopandas' parquet IO is an OPTIONAL backend, so `to_parquet`/`read_parquet` fail at RUNTIME in a venv where every import succeeded — the `pypandoc`-vs-`pypandoc-binary` trap again, and the reason "is it importable" is never the completeness question. **`BUNDLED_EXCLUDED` was the wrong tool and stays empty.** It excludes from the macOS BUNDLE while the extra still promises the package, so Linux and Windows would have kept carrying all 556 MB — D176's defect with the platforms swapped. The mechanism is unchanged and still guarded; it is for something the extra needs and py2app cannot carry, not for something the product no longer wants. **The cost, stated plainly:** the first render of a `.tif`/`.shp`/`.gpkg` map, a vector preview, a geometry edit, a PDF Studio session, a .pptx and the Excel PDF export now pays a one-time PY-18 install (progress row, ~1 min for the geo stack) instead of being instant. That is the trade the numbers were weighed against: everyone pays 556 MB of download and disk, once per release, versus the minority of users who open those formats paying an install, once per folder. **Two residual risks, accepted and recorded rather than hidden.** (1) Folder manifests are honoured only by the fused engine; the built-in executor ignores them (D174). D204 makes fused-when-available the default and `[bundled]` ships the engine on 3.11+, so packaged apps are on the honouring path — but a user who explicitly pinned `builtin` in Preferences, or a 3.10 `pip install` where the `fused` marker skips the engine, now gets a `ModuleNotFoundError` from `map`/`vector`/`pdf_studio` with no install flow, where before it worked. (2) `matplotlib` in a notebook cell now depends on the kernel's interpreter, so inline figures need a user-picked env. **Enforced, not asserted (D177).** The three-part PY-16 invariant only ever constrained folders that ALREADY had a manifest, which made this change invisible to the suite: a fourth part was added first — `test_a_template_declares_whatever_the_app_does_not_ship`, judged against the BUNDLE and exempting self-managed files — and watched go red for exactly the six folders before any manifest was written. `pyproj` and `pyogrio` joined `_IMPORT_TO_DIST` in the same pass: never in `[bundled]`, but on the app interpreter via geopandas/rasterio, so `map/vector_engine.py` importing them directly had never been checked. The advertising side got the same treatment, because a promise is a list too: `check_libs.SUPPORTED` may only name what the bundle ships, and the Learn page's static table and `fused-render-authoring`'s bullet list must equal SUPPORTED exactly. Deriving those three at build time was considered and rejected — the packaged app ships no `pyproject.toml`, the skill is read as plain Markdown, and `importlib.metadata` cannot tell a promised library from a transitive one — so this is D177's third rung: the copies stay, their divergence fails. |

## Open items (small, non-blocking)

- Shell visual design: v1 = clean minimal; real design pass later.
- **Param defaults accreting on a folder URL.** The listing's preview pane runs a
  template against the FOLDER's URL, so a folder accretes the stamped defaults
  (D271) of every file type previewed in it —
  `…/local?sel=x.zip&sort=&offset=0&zipPath=&zipMember=`. It costs no history
  entry and reproduces correctly, but it is visible in the address bar and rides
  into a copied link. Undecided: whether the stamp should be suppressed for a
  pane whose target is not the URL's own path.
- **Making the nav epoch discriminate param-only `popstate`s**, so Back stops
  remounting the whole view now that D270 notifies without one — and **reserving
  `sel`/`sort`/`dir`/`snapshot`** as shell-owned keys — are both still open owner
  decisions.
- `fused.ready()` promise: not needed in v1 (params readable synchronously from parent URL).
- Fused account (§27): a manual DMG smoke of the login round-trip on macOS is
  still outstanding — the packaged-app path (bundled CLI, Keychain secrets
  store) can't be exercised from CI/Linux.
- Drive bring-your-own OAuth client ID: **shipped, and no longer optional** — see
  the rewritten D205. It was held as a nice-to-have for users hitting rclone's
  shared-client rate limits; Google retiring that shared client made it the only
  way Drive works at all.
- Drive OAuth: the `rclone authorize` round-trip needs a manual smoke on a real
  Google account; CI has no browser and no credentials to complete a consent.

## How to continue this project

1. Read `SPEC.md` (what & why), `ARCHITECTURE.md` (exact contracts), this file (decisions).
2. M1 definition-of-done = ARCHITECTURE §9 verification checklist.
3. Don't re-litigate decisions D1–D18 without the owner asking.
4. After M1: pick from follow-up list with the owner.
